title: "The Acolyte's Initiation" subtitle: "The Acolyte's Ascent — Book 1" author: "Origin Loom" lang: en

Chapter One: The Shadow in the Stacks

The library clock read 2:47 AM, and Drevik Markinsoul was fairly certain the numbers were mocking him.

He'd been staring at the same page of his Advanced Mythology textbook for forty minutes, and the words had long since blurred into an indecipherable mass of ancient names and theoretical frameworks. The heating system hummed overhead, fighting a losing battle against the November chill that seeped through Ashford University's ancient windows. His coffee had gone cold an hour ago, and his scholarship—his full scholarship—hung in the balance depending on his performance in tomorrow's final.

Three point two GPA. That was the magic number. Anything below that, and the Merit Foundation pulled their funding. And without their funding, Drevik Markinsoul, orphan and professional disappointment, would be heading back to Millbrook with nothing to show for two years of grinding but debt and fractured dreams.

He rubbed his eyes and forced himself to focus. The liminal spaces between realms have long fascinated scholars of the arcane, though empirical evidence of such thresholds remains—

The fluorescent light above study carrel fourteen flickered once. Then again.

Drevik glanced up, frowning. The entire third floor of Ashford's library was empty except for him—or it was supposed to be. Mrs. Chen, the evening librarian, had made her final rounds at midnight, clicking her tongue at his refusal to leave and ultimately leaving him with a spare key and a disappointed expression that reminded him painfully of his grandmother.

The light steadied. Drevik exhaled and returned to his book.

—remains conjectural at best. The so-called 'shadow-walking' phenomenon described in medieval texts likely stems from—

A sound came from the stacks behind him. Not footsteps, exactly. More like the whisper of fabric against fabric, or the sound a shadow might make if shadows had weight.

Drevik spun his chair around.

The third floor of Ashford Library was a maze of towering shelves, each one stuffed with books that had likely not been checked out since the Carter administration. The lamplights between the rows cast pools of amber glow that didn't quite meet, leaving pockets of darkness that the university's administration had never bothered to address. Budget cuts. Everything came down to budget cuts.

He peered down the nearest aisle. Empty.

"Hello?" His voice came out steadier than he felt. "If you're security, I have permission to be here. Mrs. Chen gave me the key."

Silence answered. And yet—something in that silence felt wrong. Like the held breath before a scream.

Drevik was not a brave person. He was, in fact, a committed coward who had survived twenty-one years of life by keeping his head down, his mouth shut, and his expectations low. The smart move—the obvious move—would be to pack up his things, return the key to the overnight drop, and retreat to his off-campus apartment where the worst thing waiting for him was a judgmental landlord and ramen for dinner.

Instead, he stood.

His feet carried him toward the sound before his brain could catch up. The aisle between the shelves stretched ahead, amber light and shadow alternating like a checkerboard. He walked past the philosophy section, the sociology texts, the collected works of authors whose names he'd struggled through in required courses.

The sound came again. Closer now.

Drevik rounded a corner and stopped dead.

The back wall of the library's third floor was solid brick—the original foundation of the building, according to the campus tour he'd suffered through during orientation. No doors, no windows, just aged brick that had been there since the university's founding in 1892.

And yet.

A man stood before the wall, his back to Drevik. Tall, broad-shouldered, wearing a dark coat that drink in the lamplight. His posture was rigid, controlled, like a soldier at attention. He raised one hand and pressed his palm flat against the brick.

Drevik opened his mouth to say something—to ask who this person was, what they were doing, whether they knew the library was supposed to be closed—but the words died in his throat.

Because the wall was moving.

Not the brick itself, but something in the brick. Shadows that had no source, no shape, no reason to exist. They rippled outward from the man's touch like water disturbed by a stone. The solid wall thin, to become something other than wall—a darkness that had depth and dimension and hunger.

The man stepped forward.

Not into the wall. Through it. His body passed into the shadows like a stone sinking into a pond, and for one impossible moment, Drevik saw him walking through something that wasn't space, something that existed where space shouldn't—

Then the man was gone. The wall solidified. The shadows retreated to their natural corners, and Drevik was left standing alone in the aisle, his heart attempting to beat its way out of his chest.

"What the fuck," he said.

"I was hoping you hadn't seen that."

The voice came from directly behind him, and Drevik's body moved without conscious input—years of self-preservation kicking in as he spun around, already stepping backward, already calculating the nearest exit.

The woman stood in the center of the aisle, arms crossed, blocking his path to the main reading room. She was about his age, maybe a year or two older, with auburn hair that caught the lamplight like copper wire. Athletic build, sharp features, green eyes that fixed on him with an intensity that made his stomach clench. Beautiful in the way that predators were beautiful—all coiled threat and dangerous grace.

She wore a dark jacket over a fitted top, practical boots, and an expression that suggested she'd already measured him for a coffin.

"You're Drevik Markinsoul," she said. Not a question.

"How do you know my name?"

She ignored him, taking a step forward. He took a step back. His spine hit the bookshelf behind him—the philosophy section, his mind supplied unhelpfully. Plato's Republic digging into your left kidney. Fitting.

"Scholarship student. 3.4 GPA. Orphan raised by a grandmother in Millbrook. No living relatives except an elderly aunt in Columbus who sends you birthday cards you never answer." Her voice was flat, reciting facts like a grocery list. "You've been at Ashford for two years, major in Comparative Mythology with a minor in History, and you spend every Thursday volunteering at the campus food pantry even though you can barely afford to feed yourself."

Drevik's mouth opened. Closed. His pulse thudded in his ears.

"Who are you?" he managed.

"Someone who's about to make your life very complicated." She took another step forward, and this time he had nowhere to go. The bookshelf pressed against his back, and she stood close enough that he could smell her—something sharp and dark, like pine needles after rain. "The question is, what are we going to do about what you just saw?"

"I didn't see anything." The lie tasted sour on his tongue. "I was studying. I don't know what you're talking about."

Her eyes narrowed. "Don't treat me like I'm stupid, Markinsoul. I've been watching you for three weeks. I know you saw something—your face gave it away the moment I appeared. You've got a terrible poker face."

"Three weeks?" The word came out strangled. "You've been following me for three weeks? That's—what kind of stalker—"

"Not following. Observing." She leaned closer, and Drevik forgot how to breathe. Her face was inches from his, those green eyes boring into him like she could read every thought he'd ever had. "We needed to know if you were a threat."

"A threat to what? I'm a mythology student with three dollars in my bank account and a dying laptop. The only thing I'm a threat to is the campus coffee supply."

The corner of her mouth twitched. It might have been amusement. It might have been contempt. With her, it was impossible to tell.

"Interesting that you can joke right now. Most people would be panicking."

"I'm too confused to panic. You keep talking in riddles."

She studied him for a long moment. Something shifted in her expression—a flicker of what might have been surprise, quickly buried.

"You saw him walk through the wall," she said finally. "Not just through it—into it. Into the space between spaces. And you didn't scream, didn't run, didn't faint. You stood there and tried to understand what you were seeing. That's... unusual."

Drevik swallowed. "I thought I was hallucinating. Sleep deprivation and stress do strange things to the brain."

"You weren't hallucinating."

"Then explain what I saw, because walls don't work that way."

"No," she agreed. "They don't. But shadows do."

She reached out, and Drevik flinched—but her hand only pressed against his chest, holding him in place. Her palm was warm through his thin shirt, and despite the absurdity of the situation, despite the fear coursing through him, part of Drevik's brain registered the contact with startling clarity. The pressure of her fingers. The subtle calluses on her palm. The way her nails bit slightly into the fabric.

"Most people," she said, "can't see it. The Veil, we call it. The boundary between here and... elsewhere. The man you saw is one of us. A walker. He was returning from the Shadowlands."

"Shadowlands." Drevik's voice came out hoarse. "That sounds like something out of a fantasy novel."

"Your mythology textbooks mention it. You just didn't know what you were reading."

She was right. Drevik's mind raced through texts he'd studied—oblique references to liminal spaces, to the twilight realm between worlds, to the walkers who could pass between. He'd assumed they were metaphors, allegories, the ramblings of medieval scholars who'd had too much wine and not enough scientific method.

"You're telling me magic is real," he said. "Shadow magic. People walking through walls. And you're... what? Some kind of secret society?"

"The Order of the Eclipsed Veil." Her mouth curved, though the expression held no warmth. "And we're considerably more than a society. We're the reason the things in the dark don't eat you in your sleep."

Drevik laughed—a short, sharp sound that bordered on hysteria. "You're insane. This is insane. I'm having a mental break from studying too hard, and in a minute I'm going to wake up in my apartment with drool on my face and a desperate need to drop out of school."

"You're not having a mental break." Her hand pressed harder against his chest. "You're awakening."

"Awakening to what?"

"To what you actually are."

She removed her hand, and Drevik felt the loss like a physical thing. His body was reacting to her in ways that were entirely inappropriate given the circumstances—his pulse quickening, his skin hypersensitive, a strange heat pooling low in his stomach. He attributed it to fear. He had to attribute it to fear, because the alternative was that he was attracted to a woman who had just admitted to stalking him and claimed to belong to a magical secret society.

Which was, of course, the more reasonable explanation.

"What I am," he repeated flatly, "is a broke college student who needs to pass his finals so he doesn't lose his scholarship. That's it. That's the whole story. There's no hidden magical destiny, no secret power waiting to be unlocked. Just a guy trying to survive until graduation."

"You're wrong." Her voice softened, though her eyes remained hard. "You've been wrong your whole life, and you don't even know it. The way you see things—the way you've always seen things, even when you convinced yourself it was imagination—that's not normal, Drevik. It's a gift. Or a curse, depending on how you look at it."

"I don't know what you're talking about."

"Don't you?" She tilted her head, studying him. "Never had moments where the world looked... different? Where shadows seemed deeper than they should be, where you could swear you saw something moving in corners that were supposed to be empty? Never had dreams that felt more real than waking, dreams of places that don't exist?"

Drevik's throat went dry.

He'd had those dreams. He'd had those moments. His whole life, he'd caught glimpses of things that weren't there—shadows with weight, spaces that stretch infinitely, shapes that scattered when he looked directly at them. His grandmother had called it an overactive imagination. His doctors had called it stress. He'd learned to ignore it, to tell himself it wasn't real.

"You're wrong," he said again, but his voice wavered.

"You know I'm not." She stepped back, creating distance that was like loss. "My name is Lexi Thornwood. I'm an acolyte in the Order—the same position you're about to assume."

"I'm not assuming anything."

"You don't have a choice." Her expression shifted, something almost like sympathy flickering across her features before the mask fell back into place. "You saw something you weren't supposed to see. That means you're one of two things: a threat to be eliminated, or a potential member to be trained. I'm recommending the latter."

"Recommending to who?"

"The Elders. They'll want to assess you. Test your abilities, determine your potential." She crossed her arms again, and Drevik found himself missing the pressure of her hand. "Some people can't handle the truth. They break under the weight of it, go mad, become liabilities. Others thrive. They embrace the power that comes with understanding the world as it truly is."

"And which one do you think I am?"

Lexi was quiet for a moment. Her gaze swept over him—his face, his posture, the way his hands trembled slightly at his sides. Whatever she saw satisfy something in her.

"I think," she said, "that you've spent your whole life waiting for something like this. I think you've felt different and alone and out of place, and you've never understood why. I think you're going to come with me, not because I'm threatening you, but because part of you has been begging for an excuse to believe that there's more to this world than what you've been told."

Drevik wanted to argue. He wanted to laugh in her face, gather his books, and walk away. He wanted to believe this was a prank, a hallucination, anything other than what she was saying.

But she was right. God help him, she was right.

He'd spent twenty-one years feeling like he was waiting for something. Like everyone else had been given a script he'd never received, like the world had rules he couldn't quite grasp. Every strange vision, every impossible dream, every moment of disconnection—he'd buried them, told himself they were symptoms of a mind that didn't work quite right.

What if they were symptoms of a mind that worked differently? What if everything he'd thought was broken was actually... whole?

"Say I believe you," he said finally. "Say I go with you for this... assessment. What happens if I fail?"

Lexi's expression didn't change, but something in her eyes went cold.

"Then we have a problem," she said. "Because I don't think you're going to fail, Drevik. And that means your life as you know it is over."

She turned and walked toward the end of the aisle, clearly expecting him to follow. Drevik stood frozen for a long moment, his mind racing through possibilities, through escape routes, through every logical reason to turn around and run.

Instead, he followed.

The shadows between the stacks seemed deeper as he walked, the darkness pressing against the edges of his vision. For the first time in his life, Drevik didn't look away from them. He let himself see—and in the seeing, he caught glimpses of shapes that moved and breathed and waited.

Lexi stopped at the end of the aisle and turned to face him. Her auburn hair caught the lamplight, and for one moment, she looked almost soft. Almost kind.

"One more thing," she said. "The Order isn't just about power. It's about protection—protecting the world from what lives in the dark, and protecting our own from those who would destroy us. If you join, you're joining a family. A family that will expect everything from you and give you everything in return."

"I've never had a family," Drevik heard himself say.

"I know." Her voice was quiet. "That's why I think you're going to survive this."

She held out her hand—not in offering this time, but in expectation. A gesture that demanded trust from a man who had every reason not to give it.

Drevik looked at her hand for a long moment. Then he looked at her face—at the challenge in her green eyes, at the subtle tension in her jaw that suggested she was waiting for him to refuse.

He took her hand.

Her fingers closed around his, warm and strong and certain, and Drevik felt something shift in his chest. A click, like a lock finally finding its key. A settling, like a puzzle piece sliding into place.

"Good choice," Lexi said, and pulled him forward into the dark.

Chapter 2: Beneath the University

The wall didn't just move—it breathed.

One moment Drevik was staring at brick and mortar in a dead-end maintenance corridor, the next the surface rippled like disturbed water, and Lexi's hand was iron around his wrist, yanking him forward into darkness.

"Move," she commanded. "Unless you'd rather explain to campus security why you're crawling through restricted access tunnels."

He stumbled after her, feet finding uneven stone where there should have been smooth concrete. The wall sealed behind them with a sound like a sigh, and every trace of fluorescent campus lighting vanished. What replaced it was somehow worse—nothing. Absolute darkness that pressed against his eyes like velvet.

"Lexi—"

"Quiet." Her voice echoed strangely, bouncing off surfaces he couldn't see. "You'll adjust in a moment. The path doesn't like noise."

The path doesn't like noise. As if the tunnel itself were listening. Drevik pressed his lips together and kept walking, one hand trailing against what was like rough-hewn stone. His chest tightened against his ribs, each beat a question he couldn't ask.

What have you gotten yourself into?

The answer came in fragments. First, a faint luminescence that seeped into the air around them, casting everything in pale blue shadow. Then the tunnel walls revealed themselves—not brick, not concrete, but something older. Carved stone covered in symbols that shift when he looked directly at them.

"You're seeing the first layer," Lexi said, and something in her voice had changed. Less hostile. More... assessing. "Most people walk through here and see nothing but damp walls. The fact that you can perceive the glow means you have some potential."

"Potential for what?"

She stopped walking. In the strange light, her auburn hair looked like burnished copper, her green eyes reflecting luminescence like a cat's.

"That's not for me to tell you." She turned and resumed walking, faster now. "Elder Thornwood will explain everything. Or nothing, depending on what he decides you need to know."

"Thornwood." The name scratched at something in Drevik's memory. "Wait—is that—"

"My father, yes." The words came clipped, controlled. "Don't let that make you think you'll get special treatment. If anything, he'll be harder on you because I'm the one who found you."

Found you. Like he was something that had been lost.

They walked for what was like ten minutes but might have been longer. Time moved strangely in the tunnels, each step taking him further from the world he knew. The carved symbols grew more intricate, the light stronger, and twice they passed intersections where other corridors branched off into shadows that seemed to move on their own.

"Those lead to places you don't want to go," Lexi said without being asked. "Not yet. Maybe not ever."

"Where do they lead?"

"Somewhere between here and there." She glanced back at him, and he caught something in her expression—wariness, maybe. Or warning. "The Shadowlands touch our world in places like these. Thin spots. Veil tears. Most people can't perceive them at all, but for those with talent..." She trailed off.

"What happens to people with talent?"

Lexi stopped at a junction where the corridor opened into something larger. The light here was different—warmer, tinged with gold, emanating from crystals embedded in archways that rose above them like ribs of some vast underground beast.

"They see things," she said. "Things that have crossed through. Things that want to cross through. And sometimes—" Her jaw tightened. "Sometimes the things see them back."

A shiver traced down Drevik's spine that had nothing to do with the temperature. "And me? What am I seeing?"

"Right now? Ancient architecture designed to keep those things out." She gestured toward the archways. "These tunnels have existed for centuries. The Order built the university on top of them in the 1800s to hide the entrance. Hiding in plain sight—it's what we do."

We. The word hung in the air between them.

They passed through the archway, and Drevik's breath held.

The space beyond wasn't a room—it was a revelation. The ceiling vaulted upward into shadows that swallowed the light, but the walls were visible: stone carved with intricate patterns that moved. Not like living things, but like recordings. Scenes played out in the carvings—figures in robes performing rituals, shadowy shapes being pushed back through glowing barriers, people walking through walls as if they were made of smoke.

"Welcome," Lexi said, her voice softer now, "to the antechamber of the Order of the Eclipsed Veil."

Drevik couldn't respond. His eyes moved over everything at once—the carved walls, the modern security panels mounted in ancient stone, the guards who stepped out of shadows as if they'd been made of darkness themselves. They wore dark clothing, practical and unremarkable, but their eyes held the same strange luminescence he'd seen in the tunnels.

"Lexi." One of them nodded, his gaze shifting to Drevik. "This the one?"

"This is him." She produced something from her pocket—a card, but not plastic. Something that shimmered like oil on water. "He triggered the perimeter wards last night. I witnessed it."

The guard studied the card, then Drevik, then the card again. His expression revealed nothing.

"Thornwood's waiting," he said finally. "Go straight through. No detours."

"Wasn't planning on any."

They crossed the antechamber, and Drevik became aware of other corridors branching off, other figures moving through distant doorways. The space was larger than he'd first realized—much larger than could possibly exist beneath a university campus.

"This isn't... this can't be real," he heard himself say. "The geology alone—"

"Doesn't apply." Lexi's voice carried a hint of something that might have been amusement. "Spatial compression. One of our founders developed the technique in the 1400s. From the outside, this is a maintenance sub-basement. Inside..." She gestured around them. "The Order has operated here for six hundred years."

Six hundred years. The number hit Drevik like a physical force. "And I've never—how did I not know about this?"

"You weren't supposed to." She stopped at a heavy wooden door set into stone, its surface carved with the same moving symbols he'd seen before. "Most people never know. They live their whole lives in a world that's bigger and stranger than they can imagine, and they never see past the veil." Her hand rested on the door but didn't push. "But you're not most people, Drevik Markinsoul. Your bloodline saw to that."

"My bloodline?"

But she was already pushing the door open, and the words died in his throat.

The chamber beyond was smaller than the antechamber, but it felt larger somehow—more significant. Bookshelves lined the walls, filled with volumes that looked older than any he'd seen in the university's rare collection. A massive desk dominated the center, and behind it sat a man whose presence filled the room like a physical force.

Elder Thornwood looked nothing like Drevik had expected. He wasn't ancient or weathered—in fact, he appeared no older than fifty, with silver-streaked dark hair and the same green eyes as his daughter. His face was handsome in a severe way, all sharp angles and controlled expression. When he stood, Drevik saw that he was tall, broad-shouldered, still carrying the build of someone who'd been formidable in his youth.

Maybe still is.

"Alexa." The elder's voice was deep, measured. "You've brought our guest."

"I have, Father." The word came out clipped, formal. Whatever relationship these two had, it wasn't warm. "He passed through the first ward threshold without incident. The path accepted him."

"Did it." Thornwood's gaze settled on Drevik, and for a moment he felt as if he were being weighed, measured, catalogued. "Interesting. Come forward, boy. Let me look at you."

Drevik walked toward the desk, his legs moving without conscious decision. The elder's eyes tracked him, and he saw something shift in that assessing stare—recognition, perhaps, or memory.

"Drevik Markinsoul," Thornwood said. "Twenty-one years old. Junior at the university, majoring in history with a minor in archaeology. Scholarship student, resident of Wexler Hall, room 314. Grandson and sole living relative of Marta Markinsoul, age seventy-eight, currently residing in—"

"Stop." The word came out sharper than Drevik intended. "How do you know all that?"

Thornwood smiled, and it transformed his face—made it somehow more dangerous. "The Order keeps careful track of those with potential. We've had our eye on you since you were a child."

The temperature in the room drop.

"My mother," Drevik said. "You knew my mother."

"Knew her?" Thornwood moved around the desk with a grace that seemed almost unnatural. "I trained her. Watched her rise through our ranks. Watched her fall in love with your father—another member, though from a different faction." His smile faded. "Watched her disappear."

Disappear. The word hung in the air like smoke.

"She's not dead." Drevik's voice came out certain, though he wasn't sure why. "I would know if she were dead."

"Would you?" Thornwood's eyes narrowed slightly. "Perhaps you would at that. The Markinsoul line has always been... perceptive."

"Father." Lexi's voice cut through the moment, sharp. "He deserves answers. Real ones, not the circular nonsense you give the initiates."

"Does he?" Thornwood turned to his daughter with an expression that Drevik couldn't read. "Does he deserve to know that his parents were among our most promising members before they vanished into the Shadowlands on a mission no one returns from? Does he deserve to know that his grandmother has been paid a monthly stipend from Order funds for twenty years to keep her quiet about what she knows? Does he deserve—"

"Yes." Drevik stepped forward, surprised by his own boldness. "Yes, I do."

For a moment, no one moved. Then Thornwood laughed—a short, sharp sound that held no humor.

"Bold. Your father was bold too. It got him killed." He returned to his desk, lowering himself into the chair with a deliberateness that spoke of controlled power. "Very well, Drevik Markinsoul. You want answers. I'll give you some. But first, you need to understand what you're being offered."

"Offered?"

"A choice." Thornwood steepled his fingers. "The Order of the Eclipsed Veil has existed for over a thousand years. We protect humanity from the things that exist beyond the veil—shadow creatures, malevolent spirits, entities that would consume this world if given the chance. We work in secret because humanity, by and large, cannot handle the truth of what shares their reality."

"Secret," Drevik repeated. "Like a cult."

"Like an order," Thornwood corrected. "We're not mystics chanting in basements, though I'll grant we look the part. We're soldiers, scholars, and guardians. We hold the line between your world and the darkness."

"And my parents—"

"Were soldiers. Good ones." Something flickered in the elder's expression—was it grief? "They went on a mission to the Shadowlands fifteen years ago. They never returned. We believe they're dead, though we have no proof. The Shadowlands swallow evidence."

The words hit Drevik like a wave, but he didn't drown. Somewhere in the back of his mind, something clicked into place—a sense that he'd always known, somehow, that his parents weren't simply gone. That they'd left him for something larger than themselves.

"My grandmother," he said. "She raised me to believe they died in a car accident."

"A story we encouraged." Thornwood didn't apologize. "She knew the truth, but she agreed to let you have a normal childhood. Or as normal as a Markinsoul can have." He leaned forward. "Your bloodline carries gifts, Drevik. Talents that manifest around puberty. Enhanced perception. Shadow sensitivity. And in rare cases, the ability to see possibilities—paths the future might take."

Drevik's breath held. He'd never told anyone about that. The way he sometimes saw multiple outcomes to a situation, like branches on a tree. The way he could choose which branch to follow.

"You've always known you were different," Thornwood continued. "You've always was like you were waiting for something—something that would explain why you never fit in the normal world." His voice softened marginally. "This is that something."

The room was silent for a long moment. Drevik became aware of Lexi standing slightly behind him, close enough that he felt the warmth of her presence, though she wasn't touching him.

"So what happens now?" he asked finally.

Thornwood's expression hardened. "Now you choose. Option one: we wipe your memories of the past twenty-four hours and return you to your normal life. You'll complete your degree, get a job, live an ordinary existence. You'll never know what you are or what you could have been."

"And option two?"

"You undergo testing." Thornwood's eyes held his. "We determine the extent of your abilities and whether you're suitable for initiation. If you pass, you become an acolyte—begin training, learning, serving. You rise through our ranks based on your merits." A pause. "And you might, eventually, learn what really happened to your parents."

Learn what happened. The words sparked something in Drevik's chest—a fire he hadn't known he was capable of feeling. For twenty-one years, he'd been living half a life, always sensing that something was missing. Always waiting.

"I don't need to think about it," he said.

Thornwood's eyebrows rose slightly. "No?"

"No." Drevik straightened, meeting the elder's gaze directly. "I choose to be tested."

For a moment, the room was completely still. Then Thornwood smiled—truly smiled, and it transformed his face into something almost warm.

"Your mother made the same choice," he said. "She walked into this chamber twenty-five years ago, a scholarship student with no idea what she was getting into. She chose testing. She chose the Order." The smile faded into something more serious. "She chose her fate. Let's hope yours is kinder."

He stood, moving to a bookshelf and pulling a volume that looked ancient. The book came away to reveal a hidden compartment behind it, and from this he removed a small crystal that pulsed with inner light.

"Lexi," he said, "take him to the testing chamber. Stay with him through the evaluation—I want a full report on his reactions."

"Father—"

"Don't argue with me." The words were soft, but they carried absolute authority. "You found him. That makes him your responsibility until we know what we're dealing with."

Lexi's jaw tightened, but she nodded. "Yes, Elder."

Thornwood's attention returned to Drevik. "One more thing before you go." He crossed to where Drevik stood, stopping close enough that Drevik saw the threads of silver in his dark hair, the faint lines around his eyes. "The Order isn't just an organization. It's a family. A commitment. If you pass the testing, you'll be bound to us in ways that cannot be broken." His voice dropped. "Are you prepared for that?"

Drevik thought of his grandmother, alone in her small house, waiting for his weekly call. He thought of his scholarship, his carefully planned future, his ordinary life.

He thought of his parents, walking into darkness and never returning.

"I'm prepared."

Thornwood nodded slowly. "Then let's begin."

The testing chamber was deeper than Drevik had expected—much deeper. They descended via an elevator that moved through stone without cables or motors, powered by something he couldn't see. Lexi stood beside him in silence, her shoulder occasionally brushing his in the confined space. Each accidental touch sent a jolt through him that had nothing to do with magic.

"The first test is simple," she said finally, breaking the long silence. "They'll show you images—shadows, shapes, symbols. You tell them what you see."

"And if I see things they don't expect?"

Lexi turned to face him, and in the dim light of the elevator, her expression was unreadable. "Then you'll be exactly what my father thinks you are."

"What does he think I am?"

The elevator stopped, and the doors opened onto a circular room lit by the same crystal-glow he'd seen throughout the compound. Lexi stepped out before answering.

"A catalyst." Her voice was barely above a whisper. "Someone who might change everything."

She led him to the center of the room, where a single chair sat before a low table. On the table lay the crystal Thornwood had retrieved, pulsing slowly.

"Sit," she instructed. "Place your hand on the stone. Let whatever comes come."

Drevik sat. The chair was more comfortable than he expected, molding to his body as if it had been made for him specifically. He reached for the crystal—

"Wait."

Lexi's hand closed around his wrist. Her skin was warm, her grip firm but not painful. In the strange light, her eyes shift color, green deepening to something almost blue.

"The testing isn't just about ability," she said. "It's about control. About whether you can handle what you'll see." She leaned closer, close enough that he could smell her—something like smoke and lavender. "My father may think you're special, but I've seen promising candidates break under less pressure than this. Whatever happens in the next few minutes, don't fight it. Let it flow through you."

"And if I can't?"

Her mouth curved in something that wasn't quite a smile. "Then I'll be here to pull you back."

She released his wrist, and her fingers brushed against his palm as she withdrew—a touch so light it might have been accidental. But something passed between them at that moment, a spark that had nothing to do with the crystal's power. Drevik felt it race up his arm, settling somewhere in his chest like a coal that refused to go out.

Lexi stepped back, her expression unreadable. "Begin."

Drevik placed his hand on the crystal.

The world shifted.

He saw shadows first—dark shapes that moved across his vision like clouds across the moon. Then came the symbols, burning with light that seared itself into his memory. They formed patterns he somehow understood, though he'd never learned them.

This is the language of the veil, a voice said in his mind. This is how we speak to the darkness.

The shadows coalesced into figures—people he didn't recognize, places he'd never been. He saw his parents, young and alive, walking through corridors much like these. He saw them laughing, training, falling in love.

He saw them standing at the edge of something vast and dark, holding hands.

He saw them step forward together, into nothing.

The Shadowlands, the voice said. Where they went. Where they might still be.

The vision shifted. He saw himself—older, changed, standing in a circle of light with figures he couldn't make out. Power hummed through him, power he didn't understand. And beside him stood someone with auburn hair and green eyes, watching him with an expression that held fear and something deeper.

Lexi, he thought. I see Lexi.

The vision fractured into a thousand possibilities. He saw branches spreading from a single point—his decision in this moment, this breath. In some branches, he rose through the Order's ranks. In others, he fell. He saw love and loss, triumph and failure, a dozen lifetimes playing out simultaneously.

And through it all, one truth burned bright and undeniable:

He was meant to be here.

The crystal pulsed one final time beneath his palm, and the visions faded. Drevik opened his eyes to find Lexi crouched in front of him, her hands on his shoulders, her face pale.

"Drevik." His name came sharp. "Drevik, look at me. Are you back?"

He blinked. The testing chamber came into focus—crystal light, carved walls, Lexi's worried face inches from his own.

"I'm back," he said. His voice sounded strange to his own ears, rough and distant. "I saw... I saw everything."

Lexi's grip tightened briefly, then released. She stood, composing herself with visible effort.

"You were under for two hours," she said. "Most candidates last fifteen minutes. Twenty if they're strong."

Two hours. Drevik looked down at his hands. They were trembling.

"What did you see?" Lexi's voice was carefully neutral. "What did the crystal show you?"

He could have told her about his parents. About the Shadowlands. About the visions of her standing beside him in a circle of light. But something held him back—a sense that these were secrets he needed to keep, at least for now.

"Symbols," he said. "Shadows. Possibilities."

Lexi studied him for a long moment, and he had the sense that she knew he wasn't telling her everything. But she didn't push.

"Elder Thornwood will want a full report," she said. "Come on. There's more testing to do."

She offered him her hand. Drevik took it, feeling that same spark when their skin touched—a connection that resonate in the space between them. Lexi pulled him to his feet, but she didn't let go immediately. For a moment, they stood close enough that he saw the flecks of gold in her green eyes, the faint scar along her jawline.

"Whatever happens next," she said, "remember that you chose this. You can still walk away."

"Can I?"

She released his hand, stepping back. The moment broke, and Drevik found himself missing something he hadn't known he wanted.

"That's what the testing is for," she said, her voice cool again. "To see if you can walk away. To see if you want to." She turned toward the door. "Come on. We have a long night ahead of us."

Drevik followed her out of the testing chamber, through corridors that now seemed less foreign and more like something he was meant to find. His hand still tingled where she'd touched him, and somewhere in his chest, that coal continued to burn.

He didn't know what lay ahead. He didn't know if he would pass the tests, if he would survive the training, if he would ever learn what had happened to his parents. But as he walked beside Lexi through the ancient halls of the Order of the Eclipsed Veil, Drevik Markinsoul knew one thing with absolute certainty:

For the first time in his life, he was exactly where he was supposed to be.

And whatever came next, he wouldn't face it alone.

Chapter 3: The Initiate's Trial

The blindfold smelled like old copper and dried sage.

Drevik sat in a wooden chair that had probably been new when his grandmother was a girl, hands resting on his knees, waiting for whatever came next. The room around him hummed with said conversations he couldn't quite make out—like listening to a radio tuned to the wrong frequency. Someone had told him three days of testing. Someone else had mentioned that initiates failed. No one had mentioned what failure meant.

He didn't ask. He wasn't sure he wanted to know.

"The first trial concerns knowledge," a woman's voice said from somewhere to his left. Cultured. Precise. Like a professor who'd been teaching the same material for decades and lost patience somewhere around year ten. "You will answer truthfully, or you will answer nothing. The choice is always yours, Initiate Markinsoul. Choose poorly, and the trial ends."

"I understand." His voice came out steadier than he felt.

"Good. Remove your covering."

Drevik pulled the blindfold off and squinted against sudden candlelight. The room was smaller than he'd expected—a semicircular chamber with stone walls that wept moisture in the flickering light. Three figures sat at a curved table: a woman with iron-gray hair pulled into a severe bun, a man whose face was half-hidden by shadow, and an elderly man with folded hands and closed eyes.

The written examination lay before him on a plain wooden desk. A single piece of parchment covered in symbols he didn't recognize, questions written in a language that looked like it had evolved from Latin and something older. Much older.

"Begin," the woman said.

Drevik picked up the pen.

The first question asked about the relationship between lunar phases and shadow conductivity. He'd never studied astronomy. He'd never heard of shadow conductivity. But as he read the words, something stirred in the back of his mind—knowledge that was like remembering rather than learning. The moon's pull affected the veil between worlds. During the new moon, the barrier thinned. During the full, it strengthened. Shadow magic flowed like water through those channels, and a skilled practitioner could—

He blinked. Where had that come from?

He wrote his answer.

The second question concerned the taxonomy of Shadowlands entities. Class One through Five, with subcategories he didn't understand but somehow knew. Feeders. Stalkers. Weavers. The names felt right, like fitting a key into a lock he'd forgotten existed. The third question asked about binding oaths—the consequences of breaking them, the mechanics of their creation.

By the tenth question, Drevik's hand was cramping and his head throbbed with a dull ache behind his eyes. He kept writing. History of the Order. Notable breaches in the veil. The proper method of dispatch for various entity classifications. The significance of the Eclipsed Veil symbol—a moon partially obscured by shadow, representing the balance between light and dark, knowledge and mystery.

When he finished, he set down the pen and waited.

The elderly man opened his eyes. They were pale blue, almost colorless, and they fixed on Drevik with unsettling intensity.

"You answered every question."

"I did."

"You have never studied our texts."

"No, sir."

The man smiled. It wasn't a comforting expression. "And yet you wrote as though you'd been raised within our walls. As though you'd spent years learning what should take decades to absorb." He tilted his head. "Tell me, Initiate Markinsoul. Where does knowledge come from?"

Drevik thought about his grandmother's stories. About the dreams he'd had since childhood—dreams of running through endless twilight, of voices speaking in languages he shouldn't understand. About the way shadows had always move when he wasn't looking directly at them.

"I don't know," he said honestly. "It feels like... remembering. Like I knew it somewhere else, and I'm just now finding it again."

The three examiners exchanged glances. The woman made a note on her own parchment. The man in shadow shifted slightly, revealing a scar that ran from his jaw to his collarbone before retreating back into darkness.

"The first trial is concluded," the elderly man said. "You have passed, Initiate. Rest now. The second trial begins at dawn."

His cell was barely larger than a closet.

A cot, a basin of cold water, a window set too high in the stone wall to see anything but the ceiling of whatever larger chamber lay beyond. Drevik lay on the thin mattress and stared at the ceiling, trying to process what had happened.

He'd never believed in anything supernatural. Ghost stories were for children. Magic was for movies and escapist fiction. But the knowledge he'd accessed during the examination—that hadn't come from nowhere. Something had opened inside him, like a door swinging wide in a dark room he hadn't known existed.

His phone had been taken. His watch. The small leather journal where he kept notes for his literature class. Everything that connected him to the world outside these walls. He was alone with questions he couldn't answer and a headache that wouldn't fade.

Sleep came in fragments. Dreams of running. Dreams of searching for something he couldn't name. Dreams of eyes watching him from the darkness—amber eyes, golden eyes, the pale blue of the examiner who'd smiled like a predator scenting prey.

When the knock came at his door, he was already awake.

The second trial took place in a chamber that smelled like sweat and old blood.

Drevik stood in a loose group of perhaps a dozen others—all young, all dressed in the same plain gray clothes he'd been given, all wearing expressions that ranged from terrified to determined. He recognized none of them. They'd been processed separately, kept isolated, fed just enough information to know that they were being tested and that failure carried consequences.

The chamber was circular, with a packed dirt floor and high stone walls. Weapons lined the walls—swords, spears, chains, things Drevik couldn't identify. He'd never held a weapon in his life. The closest he'd come was a fencing demonstration at a campus fair, and he'd spent most of it trying not to look like an idiot.

"Combat is the oldest test," a man's voice boomed from somewhere above. Drevik looked up and saw a gallery of observers—faces in shadow, watching from behind a wrought-iron railing. "The Order requires strength as well as knowledge. The Shadowlands do not respect scholarship alone."

A figure emerged from a side door, moving with the easy grace of someone who'd spent years training. Female, athletic, with auburn hair pulled back in a practical tail. She wore the same gray clothes as the initiates, but she carried herself differently—shoulders back, chin lifted, the confidence of someone who'd never been uncertain of her place in the world.

Drevik recognized her immediately.

Lexi Thornwood. The woman from the campus coffee shop. The one who'd watched him like he was a problem to be solved, a variable in an equation she hadn't finished calculating. She'd been in the gallery during his first trial, he realized now. Watching. Assessing.

She stepped into the center of the chamber and surveyed the group of initiates with cold green eyes.

"I need volunteers," she said. Her voice was clear, commanding. "Combat assessment. Full contact, submission rules only. Show me what you can do."

No one moved.

"Let me clarify." She smiled, but it didn't reach her eyes. "I'm testing you. Whether you volunteer or I select you, you will be tested. The only question is whether you want to show some initiative, or whether you want me to decide that you're too cowardly to step forward."

A young man near Drevik's left raised his hand. "I'll go."

"Good." Lexi gestured to the center of the chamber. "Come forward. Name?"

"Marcus Webb."

"Any training, Webb?"

"Some boxing. A little Judo in high school."

"Better than nothing." She didn't sound impressed. "Begin when ready."

The fight was short and brutal. Marcus was competent—Drevik saw that much—but Lexi moved like water. Every attack he offered, she flowed around. Every defense he raised, she found the gap and exploited it. Within thirty seconds, he was on his back, her knee on his chest, her hand raised in a gesture Drevik didn't recognize.

"Submission," she said. "Next."

Two more volunteers. Two more brief, humiliating defeats. Lexi wasn't even breathing hard.

"Anyone else?" She scanned the group, and her eyes landed on Drevik. Held. "You. In the back. You haven't moved since you came in."

He felt the attention of everyone in the room. The other initiates, grateful not to be chosen. The observers in the gallery, watching with expressions he couldn't read. And Lexi, waiting for his response with something that might have been anticipation.

He stepped forward.

"Name?"

"Drevik Markinsoul."

"Any training, Markinsoul?"

He thought about the question. "I've been in a few fights."

"That's not the same thing."

"I know."

She studied him for a long moment. "You were the one who passed the knowledge trial yesterday. The one who answered questions he shouldn't have known."

"I didn't cheat, if that's what you're asking."

"I'm not asking that." She shifted her stance, settling into a position that looked almost casual. "I'm asking whether you're as unusual as they say, or whether you're just another initiate who got lucky."

"I don't know." He rolled his shoulders, trying to loosen muscles that had tensed beyond reason. "Why don't you find out?"

Her smile changed. Still cold, but with an edge of something else now. Interest, maybe. Or the anticipation of a challenge she hadn't expected.

"Begin."

She moved first—a testing strike, fast and precise, aimed at his jaw. Drevik saw it coming before she threw it. Not with his eyes, but with something deeper. A sense of where she would be, where she was going, the shape of the fight stretching out ahead of them like a path through unfamiliar terrain.

He slipped the punch and stepped back.

"Good reflexes." She circled left. "But reflexes aren't enough."

She came at him again, faster this time. A combination—punch, punch, kick. He saw the sequence before it began. Blocked the first strike, dodged the second, caught her leg on the kick and shoved.

She recovered her balance instantly, but he saw the surprise flicker across her face before she masked it.

"Not bad." Her voice was lower now. Focused. "You've got some instincts."

"I told you I've been in fights."

"You did." She attacked again, and this time she wasn't testing.

Drevik had never studied martial arts. He'd never had formal training of any kind. But he'd survived three years in a group home that wasn't kind to quiet, intelligent boys, and he'd spent two more years learning how to avoid fights he couldn't win. The streets around his grandmother's house had taught him that the best fight was the one you walked away from. The second best was the one you ended quickly.

Lexi pressed him hard. She was faster, more skilled, and clearly stronger. But he saw what she was going to do before she did it. Not always—sometimes the visions were unclear, like watching television through a fog—but often enough. Often enough to block, to counter, to stay standing when he should have been on the ground.

She swept his legs. He rolled with the fall, came up with dirt on his face and a bruise forming on his shoulder. She pressed the advantage, driving him back toward the wall. He saw her next three moves, found the gap in her offense, and slipped through it.

For a moment, they were pressed close—her shoulder against his chest, his hand locked around her wrist, both of them breathing hard.

"Interesting," she said. "Very interesting."

Then she twisted out of his grip and swept his feet again, and this time she followed him down. A knee on his chest, her hand raised in that submission gesture, her face inches from his.

Her eyes were greener than he'd realized. A ring of amber around the pupil, catching the light.

"Yield," she said.

He yielded.

She stood and offered him a hand. He took it, let her pull him to his feet, and tried to read the expression on her face. She was harder to parse now. Still cold, still assessing, but something had shifted in the way she looked at him. As though he'd become a puzzle she wanted to solve rather than a problem she wanted to eliminate.

"Who trained you?" she asked.

"No one. I just... see it."

"See what?"

"The fight. Before it happens." He wasn't sure how to explain it. "Like remembering something that hasn't occurred yet."

Her eyes widened fractionally. Whatever he'd said, it meant something to her. Something she hadn't expected.

"Get cleaned up," she said. "The third trial begins tonight. You'll need your strength."

She turned and walked away without another word, but Drevik caught the glance she threw over her shoulder. The wariness. The reluctant respect.

He'd passed. He wasn't sure how, but he'd passed.

The third trial began at midnight.

They brought him to a chamber deep beneath the compound—a place that felt older than the building above it, older than the university, older than the city itself. The walls were rough stone, carved with symbols that shift when he wasn't looking directly at them. In the center of the room stood a pedestal, and on the pedestal rested an artifact that looked like a sphere of solidified shadow.

It wasn't glass. It wasn't crystal. It was something else entirely—something that absorbed the light around it and gave back nothing in return.

"Touch it," the elderly examiner said. He'd introduced himself as Brother Aldric, though Drevik suspected the title was ceremonial rather than familial. "Let it show you what lies within."

Drevik approached the pedestal. The sphere pulse as he drew closer, though he couldn't have said whether the movement was real or imagined.

"What will it show me?"

"Truth." Aldric's voice was soft. "The truth of your potential. Your gifts. Your weaknesses. Some initiates see nothing—they are dismissed. Some see shadows—accepted but unremarkable. A few see clearly, and those are marked for advancement." He paused. "What do you expect to see, Drevik Markinsoul?"

"I don't know." He reached out. "I've never been good at predicting the future."

His fingers brushed the surface.

Cold. So cold it burned. The sensation shot through his hand, up his arm, into his chest, his mind, his—

Visions.

Not one future. Not a single path stretching ahead of him, clear and certain. Dozens. Hundreds. Futures branching and splitting and merging again, each one different, each one possible. He saw himself standing in this chamber, accepting a robe and a title. He saw himself walking away, returning to his grandmother's house, living an ordinary life. He saw himself dying—here, there, a hundred different deaths in a hundred different places.

He saw Lexi. Fighting beside him. Fighting against him. Standing over his grave with an expression he couldn't read. Pressed against him in a room he didn't recognize, her hands in his hair, her mouth—

The visions shifted. He saw the compound in flames. He saw an Elder standing over a body, holding a blade. He saw himself making choices he didn't understand, consequences he couldn't predict. He saw—

He pulled his hand away.

The visions faded, but they didn't disappear entirely. He could still feel them, just beyond the edge of his awareness. Futures that might come to pass. Futures that never would. All of them possible, none of them certain.

Aldric was staring at him with an expression Drevik couldn't interpret. The other examiners had emerged from the shadows, and they all wore the same look.

"How long?" the woman asked. Her precise voice was less controlled now. "How long did you touch the sphere?"

"I don't know. A few seconds."

"Longer." She looked at Aldric. "Much longer. Nearly three minutes."

"Most initiates break contact within thirty seconds," Aldric said. "The sphere overwhelms them. They see too much, or too little, and they pull away before the truth can settle." He stepped closer, studying Drevik's face. "What did you see, Initiate?"

"Many futures." Drevik's voice sounded strange to his own ears. "Branching paths. Possibilities."

"You saw more than one?"

"Hundreds. Maybe more."

The examiners exchanged glances. Aldric's expression had shifted into something that looked almost like reverence.

"Foresight," he said. "True foresight. Not the cheap fortune-telling of charlatans, but the genuine sight—the ability to see what might be, to understand the consequences of choices before they're made." He shook his head slowly. "In all my years, I've seen perhaps a dozen initiates with that gift. And you... you saw clearly? You could distinguish the paths?"

"I think so. They were... distinct. Different."

"And you remained conscious throughout." The woman's voice was sharp, but there was respect beneath it. "Impressive. More than impressive." She made a note on her parchment. "The Order will have questions for you, Initiate. Many questions."

"I understand."

"I'm not sure you do." She looked up from her notes. "But you will."

They gave him new clothes—black robes, simple but well-made, with the symbol of the Eclipsed Veil embroidered at the collar. They gave him a room that was larger than his cell had been, with an actual bed and a window that looked out on an underground garden he hadn't known existed. They gave him a title—Initiate—and a set of responsibilities he didn't yet understand.

And they gave him a handler.

Lexi caught him in the hallway outside his new quarters, her arms crossed, her expression caught somewhere between resignation and irritation.

"You've been assigned to my care," she said without preamble. "For training, orientation, and assessment. I'm to report on your progress to the Elders directly."

"I didn't ask for—"

"I know you didn't." She cut him off. "I know you didn't ask for any of this. You stumbled into our world by accident, passed trials you shouldn't have been able to pass, and demonstrated abilities that haven't been seen in decades." Her voice was controlled, but Drevik saw the tension in her shoulders. "And now I'm responsible for you. For your training, your integration, your survival."

"I'm sorry." He meant it. "I know this isn't what you wanted."

"No. It isn't." She studied him for a long moment, and her expression softened fractionally. "But I don't have a choice. The Elders have decided you're valuable, and valuable things must be protected." She turned and started walking. "Come. There's much to discuss, and little time to discuss it."

He followed her through corridors that twisted and branched in ways that seemed impossible. The compound was larger than he'd realized—rooms branching off from rooms, staircases leading up and down, passages that double back on themselves. He tried to memorize the route, but after the third turn, he gave up.

"You're wondering why you," Lexi said without looking back. "Why you passed the trials. Why you can see futures that haven't happened yet."

"The thought occurred to me."

"Some people are born into the Order." She stopped at a door and turned to face him. "They grow up knowing our ways, learning our history, developing abilities that manifest gradually over years. Some people are recruited—identified through various means, brought in as adults, trained to serve specific purposes. And some..." She paused. "Some are found. They don't know what they are until they're tested. Their abilities have always been there, dormant, waiting for the right catalyst to awaken them."

"You think I'm one of those."

"I think you're something we haven't seen before." Her voice was careful. "Your abilities don't match the standard profiles. Your knowledge shouldn't exist. And your foresight..." She shook her head. "Foresight is rare. True, clear, conscious foresight is nearly unheard of. And yet you touched the sphere for three minutes and walked away."

"Is that significant?"

"The last person who touched the sphere for longer than two minutes died." Her green eyes held his. "The last person with foresight like yours was my grandmother. She saw the consequences of every choice, every action. She could predict the movements of our enemies and the failures of our allies. She was the most powerful seer the Order has seen in five generations."

"What happened to her?"

"She was killed." Lexi's voice was flat. "By someone who feared what she saw."

The words hung in the air between them. Drevik felt a chill that had nothing to do with the temperature of the corridor.

"I'll be careful," he said.

"You'll have to be." She opened the door and gestured him through. "Because right now, you're the most interesting thing that's happened to the Order in decades. And interesting things have a way of attracting dangerous attention."

He stepped past her into a room lined with books and artifacts, his mind already turning over the implications of everything she'd said. Lexi followed, closing the door behind them, and for the first time since he'd arrived, Drevik felt something that might have been hope.

He was an initiate now. He had a place, a purpose, a path forward.

But as Lexi began explaining the structure of the Order—the hierarchy, the factions, the delicate balance of power that kept the organization functional—he couldn't shake the feeling that he'd stepped into something far more complicated than he'd imagined.

The trials were over.

The real test was just beginning.

They brought him to his quarters as the compound settled into the quiet rhythms of late night. The corridors were emptier now, the examiners and observers having dispersed to their own spaces. Drevik walked between two senior initiates—quiet, formal young men who view him as a curiosity rather than a colleague.

"Your training begins tomorrow," one of them said as they reached his door. "Lexi Thornwood will collect you at dawn. I suggest you rest."

"I will."

The second initiate lingered after the first walked away. Younger than Drevik had initially thought, with close-set eyes and a nervous energy that radiated from every movement.

"Hey," he said, pitching his voice low. "Can I give you some advice? Something that might help you survive this place?"

Drevik turned. "Sure."

The initiate glanced down the corridor, confirming they were alone. "Watch your back." His voice dropped even lower. "Lexi's father killed the last guy who disappointed him."

Before Drevik could respond, the initiate was gone—sprinted down the corridor and around a corner before he could ask any of the questions suddenly crowding his mind.

He stood in the doorway of his new quarters, alone with the weight of the warning and the strange, uncertain future stretching out before him.

In the visions the sphere had shown him, he'd seen many versions of Lexi Thornwood. Enemy. Ally. Something else entirely—something that made his pulse quicken when he remembered it.

He'd also seen himself standing over graves.

He wasn't sure which future he was walking toward.

But he knew one thing for certain: in the Order of the Eclipsed Veil, nothing was exactly what it seemed.

And everyone had secrets worth killing for.

Chapter 4: Normal Life

Chapter 4: Normal Life

The lecture hall was like it was underwater.

Drevik blinked at the chalkboard, where Professor Hendricks was writing something about macroeconomic theory, but the words kept sliding around like oil on glass. He'd been awake for thirty-six hours. Maybe forty. The Shadow Walking training sessions happened between midnight and four in the morning, and apparently the Order didn't believe in easing new recruits in gently.

"Mr. Markinsoul?"

He jerked upright. The girl next to him—a business major with a pixie cut and a nose ring—snickered softly.

"Can you explain the concept of market equilibrium?"

Drevik's mouth opened. Closed. He'd read the chapter. He knew this. But his brain felt stuffed with cotton.

"Price point," he managed, voice rough from too much coffee and not enough sleep, "where supply meets demand. No surplus, no shortage. Everyone's happy."

Hendricks raised an eyebrow. "That's... correct, actually. Though I'd appreciate it if you looked at me instead of your desk while answering."

A few students laughed. Drevik felt his ears go hot. Normal. This was supposed to be normal. Just a college junior failing to stay awake in Econ 201, not some initiate of a shadow cult who'd spent the previous night learning to step between dimensions.

The professor moved on, and Drevik slumped back in his seat. His phone buzzed in his pocket. A text from an unknown number:

Training tonight. Midnight. Don't be late. —L

He shoved the phone deeper into his pocket and tried to focus. But his mind kept drifting to the feeling of shadow—how it had wrapped around him like cold water, how the world had folded in on itself when he'd stepped through that first portal. The memory of it was terrifying and exhilarating in equal measure, leaving him with an ache in his chest that was like hunger.

Focus. This is your life now. Both of them.

The lecture droned on.

After class, Drevik made his way across the quad, weaving through clusters of students who seemed impossibly bright and alive. The autumn sun felt good on his face, warm enough to make him drowsy. He needed food. Real food, not the vending machine snacks he'd been surviving on. And sleep. God, he needed sleep.

"Hey! Wait up!"

He turned. A girl was jogging toward him, her dark hair catching the light. She wasn't someone he recognized, which was unusual—he'd been at Westbrook University for three years, and the campus was small enough that faces became familiar even when names didn't.

She caught up to him, slightly out of breath, smiling in a way that made him acutely aware of the shadows under his own eyes.

"You're Drevik Markinsoul, right? I'm Seraphina. Seraphina Vane. I just transferred."

She extended a hand. He took it automatically. Her grip was firm, her skin cool.

"Sorry, have we met?" He tried to place her face. She was striking—sharp cheekbones, full lips, eyes that shift between gray and blue depending on the light. A small silver pendant hung at her throat, shaped like a crescent moon.

"No, but I've heard about you." Her smile widened. "Professor Hendricks mentioned you in class last week. Said you were one of his more 'interesting' students. I'm an econ major too."

Drevik felt a prickle of unease. Hendricks had said that? He couldn't recall anything remarkable about his performance in that class. "That's... weird. I'm not exactly a star student."

"Oh, I don't know about that." Seraphina tilted her head, studying him. "I saw your paper on cryptocurrency regulation. You have a way of seeing patterns that others miss."

Now the unease was definite. He'd written that paper sophomore year. For a different professor. In a different department.

"What did you say your major was again?"

"Economics." Her eyes glinted. "Minor in esoteric studies. It's a new program."

There was no esoteric studies minor at Westbrook. Drevik was sure of it.

"Look, I should get going—"

"Let me buy you coffee." She stepped closer, and he caught a hint of her perfume—something floral with an undertone of smoke. "You look like you could use it. Late night studying?"

The question felt loaded. Drevik's pulse quickened.

"I'm fine. Thanks."

"Are you?" Seraphina's voice dropped, just slightly. "Because you don't look fine. You look like someone who's been asked to carry more than they can hold."

He stared at her. The words landed too close to the truth, and for a moment he wanted to tell her everything—the Order, the training, the way the shadows called to him even now, whispering at the edges of his consciousness.

"I'll see you around, Seraphina."

He walked away without looking back. But he felt her gaze on him, a pressure between his shoulder blades that didn't fade until he'd rounded the corner of the library building.

The training room was in the basement of a building that, on the surface, housed the university's archives. Drevik had walked past it a hundred times without noticing anything unusual. Now he knew better. The door to the basement was hidden behind a shelf of outdated encyclopedias, accessible only to those who knew the pattern of knocks and carried the small obsidian token he'd been given after his initiation.

Lexi was waiting when he arrived.

She stood in the center of the room, arms crossed, wearing the same fitted black clothing she'd worn every time he'd seen her. Her auburn hair was pulled back in a tight braid, and her expression was what he'd come to think of as her default: cold assessment.

"You're late."

"I'm three minutes early."

"Late means not early enough." She uncrossed her arms. "Tell me what you've practiced."

Drevik's body ached from exhaustion, but he straightened. "Shadow Walking. I can do the basic step now, the one you showed me. Move from one shadow to another in the same room."

"Show me."

He looked around the training space. It was a large room, roughly forty feet square, lit by flickering torches that cast pools of shadow into the corners. Stone walls, stone floor, no windows. The air smelled faintly of incense and something older, earthier.

Drevik focused on the shadow in the far corner. He felt it, even from this distance—a kind of coldness that had nothing to do with temperature. He reached for it with his mind, visualizing the step, the way the darkness would fold around him—

The world shifted.

For a moment, he was nowhere. Everywhere. Floating in a space that wasn't space, surrounded by whispers in languages he almost understood. Then the shadow spat him out, and he was standing in the corner, pulse quickening, hands trembling.

Lexi nodded. Once.

"Acceptable. Now do it again. The opposite corner."

He did. And again. And again. Each time, the transition became slightly easier, but the cost grew. His legs were shaking now. His vision blurred at the edges.

"Again."

"I need a minute—"

"Now."

He reached for the shadow. It resisted. He pushed harder. The darkness surge toward him, eager and hungry, and for a moment he was falling, falling through a void that had no bottom—

Lexi's hand closed around his wrist, yanking him back.

"Enough." Her voice was sharp. "You're pushing too hard. The shadow will take everything you give it and ask for more. You have to learn control."

Drevik sagged, catching himself against the wall. "I had it."

"You had nothing. You were about to slip into the Shadowlands." Lexi's grip on his wrist tightened, then released. "That's not a place you want to go unprepared. People don't come back the same."

"What do you mean?"

But she was already moving away, her face closed off. "We're done for tonight. Go home. Eat. Sleep. Tomorrow we work on Veil Sight."

"Lexi—"

"I said we're done."

He watched her, trying to read something—anything—in her posture. She was rigid, her shoulders tight. There was a story there, he realized. Something she wasn't telling him.

"Thank you," he said. "For the training."

She didn't turn around.

He made it halfway across the quad before his legs gave out.

One moment he was walking, the next he was on his knees, the grass cool against his palms. His head spun. The world tilted. He tried to push himself up, but his arms wouldn't cooperate.

"Whoa, hey—"

Hands caught him, strong and careful. He was dimly aware of being guided to a bench, of someone pressing something cold into his hands. A water bottle.

"Drink."

He drank. The water helped, a little. His vision cleared enough to see the person crouched in front of him.

Lexi.

Her face was unreadable, but something flickered in her green eyes. Concern? Annoyance? He couldn't tell.

"You used too much energy," she said. "Shadow Walking drains you. You need to replenish."

"I'm fine."

"You're not fine. You're about thirty seconds from unconscious." She stood, arms crossed again, but her posture had changed. Less rigid. "Can you walk?"

"I think so."

He tried to stand. His legs wobbled. Lexi caught his arm, steadying him.

"I'll take you back to your dorm."

"I can make it—"

"That wasn't a question." Her grip was firm. "Which building?"

Drevik's room was small—a bed, a desk, a window overlooking the parking lot. Lexi guided him inside, deposited him on the bed, and began looking through his mini fridge.

"You have nothing but leftover pizza and energy drinks."

"I haven't had time to shop."

She made a sound that might have been disgust. "Stay here."

She was gone before he could protest. He lay back against the pillow, staring at the ceiling. His whole body was like it had been wrung out. The shadows in the corners of the room seemed deeper than usual, and he had to remind himself that they were just shadows. Ordinary shadows.

Except nothing was ordinary anymore.

Lexi returned twenty minutes later with a paper bag. She set it on his desk and began pulling out containers—soup, bread, some kind of protein bar.

"Eat. All of it."

"Are you going to watch me?"

"Someone has to make sure you don't die your first week." She pulled his desk chair over to the bed and sat, crossing her legs. "The Order doesn't have the best track record with new recruits."

Drevik forced himself upright, reaching for the soup. It was chicken noodle, still hot. His stomach growled.

"How many recruits have you trained?"

Lexi's expression flickered. "You're the first."

"Really?"

"It's not... I don't usually do this." She looked away. "I requested it."

"Why?"

She was quiet for a long moment. When she spoke, her voice was softer than he'd heard it before.

"Because the last person I was supposed to protect died. And I wasn't there when it happened."

The words hung in the air. Drevik set down the soup.

"I'm sorry."

"It was two years ago. He was a friend. More than a friend, maybe." Lexi's jaw tightened. "He thought he could handle the Shadowlands on his own. He couldn't."

Drevik remembered what she'd said earlier. People don't come back the same.

"Is that why you pushed me so hard? The training?"

"You have potential. More than he did. More than most." She met his eyes, and for the first time, he saw something raw in her expression. Vulnerability. "If you don't learn control, the shadow will consume you. I won't let that happen again."

He wanted to say something—something that would bridge the gap between them, acknowledge the trust she was placing in him. But his eyelids were heavy, and his body was demanding sleep.

"Thank you," he managed. "For everything."

Lexi stood, moving toward the door. But she paused at the threshold.

"Get some sleep. Tomorrow will be harder."

She left the door cracked open. Drevik listened to her footsteps in the hallway, fading into silence.

He woke to dim light and the wrongness of someone watching him.

Drevik's eyes snapped open. For a moment, he didn't know where he was—his dorm room, yes, but something was different. The shadows were too thick. The air too cold.

Then he saw her.

Lexi was in the chair beside his bed, asleep. Her head had fallen back against the wall, her braid loosened, her face relaxed in a way he'd never seen it. She looked younger. Softer. Her lips were parted slightly, and her hand rested near his pillow, close enough that if he moved, his fingers would brush hers.

He lay still, watching her breathe. The cold, distant trainer from the Order was gone, replaced by this—a woman who'd stayed to make sure he was okay. Who'd let her guard down because she was too exhausted to maintain it.

More than a friend, maybe.

The words echoed in his mind. He wondered what that meant. What she'd lost. What she was afraid of losing again.

As if sensing his attention, Lexi stirred. Her eyes opened, unfocused at first, then sharpening as she saw him watching. For a moment, neither of them moved. The silence held between them, heavy and strange.

"You stayed," Drevik said.

"You passed out." Her voice was rough. "I wanted to make sure you didn't die in your sleep."

"Did you sleep at all?"

Her gaze slid away. "Someone had to keep watch. The shadows are drawn to you. It makes you a target."

"Lexi—"

"Get dressed." She stood abruptly, the vulnerability gone, replaced by her usual mask. "You have class in an hour. I'll check on you tonight."

She was gone before he could respond. But he noticed that her hand lingered on the doorframe for just a moment, her fingers pressing into the wood as if steadying herself.

The day passed in a blur of lectures and coffee. Drevik found himself looking for Seraphina in each of his classes, but she didn't appear. Had he imagined their interaction? The strange intensity of her questions?

No. She was real. And she knew something she shouldn't.

He was crossing the quad after his last class when Lexi appeared beside him, seemingly out of nowhere.

"We need to talk."

"About what?"

"About the girl you met yesterday. Seraphina Vane."

Drevik stopped walking. "How do you know about that?"

"The Order watches its members. We have eyes everywhere." Lexi's expression was grim. "She's not a transfer student. She's not even enrolled here."

"Then who is she?"

"That's what concerns me. We can't find anything on her. No records, no history, no paper trail. It's like she didn't exist until two days ago."

Drevik remembered the way Seraphina had looked at him. The way her questions had cut too close to the truth.

"What do you think she wants?"

"I don't know. But there's something wrong with her." Lexi's voice dropped. "Stay away from her. Promise me."

"I barely know her—"

"Promise me, Drevik."

He'd never heard her use his first name before. It sounded different in her mouth—urgent, almost desperate.

"I promise."

Lexi nodded, but she didn't seem satisfied. Her eyes kept scanning the quad, watching.

"Go back to your dorm. Don't talk to anyone you don't know. I'll come by tonight for training."

She walk away, then stopped. Turned back.

"Drevik."

"Yeah?"

"She's dangerous. I can feel it. And I don't say that lightly."

He watched her go, disappearing into the crowd of students. When she was gone, he let out a breath he hadn't realized he was holding.

The sun was setting, painting the quad in shades of orange and purple. Students were heading to dinner, to parties, to the normal routines of college life. Drevik stood still, feeling the weight of his double life pressing down on him.

He turned to head toward his dorm—and froze.

Across the quad, standing perfectly still while students flowed around her, was Seraphina.

She was watching him. And when their eyes met, she smiled.

It was a knowing smile. A hungry smile.

Then the light shifted, just for a moment, and Drevik could have sworn her eyes glowed. A faint silver luminescence that flickered and faded like candlelight.

He blinked. Rubbed his eyes. Looked again.

She was gone.

But the feeling of being watched didn't leave him for the rest of the night.

Chapter 5: The First Oath

Chapter 5: The First Oath

The blood in Drevik's veins had turned to ice water.

He stood at the threshold of the central chamber, staring up at the domed ceiling where someone had painted constellations that didn't exist in any sky he'd ever known. The stars spiraled inward toward a dark center—an eclipse, he realized. The point where all light vanished.

"You're going to stare at it all night, or are you coming in?"

Lexi's voice cut through the fog in his head. She stood beside him in ceremonial robes the color of deep wine, her auburn hair pulled back from her face in a way that made her look older. More severe. The green of her eyes caught the candlelight and threw it back like polished glass.

"Sorry." Drevik stepped forward, his own black robes swishing around his ankles. "Just trying to memorize the ceiling in case I don't survive this."

"Comforting." She fell into step beside him, her shoulder brushing his. "The Oath isn't death. It's just... permanent."

"That's what everyone keeps saying about everything in this place."

The chamber was circular, ringed with pillars of black stone that drink the light from the hundreds of candles scattered throughout. At the center stood an altar—or what Drevik assumed was an altar. It looked more like a slab of obsidian that had grown directly from the floor, its surface carved with symbols that shifted when he looked at them too long.

Twelve figures waited in a semicircle behind the altar. The Elders, he'd been told. Men and women of various ages, their faces masked by shadows that had nothing to do with the lighting.
