At the center stood a woman who needed no introduction—the Veiled One, the Order's supreme leader. Her features were impossible to focus on, as if his eyes simply refused to hold her image.

"Kneel," Lexi said.

Drevik dropped to one knee, the cold stone seeping through the thin fabric of his robes. Lexi knelt beside him, close enough that he could smell something sharp and clean beneath the incense—wintergreen, maybe. Or just the scent of her skin.

"Acolyte Drevik Markinsoul." The Veiled One's voice came from everywhere and nowhere, resonating in his chest like a second heartbeat. "You stand before the Order of the Eclipsed Veil to bind yourself to our purpose. Do you come freely, or do you come by coercion?"

"Freely." The word scraped out of his dry throat.

"Do you understand that this bond, once forged, cannot be broken save by death or the judgment of this Order?"

"I understand."

"Do you swear to hold our secrets, guard our boundaries, and serve the balance between worlds until your final breath releases you?"

"I swear."

The Veiled One raised one hand, and the shadows around the room lean inward. "Then take the hand of your sponsor and speak the words that will seal your fate."

Lexi's fingers found his.

The moment their skin touched, something electric shot up Drevik's arm. Not painful—startling. Like touching a live wire wrapped in velvet. He heard Lexi's sharp intake of breath and knew she'd felt it too.

Her eyes met his, wide for just a second before the careful mask slid back into place.

What was that?

No time to ask. The words were rising in his throat unbidden, as if someone had written them on the inside of his skull.

"By shadow's grace and moon's embrace, I bind myself to this Order's face. My secrets theirs, my silence deep, my promises eternal keep. Through darkness walking, light I'll seek, until my spirit's final speak."

The words tasted like copper on his tongue. As the last syllable left his lips, the symbols on the obsidian altar flared silver-white, and Drevik felt something wrap around his heart. Not physically—but something deeper. Something that had found a place inside him and settled there like a stone dropped into still water.

"It is done." The Veiled One lowered her hand. "Rise, Acolyte Markinsoul. You are one of us now."

The ceremony had taken less than twenty minutes.

The aftermath lasted hours.

Drevik sat on a bench in one of the compound's many corridors, his head between his knees, trying to remember how to breathe normally. The black robes had been exchanged for his regular clothes, but nothing felt regular anymore. The weight in his chest was constant—a presence that pressed against his ribs like a second skeleton.

"You look like you're about to vomit."

He lifted his head. Lexi leaned against the wall across from him, arms crossed, watching him with an expression caught between amusement and something softer.

"Feel like it too." He straightened, running a hand through his hair. "Is it always like this? This... heaviness?"

"You get used to it." She pushed off the wall and walked toward him, stopping just outside what he was beginning to think of as her personal space. "The Oath settles after a few days. Becomes background noise."

"Background noise." Drevik laughed, but it came out strangled. "Great. So I'll just have a magical collar around my soul that I can eventually learn to ignore."

Something flickered across her face. "It's not a collar. It's a promise."

"A promise I can't break."

"No." Her voice was quiet. "You really can't."

He stood, frustration pricking at him. "And what happens if I do? If I slip up, say something I shouldn't, break some rule I don't even know exists yet?"

Lexi didn't answer. Her silence was answer enough.

Drevik moved past her, heading for the corridor that would lead him back to the surface, back to the normal world where magic wasn't real and people didn't bind their souls to shadow cults—

Pain exploded through his chest.

He doubled over, gasping, his vision going white at the edges. It was like someone had reached inside him and squeezed his heart until it nearly burst. The weight of the Oath had been a presence before; now it was a weapon, crushing him from the inside out.

"Drevik!" Lexi's hands were on his shoulders, steadying him. "What—what did you do?"

"I don't—" He couldn't finish the sentence. The pain was receding, leaving him shaking and sweat-soaked. "I was just... I was trying to leave."

"Trying to—" Her eyes went wide. "You were thinking about leaving the Order?"

"I was thinking about going back to my dorm!"

The pain spiked again, less severe this time but still enough to make him stagger. Lexi caught him, her arm wrapping around his waist to hold him upright.

"Stop," she said. "Stop thinking about it. The Oath punishes intent, not just action. If you're genuinely considering breaking your bond—even subconsciously—it will correct you."

"Correct me." Drevik wheezed the words. "That's what we're calling it?"

"Focus on something else." Her voice was close to his ear, her breath warm against his skin. "Think about... about your classes. About anything that ties you to this life."

He tried. He thought about his art history final, about the coffee shop on campus that never got his order right, about the way his grandmother used to sing while she cooked—

The pain faded. His heartbeat steadied.

Lexi guided him back to the bench, and this time she sat beside him. Close enough that their knees touched.

"The Oath interprets through your own mind," she said. "If you genuinely want to leave, it will stop you. But if you're just... overwhelmed, scared, needing space—it can tell the difference. Usually."

"Usually."

"It takes practice." She was looking at her hands, folded in her lap. "I've seen grown men cry during their first week. One of them tried to run. He made it three steps before the Oath put him on the ground."

Drevik's mouth was dry. "What happened to him?"

"He learned." Her jaw tightened. "Or he didn't, and he died during his first mission because he was too focused on escaping to pay attention to the shadows."

The words hung between them. Drevik studied her profile—the strong line of her nose, the tension in her shoulders, the way she wouldn't quite meet his eyes.

"You really hate that I'm here," he said. "Don't you?"

"I don't hate you."

"That's not the same thing."

Lexi was silent for a long moment. Then, so quietly he almost missed it: "I had a trainee once. Before you."

The confession hung in the air. Drevik waited.

"His name was Marcus." Her voice was flat, controlled. "He was born into the Order like me. We grew up together. Trained together. When I was promoted to Seeker, they assigned him to me as my first acolyte."

She finally looked at him, and her eyes were raw. "I was supposed to protect him. Teach him. Keep him alive long enough to become a Seeker himself."

"What happened?"

"We were patrolling during a new moon. Standard reconnaissance—nothing dangerous. But there was a breach we didn't see coming. A shadow creature had slipped through somewhere in the city, and we walked right into its path." Her hands had clenched into fists. "I told him to run. He stayed to cover my retreat."

Drevik didn't need her to finish. He saw it in the way she held herself, the defensive curl of her shoulders.

"He didn't make it," she said. "And every time I look at you, I remember that I'm the reason someone's son didn't come home."

The weight of her words settled over them. Drevik understood now—the sharpness in her voice when she corrected him, the way she pushed him harder than seemed fair. It wasn't distrust. It was fear.

"Lexi." He kept his voice gentle. "I'm not him."

"I know."

"And you're not going to get me killed."

"You don't know that."

"No." He reached out, hesitated, then let his hand settle over hers where it rested on her knee. Her skin was warm. "But you're going to teach me how to survive. And I'm going to listen."

Her eyes searched his face, looking for something—mockery, maybe, or empty promises. Whatever she found there made her exhale slowly.

"You're really not what I expected," she said.

"Is that a compliment?"

"I haven't decided yet." But her fingers turned under his, clasping back. The touch was brief—just a moment of contact—but something in it resonated. The same electric warmth he'd felt during the ceremony, muted now but still present.

She pulled back first, standing abruptly. "Come on. There's someone you need to meet before your first patrol."

The library was tucked into a corner of the compound that Drevik hadn't explored yet, hidden behind a door that looked like solid stone until Lexi pressed her palm to a specific spot and the wall simply... moved.

Beyond it lay a space that shouldn't have been possible. Shelves stretched toward a ceiling lost in shadow, filled with books and scrolls and objects Drevik couldn't name. The air smelled of old paper and something sweeter—jasmine, maybe, or honeysuckle.

"Ivy?" Lexi's voice echoed slightly. "Are you in here?"

A head popped out from between two towering bookcases. Dark hair cut in a sharp bob. Round glasses perched on a nose dusted with freckles. A face that might have been anywhere from twenty to thirty, with an expression of perpetual curiosity.

"Finally!" The woman—Ivy—emerged fully, clutching a leather-bound tome to her chest. "I've been waiting for hours. Is this the new one?"

"Drevik Markinsoul." Lexi gestured. "Ivy Chen. She runs the archives and handles mission briefings for acolytes."

"Pleasure to meet you." Ivy pushed her glasses up her nose and smiled. It was a warm smile, genuine in a way that made Drevik's chest ache. "Welcome to the Order. Try not to die in your first month—it's embarrassing for everyone."

"I'll... do my best?"

"She's joking." Lexi's voice was flat. "Mostly."

Ivy set the tome on a nearby table and began rifling through its pages. "Your patrol assignment came through this morning. New moon is in three days, which means you'll be walking the eastern boundary of the campus. Standard reconnaissance, minimal risk."

"Minimal risk." Drevik repeated the words. "That's what Lexi said about her last mission with a trainee."

Ivy's hands stilled. Her eyes lifted to meet his, and something shifted in her expression. "You've been told about Marcus."

"Heard about it, more like."

"Then you understand why Lexi is the way she is." Ivy's voice was gentle. "She blames herself. She'll always blame herself. But she's also the best trainer the Order has ever produced. If anyone can keep you alive, it's her."

She pulled a folded parchment from the tome and handed it to Drevik. "Your patrol route. Memorize it before the new moon and destroy the paper. We don't leave evidence lying around."

Drevik unfolded the parchment, studying the lines that traced a path through the campus. "Eastern boundary... this goes through the old cemetery behind the chapel."

"Cemeteries are thin spots," Ivy said. "The veil between worlds is weaker there. We patrol them to make sure nothing slips through."

"Charming."

"You'll get used to it." Ivy smiled again, that warm, genuine expression that made Drevik want to trust her immediately. "And if you have questions, I'm always here. The archives are my domain, but I make exceptions for people who bring me coffee."

"I'll remember that."

"See that you do." She turned back to her books, dismissing them with the ease of someone who had far too much to do and not enough time to do it. "Good luck, Acolyte Markinsoul. You'll need it."

Lexi walked him back toward the surface in silence. The compound's corridors seemed longer in the aftermath of the Oath ceremony, the shadows deeper. Or maybe Drevik was just seeing them more clearly now.

"The pain will fade," Lexi said as they climbed the stairs that led to the hidden exit. "The Oath is aggressive at first because it's establishing boundaries. Once you understand what you can and can't do, it stops being so... insistent."

"And if I want out later? After I've proven myself?"

She stopped at the top of the stairs, her hand on the door that would lead them back to the ordinary world. Her profile was lit by the single bulb overhead, casting half her face in shadow.

"There is no out, Drevik. Not really. Once you're bound, you're bound for life. The only release is death." Her voice was soft. "That's why the Oath ceremony is so important. That's why we make sure initiates understand what they're agreeing to before they speak the words."

"Did you understand? When you took your Oath?"

"I was born into this." She opened the door, letting in the cool night air. "I never knew anything else."

They emerged into a narrow alley between two campus buildings, the entrance to the compound hidden behind a illusion that made the wall look like solid brick. Drevik's dorm was a ten-minute walk from here. His art history textbook was waiting on his desk. His normal life was right there, just within reach.

But the weight in his chest reminded him that normal wasn't really an option anymore.

"I'll see you for training tomorrow," Lexi said. "Six AM. Don't be late."

"Lexi." He caught her arm before she could disappear back into the compound. "Thank you. For telling me about Marcus."

She looked at his hand on her sleeve, then up at his face. "Don't make me regret it."

She pulled free and stepped back through the hidden door. It closed behind her with a sound like a sigh.

Drevik stood alone in the alley, the parchment with his patrol route pressed against his chest. Three days until the new moon. Three days until his first real mission.

He started walking toward his dorm, his mind racing through everything he'd learned. The Oath, the weight in his chest, Lexi's confession, Ivy's briefing—

Ivy.

Something about their meeting tugged at his memory. She'd been so focused on the briefing, so professional. But there had been a moment, just before he left, when her eyes had met his and held.

He pulled out the parchment to study it again, memorizing the route while it was still fresh.

A folded piece of paper fell out from between the layers.

Drevik frowned, bending to pick it up. It hadn't been there before—he would have noticed. Which meant Ivy had slipped it to him during the briefing, quick enough that even Lexi hadn't seen.

He unfolded it, the streetlamp overhead casting just enough light to read the words.

The patrol route you were given is wrong. Don't follow it. Come see me before the new moon.

Drevik stared at the message, his heart rate spiking.

Wrong.

He looked back toward the alley where the compound entrance lay hidden, then down at the note in his hand.

The weight of the Oath pressed against his ribs—not painfully, but insistently. A reminder that he was bound now, that his choices weren't entirely his own.

But Ivy was part of the Order. Ivy had given him this note. Ivy, with her warm smile and her freckled nose and her knowledge of things that shouldn't be known.

He folded the paper carefully and tucked it into his pocket.

Three days until the new moon.

He had until then to figure out who, exactly, he could trust.

The walk back to his dorm passed in a blur. Drevik's mind churned through possibilities, each one more unlikely than the last. Ivy was the Order's librarian—why would she give him a false patrol route? Was it a test? A trap? A genuine warning from someone who saw something others didn't?

He let himself into his room, grateful that his roommate was out, and sat on the edge of his bed with his head in his hands.

The Oath hummed in his chest. Not painful—just present. A constant reminder that he wasn't alone in his own body anymore.

You asked for this, he reminded himself. You wanted to belong somewhere.

But belonging had a price. He was just beginning to understand how steep that price might be.

His phone buzzed. A text from an unknown number.

Training tomorrow. 6AM. Don't eat breakfast beforehand—you'll probably lose it. - L

Drevik almost smiled. Lexi's version of encouragement.

He typed back: Looking forward to it.

His phone buzzed again, almost immediately.

You're a terrible liar, Markinsoul.

He was still smiling when he fell asleep, the weight of the Oath settling into his bones like something that had always been there, just waiting for him to notice.

The dreams came in fragments.

Shadow creatures with too many eyes. Lexi's voice calling out a warning. A path through a cemetery that led somewhere the map didn't show. Ivy's hand pressing a note into his palm, her fingers lingering longer than necessary.

And beneath it all, a pulse of magic that beat in time with his own heart.

He woke before dawn, the Oath humming in his chest, and knew that nothing would ever be simple again.

End of Chapter 5

Chapter 6: Research and Revelations

Chapter 6: Research and Revelations

The library smelled of dust and secrets.

Drevik descended the spiral staircase carved into living stone, each step taking him deeper beneath the university campus. The air grew cooler, carrying the scent of aged paper and something else—something that prickled at the back of his neck. Old magic. The kind that seeped into walls over centuries and never quite faded.

The staircase emptied into a chamber that stole his breath.

Shelves stretched upward into shadows that the flickering amber lights couldn't penetrate. Rolling ladders clung to brass rails along the stacks, and leather-bound volumes lined every surface in neat, weathered rows. The space dwarfed any library he'd seen above ground—a cathedral of knowledge hidden beneath the mundane world.

"You're late."

He turned toward the voice. Ivy sat at a reading table near the center of the room, her pale fingers wrapped around a cup of tea that steamed in the cool air. In the dim light, her silver-blonde hair seemed almost luminous, and her gray eyes tracked his approach with an intensity that belied her small frame.

"I had to make sure I wasn't followed," Drevik said, sliding into the chair across from her. "After what happened at the ceremony—"

"I know what happened." Ivy's voice dropped lower. "Everyone knows. The question is whether you understand what it means."

The weight of her gaze settled on him. This wasn't the shy, stammering woman he'd glimpsed during his initiation. In her domain, surrounded by books and the comfortable weight of knowledge, she held herself differently. Straighter. More certain.

"That's why I'm here." He leaned forward, forearms resting on the worn wood of the table. "You said you could help me understand the warning."

"I said I might be able to." She reached beneath the table and produced a leather satchel, worn soft with age. "What I'm about to show you could get us both stripped of our standing. Or worse."

"Ivy—"

"Do you understand what 'worse' means in the Order?" She met his eyes, and he saw fear there—genuine fear. "We don't just lose membership. We lose memories. They have ways of making people forget."

The silence held between them.

"I need to know," Drevik said finally. "Whatever the risk."

Ivy studied him for a long moment. Then she nodded, more to herself than to him, and opened the satchel.

The book she withdrew was different from the others he'd seen in the Order's collection. Its cover was plain black leather, unmarked by title or symbol, but the pages inside were thick vellum covered in cramped handwriting. Some entries had been pasted in—clippings from newspapers, photographs, fragments of documents with official seals partially visible.

"This is a compilation," Ivy explained, her voice barely above a whisper. "Records that were supposed to be destroyed but... weren't. I found it hidden in a false panel behind the stacks on restricted texts. Someone wanted this information preserved."

She turned to a page near the middle and slid the book toward him.

Drevik's breath held.

A photograph had been mounted there, slightly yellowed with age. Two figures stood before a building he recognized—the old chapel on the north end of campus, now used for storage. The man had dark hair and striking green-gold eyes that look through the camera, through time itself. The woman beside him was smaller, with a determined set to her jaw and hands that gripped a bundle wrapped in blankets.

A baby. They were holding a baby.

"Markinsoul," Ivy said. "That's the name they gave when they joined. But before that—" She flipped to another page, a clipped document with portions blacked out. "Before that, they were the Thornes. A family line the Order thought had been eliminated three generations ago."

Drevik's hands trembled as he traced the edge of the photograph. "My parents."

"I thought so." Ivy's voice carried a gentleness he hadn't expected. "The resemblance is unmistakable."

"What happened to them? The official story was an accident—"

"The official story is always an accident." Ivy turned more pages. "But this compilation suggests otherwise. Your parents discovered something about the Order's leadership. Something about connections between certain Elders and the Shadowlands breaches they're supposed to prevent."

The room contract around him. "They were killed?"

"I can't prove it. The relevant pages were removed before I found this." She gestured to a section where stubs of torn paper jutted from the binding. "But someone wanted you to know the partial truth. Otherwise, this book would have been destroyed entirely."

Drevik sat back, trying to process the flood of information. His grandmother had told him his parents died in a car accident when he was two. Just two young people, gone too soon, leaving their son to be raised by a woman who loved him fiercely but never spoke of what came before.

"Why would someone want me to know?"

"That's the question, isn't it?" Ivy's gray eyes held his. "The Order isn't unified. There are factions, alliances, rivalries that span generations. Someone is positioning you for a purpose. Whether that purpose serves you or serves them..."

She let the sentence hang.

Drevik looked down at the compilation again. His parents' faces stared back at him, frozen in a moment of hope. They'd been young. Younger than he was now, with their whole lives ahead of them. And they'd held him in their arms, wrapped in blankets, unaware of whatever fate was already closing in.

"What did they discover?" he asked.

"I don't know." Ivy's brow furrowed. "But I found something else. Something that concerns you directly."

She reached into the satchel again and produced a folded map. When she spread it across the table, Drevik recognized the campus layout—the buildings, the pathways, the green spaces between. Red marks dotted certain locations, and his patrol route from the assignment ceremony was traced in careful black ink.

"Your assigned patrol." Ivy traced the line with one finger. "It passes through three marked zones."

"Marked how?"

"Active breach points." She looked up at him, and the fear was back in her eyes. "Places where the barrier between our world and the Shadowlands has worn thin. Where entities sometimes slip through."

The temperature in the library drop several degrees.

"New acolytes don't patrol breach zones," Drevik said. "We're not trained for that. We don't have the skills—"

"Exactly." Ivy's voice was grim. "Whoever made your assignment either made a catastrophic error, or they intended for you to encounter something you couldn't handle."

"Someone wants me dead."

The words fell into the silence between them. Saying them aloud made them real in a way they hadn't been before.

"I can't be certain." Ivy folded the map, her movements precise and careful. "But the pattern is troubling. Your parents were eliminated for what they knew. You're their son, possibly carrying their abilities—abilities the Order finds threatening. And now you've been assigned to a patrol route that would put you in mortal danger before you've completed basic training."

Drevik pushed back from the table and stood, pacing between the shelves. His mind raced through possibilities. Elder Vance had made the assignments personally, pulling names from what he'd claimed was random selection. But nothing in the Order was random. Every ritual, every ceremony, every seemingly arbitrary choice served a purpose.

"Who else knows about this?"

"About your parents? Perhaps a dozen people, most of them Elders." Ivy's gaze followed him as he moved. "About the patrol assignment? Only those who have access to the breach zone maps. Wardens and above."

"That narrows it."

"It narrows it to people who have every reason to protect the Order's secrets." Ivy stood as well, her slight frame seeming smaller against the towering shelves. "Drevik, I'm taking a risk just showing you this. If anyone discovers—"

"Why are you helping me?"

The question stopped her. She wrapped her arms around herself, a gesture that made her look younger, more vulnerable.

"Because I know what it's like to have questions you can't ask." Her voice was quiet. "My mother was an archivist too. She found things she shouldn't have found, asked questions that made people uncomfortable. One day she was sent on a research mission to a remote site, and she never came back."

"Ivy..."

"The official story was a Shadowlands encounter." Her smile was bitter. "But my mother was the most cautious woman I ever knew. She wouldn't have ventured near a breach zone without full preparation and backup. Someone sent her there deliberately. Someone who wanted her silenced."

Drevik stopped pacing. The parallels were too obvious to ignore—mothers killed in supposed accidents, children left with questions no one would answer. Ivy had lost her mother the same way he'd lost his parents, and she'd been living with that knowledge far longer.

"You think the same people are behind both."

"I think the Order has rot in its foundation." Ivy met his eyes steadily. "I think someone has been eliminating members who get too close to certain truths for generations. And I think you've been marked as the next target."

The words settled over him.

"Then why assign me to a dangerous patrol? Why not just... eliminate me quietly?"

"Because your death has to look like an accident. Or a training mishap. Something that won't raise questions." Ivy returned to her chair, lowering herself with a weariness that spoke of years carrying this burden. "A new acolyte dying on patrol would be tragic but not suspicious. Especially if he'd shown unusual ability but hadn't completed his training. The Order would mourn, investigate briefly, and move on."

Drevik resumed his seat across from her. The compilation lay between them, his parents' faces watching from the photograph. They'd been young, hopeful, holding their infant son. They'd had no idea what was coming.

"Who can I trust?"

The question came out rougher than he intended. Ivy's expression softened.

"Trust is dangerous in the Order. Even the people who seem supportive may be serving their own agendas." She hesitated. "But there are others who have reason to question the official narratives. Others who've lost people to 'accidents' that don't add up."

"Like you."

"Like me." She offered a tentative smile. "And perhaps the Vane woman. Seraphina. Her family has been at odds with the current leadership for years. And Lexi Thornwood—"

"Lexi?" Drevik's surprise was genuine. "She's been nothing but hostile since I arrived."

"Her mother is an Elder." Ivy's tone carried a warning. "But Lexi herself has questioned certain decisions. She's not her mother's puppet, whatever appearances might suggest. She may be watching you for her own reasons, not the ones you assume."

Drevik thought of Lexi's sharp green eyes, the way she'd looked at him during the ceremony. He'd interpreted her intensity as suspicion, perhaps hostility. But what if there was more to it?

"The way you describe it, everyone has hidden motives."

"Welcome to the Order." Ivy's smile was wry. "The trick is learning which motives align with your survival."

He looked at the compilation again. So many pages had been torn out, so many truths redacted. But someone had preserved this much—had hidden it where Ivy would find it. Someone wanted him to know about his parents, about the pattern of eliminations, about the danger he was in.

"Who left this book for you to find?"

"I don't know." Ivy's voice dropped to a whisper. "But they're still in the Order. They've been leaving me clues for years, guiding me toward information that's been buried. I think they're building toward something. A reckoning, maybe. And I think you're part of it."

The weight of her words pressed against him. He'd come here looking for answers about a warning during his initiation ceremony. Instead, he'd found a conspiracy that spanned generations, a target on his back, and a mystery that reached into his own bloodline.

"What do I do now?"

Ivy reached across the table. Her fingers brushed against his hand—a light touch, barely there—but he felt the warmth of her skin like a brand. She pulled back quickly, color rising in her cheeks.

"Learn." Her voice was slightly unsteady. "Train. Grow stronger. The Order values power above all else. If you become valuable enough, you become harder to eliminate quietly. And when the time comes..."

"When the time comes?"

"You'll know who to trust." She met his eyes again, and something shifted in her expression. The fear was still there, but beneath it was something else. Determination, maybe. Or something warmer.

"You're taking a risk, helping me," Drevik said. "If they're watching..."

"They watch everyone." Ivy's hand moved toward his again, then retreated. "But the library has... advantages. Certain sections are shielded from surveillance. Old protections, built into the walls before the current leadership took power. As long as we meet here, in the right places, we can speak freely."

"Shielded how?"

"Blood wards. Lay them yourself and they answer to you alone." Ivy gestured to the walls. "The founders of the Order believed knowledge should be protected from those who would suppress it. They built safeguards into the very architecture."

Drevik looked at the shelves with new appreciation. The library wasn't just a repository—it was a fortress. A place where truth could survive even when those in power wanted it buried.

"Can you teach me? The blood wards, the protections?"

Ivy's smile widened. "That's why I brought you here." She stood, moving toward the shelves with a confidence that transformed her bearing. "Follow me. There's something else you need to see."

He followed her deeper into the stacks, past sections labeled in languages he didn't recognize. The light grew dimmer as they walked, the amber lamps giving way to shadows that breathe with ancient power. Finally, she stopped before a shelf that looked no different from any other.

"This section is warded. Nothing that happens here can be observed from outside." She pressed her palm to a symbol carved into the wood—a crescent moon wrapped in thorns. "When you're ready, you can claim your own space. Create your own sanctuary."

The symbol pulsed faintly beneath her hand, and Drevik felt the brush of magic against his senses. Old, deep, patient.

"How?"

"Blood and intent." Ivy withdrew a small knife from her pocket—the blade no longer than her finger. "A drop is enough. You press it to the ward and speak your purpose. The magic accepts you or it doesn't."

"And if it doesn't accept me?"

"Then you're not meant to have a sanctuary here." Her gray eyes held his. "But I think you will be. The wards know bloodlines. They remember those who came before."

His parents. The thought struck him. Had they stood where he stood? Had they pressed their palms to symbols and spoken their purpose?

Ivy offered him the knife, handle first. Her fingers brushed his as he took it—a lingering contact this time, deliberate despite the flush that crept up her neck.

"Try it."

Drevik pressed the blade to his palm. The sting was brief, a single drop of blood welling against his skin. He reached for the symbol beside Ivy's and pressed his palm flat against the wood.

"I claim this space for knowledge and protection," he said, the words coming without thought. "For truth that cannot be silenced."

The wood warmed beneath his hand. The symbol glow—faintly at first, then brighter, a deep amber that matched the library's lamps. Power rushed through him, settling into his bones with the weight of centuries.

"Interesting," Ivy said. "The wards recognize you. That's... rare."

"What does it mean?"

"It means this library knows your blood." She was staring at him with something like wonder. "It means you belong here."

Drevik withdrew his hand. The cut had already healed, leaving no mark—but he felt the connection now. A thread of power linking him to the walls, the shelves, the accumulated knowledge of generations.

"Thank you." He turned to Ivy, seeing her properly for the first time. Not just a shy archivist, but a woman who'd risked everything to bring him here. A woman who understood loss and questions and the weight of forbidden truth.

Her cheeks colored under his attention. "I— there's more I need to tell you. But not here." She glanced toward the shadows between the shelves. "Someone's watching. Not the wards—someone physical. I've noticed the signs for weeks. We need to be careful about when we meet and where."

"Then how do I find you?"

"I'll send a message through the usual channels. A book request with a specific call number." Her smile was fleeting. "Archivists communicate in our own language. No one else will understand."

"Ivy." He caught her arm as she turned away. Her skin was warm through the thin fabric of her sleeve. "Why are you doing this? Really?"

She looked at his hand on her arm, then up at his face. For a moment, her mask slipped entirely, and he saw the loneliness beneath—the years of carrying her mother's death in silence, the fear that had become her constant companion, the hope that flickered despite everything.

"Because you're the first person who might actually do something about it." Her voice was barely audible. "Because when I saw you at your initiation, I recognized something. You're not just here by accident. You're meant for something. And I want to be part of it."

She pulled away gently, but her eyes stayed on his a moment longer.

"Be careful, Drevik. The patrol assignment can be changed if you petition the right people. Find an excuse—illness, injury, family emergency. Buy yourself time to train. To grow stronger." Her voice steadied. "And watch the women who watch you. Not all attention is hostile. Some of us are hoping you succeed."

Then she was gone, disappearing between the shelves with a silence that spoke of years learning to move unnoticed.

Drevik stood alone in the warded section, the compilation tucked under his arm, the weight of revelation settling onto his shoulders. His parents had been killed for what they knew. Someone in the Order wanted him dead. And a shy archivist with gray eyes and a backbone of steel had just become his most unlikely ally.

He looked down at the photograph again. His mother's jaw, determined even in a moment of joy. His father's eyes, green-gold and piercing, seeing futures perhaps even then.

What did you find? he asked the silent faces. What truth was worth dying for?

The library offered no answers. But somewhere in its depths, hidden behind wards and blood and centuries of accumulated knowledge, the truth waited.

And Drevik Markinsoul intended to find it.

The journey back to the surface felt longer than the descent. Drevik climbed the spiral staircase in a daze, his mind turning over everything Ivy had revealed. His parents murdered. His patrol assignment sabotaged. A conspiracy spanning generations.

The Order of the Eclipsed Veil had seemed like an answer to questions he'd carried his whole life—a place where he might belong, where his strange abilities would be understood and valued. Instead, it had become something far more dangerous. A nest of vipers, each one coiled and waiting to strike.

He emerged into the night air, the campus spread before him under a sky thick with stars. The moon was waning, a thin crescent that cast weak silver light across the buildings and pathways. Somewhere in those buildings, students were studying, partying, living their mundane lives with no idea of what lurked beneath their feet.

How many of them were like him? Carrying abilities they didn't understand, drawn to secrets they couldn't name? How many would the Order recruit, use, and discard?

The questions followed him across the quad. He kept to the shadows, practicing the stealth the Order had begun teaching him, testing whether anyone was following. Twice he caught movement in his peripheral vision—other students, most likely—but he couldn't shake the feeling of eyes on his back.

When he finally reached his dorm room, he collapsed onto his bed without bothering to turn on the light. The compilation lay beside him, his parents' faces visible in the glow from the window.

His phone buzzed. A text from a number he didn't recognize.

You were in the library a long time. Careful. Curiosity killed more than cats.

He stared at the message, heart pounding. Someone had seen him enter. Someone had tracked how long he'd stayed. The Order's reach extended further than he'd imagined.

His thumb hovered over the screen, but there was no point responding. Whoever had sent this knew where he'd been, knew what he might have learned. The warning was clear: we're watching.

A second message arrived.

But curiosity also reveals truth. The right questions in the right ears. You're not alone in this.

Drevik read the words twice. The first message had been a threat. The second... something else. An offer? A promise?

He typed a reply: Who is this?

No answer came. He waited, phone glowing in the darkness, but the screen stayed silent.

Finally, he set the phone aside and closed his eyes. Sleep would be impossible now, but he needed to rest, needed to think. Tomorrow he would petition to change his patrol assignment. He would find an excuse, buy himself time. And he would watch the women around him—Lexi's hostility, Seraphina's intensity, Ivy's quiet courage—searching for the truth beneath the surface.

I'm meant for something, Ivy had said. And I want to be part of it.

The Order had brought him here for a reason. His parents had died for a reason. And somewhere in the web of secrets and lies, the truth waited to be uncovered.

Drevik Markinsoul was done being a pawn.

It was time to learn the game.

Chapter 7: New Moon Patrol

Chapter 7: New Moon Patrol

The absence of the moon was like a hole in Drevik's chest.

He stood at the compound's eastern exit, flexing his fingers against the strange emptiness where his power usually hummed. During the waxing moon, shadow magic responded to his call like an eager hound. Tonight, under the black sky of the new moon, it lay dormant—buried beneath layers of mortal weakness that made him feel suddenly, acutely human.

"You're fidgeting." Lexi appeared at his shoulder, her auburn hair pulled back in a tight braid, her combat leathers creaking softly as she moved. "Stop it. You're making me nervous, and I don't get nervous."

"Everyone gets nervous."

"I don't." Her green eyes held his for a moment too long. "Especially not on routine patrol duty."

Routine. That was the word that sat wrong in his stomach. After the warning from Elder Thornwood—after the old man had looked at him with those calculating eyes and suggested tonight's patrol might be "eventful"—routine was like a lie wrapped in comfortable paper.

"Maybe I should stay behind," Drevik said. "Without my powers—"

"You're not useless just because you can't shadow-walk." Lexi checked her blade, a short sword forged from cold iron that gleamed dully in the corridor's ambient light. "You have eyes. You have a brain. And apparently you have something the Elders find interesting, even if they won't tell us what."

She said us like she belonged to the same category. Like they were both outsiders navigating a world that didn't quite accept them. Drevik wondered if that was true, or if Lexi Thornwood—daughter of an Elder, raised within the Order's embrace—could ever understand what it meant to be truly on the outside.

"The breach parameters are standard," Lexi continued, all business now. "We walk the perimeter, check the anchor points, report anything unusual. In and out in two hours. You stay behind me, you do exactly what I say, and we both come back alive." A pause. "Clear?"

"Crystal."

"Good." She turned toward the exit, then stopped, her voice dropping. "Drevik. I know my father spoke to you. I know he warned you about tonight."

The admission hung between them.

"He told me the patrol might be dangerous."

"He told you to refuse." Lexi's jaw tightened. "He does that. Creates tests, watches how people react. It's his way of sorting the useful from the expendable." Her eyes met his again, and something flickered there—something that looked almost like concern. "You should have refused. You're still learning. Your powers are unreliable. This isn't fair to you."

"Life isn't fair. I thought you knew that."

A ghost of a smile crossed her face. "Lesson one, and you've already mastered it." She gestured toward the exit. "Come on, acolyte. Let's see if you survive the night."

The campus at midnight wore a different face than the daylight version Drevik had come to know. Familiar buildings loomed like sleeping giants, their windows dark, their shadows pooling in unnatural depths that breathe. Without the moon's light, the Veil between worlds grew thin—and the darkness grew hungry.

Lexi moved through the shadows like water through cracks, her footsteps silent, her body low and ready. Drevik followed as quietly as he could, acutely aware of every rustle of his jacket, every soft impact of his boots on concrete. He felt clumsy beside her. Exposed.

"Anchor point one is ahead," she said, barely louder than breath. "The old humanities building. There's a basement access that leads to one of the oldest Veil boundaries. Watch for cold spots. Watch for—"

She stopped. Her body went rigid.

Drevik almost collided with her back, catching himself at the last moment. "What is it?"

"Quiet."

He strained his senses, trying to see what she saw. The air had changed—not colder exactly, but thinner, like the oxygen was being pulled from his lungs. A smell reached him, faint and wrong: something like rusted copper and rotting flowers.

"Shadowlands breach," Lexi said. "Small, but active. Stay close."

She drew her blade and moved forward, and Drevik had no choice but to follow.

The breach was invisible to normal sight—a tear in reality that revealed itself through sensation rather than vision. The cold intensified as they approached, seeping through Drevik's clothes and into his bones. The darkness in the alley behind the humanities building pulse with a rhythm that matched his heartbeat, and he realized with growing horror that the shadows were moving on their own.

"Lexi—"

"I see it." Her voice was steady, but he caught the tension in her shoulders. "Stay back. This shouldn't be—"

A shape emerged from the darkness.

It was wrong in ways that defied description. The creature had too many limbs, joints bending in directions they shouldn't, its body seeming to flicker between solid and shadow like a corrupted video file. Its head—if it could be called a head—was a mass of writhing tendrils that opened to reveal a mouth lined with teeth that existed in multiple dimensions at once. Looking at them made Drevik's eyes water, made his brain ache with the effort of processing something that shouldn't exist in this reality.

"That's not a standard breach entity," Lexi said, her blade coming up. "That's not supposed to be here."

The creature turned toward them. It had no eyes, but Drevik felt its attention like ice water on his spine.

"Run," Lexi commanded. "Get back to the compound. Warn the others."

"I'm not leaving you—"

"Go!" She launched herself at the creature, blade flashing in the non-light of the breach.

Drevik's feet stayed rooted to the concrete. He watched Lexi dance around the entity's lashing limbs, her movements precise and deadly, her blade carving lines of silver light through the darkness. She was magnificent. She was also outmatched.

The creature was fast—faster than anything that size should be. One tendril whipped toward Lexi's head, and she ducked barely in time. Another came from her blind side, and she twisted away, but not cleanly. The edge of the tendril caught her shoulder, tearing through leather and flesh. She said in pain but kept moving, kept fighting.

Drevik's hands clenched at his sides. He reached for his power, for the shadow magic that usually flowed so readily, but found only emptiness. He was mortal tonight. Helpless.

No, something inside him said. Not helpless. Different.

And then the visions hit.

One moment he was standing in the alley, watching Lexi fight. The next, he was drowning in possibility.

He saw her die in three different futures, each one crystalline in its clarity:

In the first, she overextended, reaching for a killing blow, and the creature's tendrils wrapped around her throat. She crumpled, her blade clattering uselessly to the ground.

In the second, she retreated toward the wall, and the entity shifted tactics, driving her backward until she had nowhere to go. It consumed her slowly, deliberately, its teeth working through her body while she screamed.

In the third, she called for Drevik to help, distracting herself for a single heartbeat. That heartbeat was all it took. The tendril pierced her chest, and she looked at him with surprised eyes as the light faded.

Three deaths. Three futures that ended in blood and silence.

But there was a fourth.

Drevik saw himself moving, his body acting without conscious thought. He saw himself sprint toward the fight, toward the creature's left flank. He saw himself grab Lexi's arm at the precise moment the entity's attention shifted, pulling her down and to the side as a tendril whipped through the space where her head had been. He saw them tumble together into a narrow alcove between buildings, pressed together in the darkness, the creature's limbs scraping uselessly against the stone walls.

In that future, they survived.

The visions collapsed back into the present, and Drevik moved.

He didn't think. He didn't plan. He simply acted, his body following the path his foresight had laid out like a map. Sprint. Left flank. Grab.

"Drevik, no—!"

His hand closed around Lexi's arm. He yanked, pulling her off-balance, sending them both tumbling toward the narrow gap between buildings. They hit the ground hard, Drevik's shoulder slamming into concrete, Lexi's weight landing half on top of him as they rolled into the alcove.

The creature screamed—a sound that wasn't a sound, that bypassed the ears and struck directly at the mind. Its tendrils lashed at the walls around them, scraping stone, searching for purchase, but the alcove was too narrow. It couldn't reach them.

They lay tangled together in the darkness, breathing hard. Lexi's face was inches from his, her green eyes wide with shock and fury and something else—something vulnerable that she quickly buried beneath anger.

"What the hell was that?" she demanded. "You could have gotten us both killed!"

"You were going to die." Drevik's voice came out hoarse. "I saw it. Three different ways. This was the only one where you lived."

Lexi stared at him. "You saw—"

"Possible futures. I think." He swallowed hard. "It's happened before. But never like this. Never so clearly."

Above them, the creature writhed and screamed, its tendrils probing the alcove's entrance but unable to squeeze through. It was trapped on this side of the Veil, and it knew it. The breach behind it was already beginning to close, the Shadowlands pulling back like a receding tide.

"We need to move," Lexi said. "Before it finds another way in."

"Can you walk?"

"Can you stop asking stupid questions?" She pushed herself up, wincing as her injured shoulder took weight. Drevik saw the blood now, dark against the torn leather, and reached for her instinctively.

"Your arm—"

"Is fine." She caught his wrist before he could touch her. Her grip was firm, but not unkind. "I've had worse. Much worse." A pause. "Thank you."

The words cost her something. Drevik understood. Gratitude didn't come naturally to people like Lexi—people who built their lives around being the strong one, the capable one, the one who saved others.

"You don't have to—"

"I know." She released his wrist and rose, pulling him up with her. "Come on. We need to report this."

The walk back to the compound was silent, but not uncomfortable. They moved together through the shadows, Lexi's pace slightly slower than before, her wounded arm held carefully against her side. Drevik stayed close—not close enough to crowd her, but close enough to catch her if she stumbled.

He kept waiting for her to dismiss what had happened. To chalk his foresight up to luck or instinct. But she said nothing, and the silence between them felt heavy with meaning.

At the compound's entrance, she finally spoke.

"What you did back there. The visions." She turned to face him, her expression unreadable in the darkness. "That's not a common ability. Among the Order, maybe one in a hundred have any precognitive talent. And what you described—seeing multiple futures clearly enough to choose between them—"

"I know." Drevik's throat was dry. "I know it's not normal."

"Nothing about you is normal." Her voice was soft now, almost gentle. "You know that, right? The shadow-walking, the way you adapted to the Order so quickly, and now this. The Elders aren't just interested in you, Drevik. They're watching you. And that's not always a good thing."

"Is that a warning?"

"It's a fact." She stepped closer, and for a moment, Drevik thought she might reach for him. Her eyes held his, and the air between them thicken. "You saved my life tonight. I won't forget that."

"I'd do it again."

"I know." A ghost of a smile. "That's what scares me."

Before he could respond, she turned and entered the compound, leaving him standing in the doorway with his heart pounding and his thoughts racing.

The Elder's chamber was cold, lit only by candles that cast dancing shadows on the walls. Elder Thornwood sat behind his desk, his weathered face unreadable as Lexi delivered her report. Drevik stood beside her, acutely aware of every word, every pause, every flicker of the Elder's eyes toward him.

"A non-standard entity," Thornwood repeated. "During a new moon patrol."

"Yes, sir. It came through a spontaneous breach. We weren't able to determine the cause before it sealed."

"But you neutralized the threat."

Lexi hesitated. "The creature was trapped on this side of the Veil when the breach closed. It couldn't reach us in the alcove. We waited until it... dispersed."

"Dispersed." Thornwood's gaze shifted to Drevik. "And you, acolyte. What role did you play in this engagement?"

Drevik felt the weight of the question. "I provided support, sir. And I..." He trailed off, uncertain how to describe what had happened.

"He saw the creature's movements before they happened," Lexi said flatly. "He predicted where it would strike and pulled me out of the way. If he hadn't, I'd be dead."

Thornwood's expression didn't change, but something flickered in his eyes—something that looked almost like satisfaction.

"Foresight," he said. "Rare. Powerful. And manifesting during a new moon, when your other abilities are suppressed." He leaned back in his chair. "You survived. Interesting."

"Sir?" Lexi's voice was sharp. "You knew something would happen tonight. You warned Drevik—"

"I suggested the patrol might be eventful. I did not arrange for a breach." Thornwood's tone was mild, but his eyes were hard. "The Veil is weakening, Lexi. This is the third non-standard entity this month. Someone—or something—is testing our boundaries. Drevik's abilities may prove crucial in the days to come."

Drevik felt a chill that had nothing to do with the room's temperature.

"You're using him as a tool," Lexi said. "He's still an acolyte. He's still learning. You can't just—"

"I can." Thornwood rose, his tall frame casting long shadows in the candlelight. "And I will. The Order's survival depends on using every resource at our disposal. Your feelings for the boy do not change that."

Lexi's jaw tightened. "My feelings—"

"Are obvious to anyone who watches you." Thornwood's gaze moved between them. "I suggest you both rest. Tomorrow will bring new challenges, and I expect you to face them with clear heads."

The dismissal was clear. Lexi turned sharply and strode from the room, and Drevik had no choice but to follow.

She stopped him in the corridor outside, her hand catching his arm. Her grip was tight, her eyes bright with frustration and something else—something that made his pulse quicken.

"Don't let him use you," she said. "My father. He'll sacrifice anyone for what he calls the greater good. He's done it before."

"I'm not planning to be sacrificed."

"No one plans it." She stepped closer, and Drevik could smell the copper tang of her blood, the faint trace of something floral beneath it. Her breath was warm against his face. "You did well tonight. Better than I expected. But that doesn't make you safe."

"Nothing makes me safe." He held her gaze. "I learned that lesson before I joined the Order."

Something shifted in her expression. The anger drained away, leaving behind a rawness that made his chest ache.

"I don't want to see you hurt," she said. "I know I've been hard on you. I know I pushed you away. But tonight, when that thing had me cornered—when I thought I was going to die—"

"You weren't going to die. Not if I could stop it."

"Why?" The word came out broken. "Why would you risk yourself for me?"

Drevik reached up, brushing a strand of hair from her face. His fingers lingered at her temple, and he felt her tremble.

"Because you're worth saving," he said simply.

Lexi made a sound that might have been a laugh or a sob. She leaned into his touch, her eyes closing, and for a moment they stood there—two people who had been fighting each other for weeks, finally letting their guards down.

Then she pulled away, and the walls came back up.

"Get some sleep, acolyte." Her voice was steady again, but something had changed. The distance between them was smaller now. "Tomorrow, we train. If you're going to survive whatever my father has planned, you need to be ready."

"Lexi—"

"Good night, Drevik."

She walked away, and he watched her go, his fingers still tingling where they'd touched her skin.

In his room, Drevik sat on the edge of his bed and tried to make sense of the night. The visions had faded, but he could still feel their echoes—three futures where Lexi died, and one where she lived. The weight of that knowledge pressed down on him like a physical burden.

He had saved her. That was what mattered.

But Elder Thornwood's words lingered, cold and sharp. The Veil is weakening. Someone—or something—is testing our boundaries.

And Lexi's warning echoed alongside them: He'll sacrifice anyone for what he calls the greater good.

Drevik lay back against the pillow, staring at the ceiling. His powers were growing, evolving in ways he didn't fully understand. The Order had accepted him, but that acceptance came with expectations—and with dangers he was only beginning to see.

Outside his window, the new moon hung invisible in the sky. Somewhere beyond the Veil, shadow creatures prowled and plotted. Somewhere within the compound, Elders made plans that might or might not include his survival.

And somewhere down the hall, Lexi Thornwood was probably lying awake too, her wounded shoulder throbbing, her thoughts as tangled as his own.

Drevik closed his eyes and let the darkness take him. Tomorrow would bring new challenges. Tonight, he had done enough.

He had saved her.

For now, that would have to be enough.

Chapter 8: Seraphina's Secret

Chapter 8: Seraphina's Secret

The coffee shop sat at the edge of campus, wedged between a used bookstore and a psychic's parlor that had never have any customers. Drevik had walked past it dozens of times without giving it a second thought. Now, standing across the street with the afternoon light slanting low through the autumn leaves, he studied it like it might hold answers to questions he hadn't known to ask until today.

The Black Roast. Clever name for a place that catered to the Order's members, though he suspected most of its regular patrons had no idea what lurked beneath the surface of their everyday lives.

His phone buzzed in his pocket. A single word from an unknown number: Inside.

Seraphina had found him first, of course. She always did.

He crossed the street, pushing through the coffee shop's heavy wooden door. The interior was all exposed brick and warm lighting, the kind of aggressively cozy atmosphere that tried too hard to feel authentic. A handful of students hunched over laptops in worn armchairs. An older man in a tweed jacket read a newspaper by the window, his coffee going cold beside him.

And in the far corner, partially hidden by a shelf of dog-eared paperbacks, sat Seraphina Vane.

She looked different than she had at the gala. Gone were the elegant gown and the confident smirk she wore like armor. Instead, she'd pulled her dark hair into a loose ponytail and dressed in clothes that blended in—dark jeans, a gray sweater that looked soft enough to sleep in, boots that had seen better days. She could have been any student killing time between classes.

Except for the way her eyes tracked every person who entered or left. The way her fingers tapped a restless rhythm against her cup. The tension in her shoulders.

Drevik approached slowly, giving her time to signal if something was wrong. She met his gaze and held up one finger—not wait, but caution. One moment.

He ordered a black coffee he didn't want and settled into the chair across from her only when she finally nodded.

"You came," she said. Her voice was pitched low, barely audible over the hiss of the espresso machine. "I wasn't certain you would."

"You said you had information. About the attack on the ceremony." He wrapped his hands around his cup, letting the heat ground him. "And about me."

Seraphina's jaw tightened. She glanced toward the window, then the door, then the back hallway that presumably led to restrooms and a storage closet. Watching. Waiting.

"I have enemies, Drevik. Enemies who would pay dearly to know I'm sitting here with you right now."

"Then why meet at all?"

She turned back to him, and for the first time since he'd known her, her confident mask slipped. What showed beneath was something raw and frightened and fiercely determined.

"Because you're the only one who might understand what I'm about to tell you. The only one with the power to do something about it."

Her hand moved toward his across the table—stopped just short of touching. "I need your help. And in exchange, I'll give you the truth about everything. The attack. The traitor. And why they targeted you specifically."

Drevik studied her face. The shadows under her eyes suggested she hadn't been sleeping. The slight tremor in her hands spoke of more than caffeine. This wasn't the Seraphina Vane who had charmed and manipulated her way through the Order's social circles. This was someone else entirely—someone who had been hiding in plain sight, just like him.

"Start talking," he said. "We don't have much time."

She exhaled slowly. Nodded.

"Not here. Somewhere with shadows."

The alley behind the coffee shop was narrow and poorly lit, sandwiched between the building and a high brick wall. The afternoon sun couldn't reach it, leaving pools of darkness between the overflowing dumpsters and the fire escapes.

Drevik followed Seraphina into the dimness, his senses sharpening. This was a terrible idea—following a woman he barely trusted into an enclosed space where anyone could be waiting. But the alternative was letting her walk away with information he needed.

She turned to face him, pressing her back against the brick wall. Even in the shadows, he saw her chest rising and falling with quick, shallow breaths.

"What I'm about to show you," she said, "cannot leave this alley. If the wrong people learn what I am..."

"They'll kill you," Drevik finished.

"Worse." Her voice dropped to a whisper. "They'll use me. Study me. Tear me apart to understand how I exist."

"Seraphina—"

She reached up and pulled the hair from her ponytail, letting it fall loose around her shoulders. Then she closed her eyes and stepped deeper into the shadow cast by a fire escape above.

Drevik's breath held.

Where the darkness touched her skin, she glow. A soft, ethereal luminescence emanated from beneath her flesh—not from outside, but from within. Veins of pale violet light traced patterns across her arms, her throat, the delicate lines of her jaw. Her hair float in currents of invisible energy, and when she opened her eyes, they weren't the dark brown he remembered.

They were silver. Completely, impossibly silver, like polished mirrors reflecting a moon that wasn't there.

"My mother," Seraphina said, and her voice had changed too—layered now, harmonic, like two voices speaking in perfect unison, "was one of the first women to successfully return from the Shadowlands."

Drevik's mind reeled. Return from the Shadowlands. The parallel dimension that gave the Order its power, that harbored creatures of nightmare and entropy. The place where Veiled Ones went and never came back.

"That's impossible," he said. "No one returns from—"

"She did." Seraphina stepped forward, and the glow intensified. "Three days she was gone. Three days in a realm where time moves differently, where reality itself is fluid. When she came back, she was... changed. Altered. And pregnant with me."

She held out her hand, palm up. The light beneath her skin pulsed in rhythm with something deeper than a heartbeat.

"I am half human, half Shadowlands entity. The only one of my kind to survive birth. The only one the Order doesn't know about."

Drevik stared at her glowing form, trying to process what he was seeing. "How is that possible? How has no one discovered you before now?"

Seraphina's glow dimmed slightly as she pulled back from the edge of revelation. The silver faded from her eyes, leaving them brown again—but Drevik had seen what lurked beneath the surface now. He couldn't unsee it.

"My mother hid me. Taught me to control it, to suppress the glow, to blend in." Her voice was bitter. "She made me promise never to reveal what I am. And then she died, and I was left alone with a secret that could destroy me."

"Why tell me?"

She met his eyes directly. "Because you have the Sight, Drevik. You can see possibilities. You can see the paths that others miss." She moved closer, and he caught her scent—something floral and dark, like night-blooming jasmine mixed with ozone. "I've watched you. Studied you. You're different from the others in the Order. You question things. You see through lies."

"That doesn't explain why you're trusting me with your life."

Seraphina's expression shifted—vulnerability bleeding through the careful mask.

"Because I'm tired of being alone." The words came out rough, almost angry. "I'm tired of hiding. And because you're the first person I've met who might actually be able to help me instead of using me."

She was close now. Close enough that Drevik felt the heat radiating from her body—warmer than it should be, like she was running a constant low fever. Her heartbeat, when he focused on it, was slower than a human's. Much slower. And that strange, layered quality to her voice...

"You said you had information about the attack," Drevik managed. "About a traitor."

Seraphina nodded, but her gaze lingered on his face with an intensity that made his skin prickle.

"The breach at the ceremony wasn't random. Someone in the Order created it deliberately. They weakened the Veil from inside, allowing entities to pass through."

"Who?"

"I don't know the name. But I know they're planning something bigger. The full moon ceremony in three weeks—they're going to use it to open a permanent gate."

Drevik's blood ran cold. A permanent gate between the mortal realm and the Shadowlands would mean chaos. Entities flooding through unchecked. Thousands of deaths before the Order could respond.

"Why tell me this? Why not go to the Elders?"

Seraphina laughed—a brittle, hollow sound. "The Elders? Half of them would order my execution for what I am. The other half would hand me over to researchers who'd treat me like a lab rat." She shook her head. "No. You're the only one I can trust with this, Drevik. You and your strange gifts and your ridiculous sense of honor."

She reached out and touched his arm. Her fingers burned against his skin, that fever-heat seeping through his sleeve.

"I need protection. A place to hide when things get dangerous. Someone who can shield me from both the entities that hunt me and the Order members who'd destroy me." Her eyes searched his face. "In exchange, I'll be your eyes and ears. I can sense Shadowlands energy in ways even the Veiled Ones can't. I'll help you find the traitor."

Drevik's mind raced through the implications. An alliance with Seraphina would give him an incredible advantage—but it would also paint a target on his back. If the Order discovered he was sheltering a half-Shadowlands being, they'd demand answers he couldn't give. If the traitor learned she was sharing information...

"You're asking me to keep a secret from everyone I'm supposed to trust."

"I'm asking you to trust your instincts over your orders." Seraphina stepped even closer. Her body heat washed over him like a wave. "I've seen how you look at me, Drevik. I've felt the pull between us."

His throat tightened. "That's not—"

"Don't." Her voice softened. "Don't pretend you don't feel it. I'm part Shadowlands entity. I can sense these things—the way your energy reaches for mine when we're close. The way your heartbeat quickens."

Her hand slid up his arm to his shoulder, and Drevik's pulse did exactly what she said it would. Quickened. His body responding to her proximity even as his mind screamed warnings.

"You're asking a lot," he said roughly.

"I'm offering a lot." She rose on her toes, bringing her face level with his. Her breath was warm against his lips. "Partnership. Protection. And something more, if you want it."

Drevik's hand came up without his permission, settling at the curve of her waist. Her body was so warm—impossibly, intoxicatingly warm—and she leaned into his touch like she'd been starving for it.

"This is dangerous," he said.

"Everything worth having is dangerous."

Her lips brushed against his cheek, feather-light, and the touch burned through him like a brand. Heat flooded his body, pooling low in his belly, and he had to fight the urge to pull her fully against him. To take what she was offering and damn the consequences.

Then she pulled back, and the moment broke.

"Think about it," she said. "I'll find you again in a few days. We can discuss terms."

She turned to leave the alley, her silhouette fading back into the shadows—

And stopped.

Her entire body went rigid. The glow beneath her skin flickered, violet light pulsing in a rapid, panicked rhythm.

"Something's here," she said. "Something from the Shadowlands."

Drevik's senses sharpened. He reached for his own power, letting it rise to the surface, and felt it—a wrongness at the edge of the alley. A presence that didn't belong in the mortal realm.

"Entity," he said. "Tracking you?"

Seraphina's silver eyes flared. "They can sense my blood. They always know where I am eventually."

A shape coalesced in the shadows at the alley's entrance. Vague at first, then solidifying—something with too many limbs and a mouth that opened sideways. It made no sound, but Drevik felt its hunger like ice water in his veins.

"Can you shadow-walk?" he asked urgently.

"Not fast enough. It would follow."

Drevik made a decision.

"Come here." He grabbed Seraphina's arm and pulled her against him, wrapping his other arm around her waist. Her body molded to his, fever-warm and trembling. "Hold your breath."

"What—"

"Trust me."

He reached for the darkest shadow in the alley—the deep pool of black beneath the fire escape—and pushed.

The world inverted. Cold darkness swallowed them, and for a heartbeat they were nowhere and everywhere at once. Drevik felt Seraphina's gasp against his chest, felt her fingers dig into his shoulders, felt the impossible heat of her body as they tumbled through the space between spaces.

Then they emerged on the other side of the wall, in a narrow service corridor behind the coffee shop's storage room.

Drevik pressed Seraphina against the wall, his body covering hers, both of them breathing hard. Her glow had faded to almost nothing, but her eyes were still silver, still fixed on his face with an expression he couldn't quite read.

"You took me through the shadow," she said. "I didn't think that was possible. Shadowlands blood is supposed to react badly to—"

"I didn't have time to worry about reactions." He was still pressed against her, still feeling the rapid rise and fall of her chest against his. "Are you hurt?"

She shook her head slowly. Her hands were still gripping his shoulders, her fingers curled into the fabric of his jacket.

"No one's ever done that for me before." Her voice was strange—soft, wondering. "No one's ever put themselves between me and the things that hunt me."

Drevik became suddenly, acutely aware of how close they were. Her breasts pressed against his chest, her hips cradled against his. Her lips were inches from his, parted slightly, and her breath came in quick, shallow pants that had nothing to do with fear.

He should step back. Put distance between them.

He didn't.

"Thank you," Seraphina said. She rose on her toes again, and this time when her lips met his cheek, she lingered. The kiss burned through his skin like a fever dream, and he felt her tremble against him.

When she pulled back, her eyes were still silver, still holding that strange, vulnerable light.

"The next breach," she said, her voice barely audible, "will be during the full moon ceremony. The traitor plans something big." She paused, her fingers still tangled in his jacket. "And they know about your parents, Drevik. Whatever happened to them—it's connected to what's happening now."

Drevik's blood went cold. "What do you mean?"

But Seraphina was already pulling away, already retreating into the shadows of the corridor.

"I'll find you," she said over her shoulder. "Watch for me. And be careful who you trust."

Then she was gone, slipping through a door that led to the street, leaving Drevik alone with the phantom heat of her body against his and the echo of her final words ringing in his ears.

They know about your parents.

He sagged against the wall, his heart pounding, his mind racing through the implications. Seraphina Vane was half-Shadowlands being. A traitor was working to open a permanent gate. And somewhere in the chaos, the truth about his parents—about who they were and what had happened to them—was waiting to be discovered.

The full moon ceremony was in three weeks.

He had that long to find the traitor, protect Seraphina, and uncover the secrets that had been buried for twenty-one years.

Drevik pushed off the wall and headed for the street, his coffee going cold in the shop across the way, his life getting more complicated by the hour.

And somewhere in the back of his mind, he could still feel the burning touch of Seraphina's lips against his skin.

He made it halfway across campus before Lexi found him.

She fell into step beside him without a word, her auburn hair catching the afternoon light. Her green eyes swept over his face, reading something in his expression that made her jaw tighten.

"You met with her." Not a question.

Drevik kept walking. "I had to."

"You're putting yourself in danger."

"I'm putting myself in a lot of danger. That seems to be the theme lately."

Lexi grabbed his arm and pulled him to a stop. Her grip was firm, her expression fierce.

"Seraphina Vane is not what she seems. My mother's files mention her—flagged as unstable, potentially dangerous."

"Your mother's files mention a lot of things." Drevik met her gaze steadily. "Does she know about the traitor? About the full moon ceremony?"

Lexi's eyes narrowed. "What are you talking about?"

"Someone inside the Order is working to open a permanent gate during the ceremony. Seraphina confirmed it. She also confirmed that the breach at the gala wasn't an accident—it was a test run."

Lexi's hand loosened on his arm. The anger in her expression shifted to something closer to alarm.

"That's... if that's true..."

"It is true." Drevik gently removed her hand from his sleeve. "I know you don't trust her. I know there's history between you two that I don't understand. But she gave me information we need, and she's putting herself at risk to help us."

"What does she want in return?"

The question hung in the air between them. Drevik thought about Seraphina's confession, her vulnerability, the way she'd looked at him in the alley. The heat of her body against his.

"Protection," he said finally. "And trust."

Lexi studied him for a long moment, and he saw something flicker in her eyes—not jealousy, exactly, but something adjacent to it.

"You're playing a dangerous game, Drevik."

"I know." He started walking again, and this time she fell into step beside him. "But I don't have a choice. The full moon ceremony is in three weeks. If we don't find the traitor before then, everything falls apart."

Lexi was quiet for several steps. Then: "My mother's files. There might be something in them about previous breaches, about patterns that could point to who's responsible."

"Can you get them?"

"I already have them. I just didn't know what I was looking for." She glanced at him sidelong. "I'll go through them tonight. See if anything matches what Seraphina told you."

Drevik nodded. "Thank you."

They walked in silence for a while, the campus sprawling around them, students hurrying to their next classes, completely unaware of the darkness gathering just beyond the veil of their everyday lives.

"Drevik," Lexi said. "What else did she tell you?"

He hesitated. The secret of Seraphina's heritage wasn't his to share. But the final words, the warning about his parents...

"She said the traitor knows about my parents. That what happened to them is connected to what's happening now."

Lexi's step faltered. She reached out and touched his arm again—not grabbing this time, just resting her fingers against his sleeve.

"We'll figure this out." Her voice was fierce. "Whatever secrets they're hiding, whatever they've buried—we'll find the truth."

Drevik looked at her, at the determination in her green eyes, and felt something loosen in his chest. He wasn't alone in this. Whatever came next, whatever dangers waited in the shadows, he had people beside him.

"I know we will," he said.

And together, they walked toward the gathering dark.

Chapter 9: Questions of Loyalty

Chapter 9: Questions of Loyalty

The pain hit Drevik like a knife between his ribs, sharp and sudden, just as Professor Hendricks called on him.

"Mr. Markinsoul? Care to explain the significance of the Marshall Court's decisions on federal Indian policy?"

Drevik gripped his desk, knuckles white. The ache radiated outward from his chest, spreading through his shoulders, crawling up his neck. His vision blurred at the edges. He'd felt the Oath's punishment before—mild warnings, brief flares of discomfort when he'd skirted too close to forbidden topics. This was different. This was the Oath sinking its teeth in and refusing to let go.

"I..." He swallowed hard. The right answer sat somewhere in his notes, which he'd barely skimmed before class. He'd meant to study last night. Instead, he'd spent hours testing the boundaries of what he could and couldn't say about Seraphina, about the shadows he'd seen moving in the library, about the file Ivy had shown him with his parents' names redacted.

Each attempt to articulate the truth had brought pain. Each lie by omission had made it worse.

"Mr. Markinsoul?" Hendricks adjusted his glasses, frowning. "Are you feeling alright? You look rather pale."

Someone behind him snickered. Drevik recognized the sound—Marcus Webb, perpetual thorn in his side since freshman year, currently dating the girl Drevik had tutored through remedial history last semester.

"I need—" Drevik started, then stopped. Speaking was like swallowing glass. The Oath coiled tighter every time he thought about explaining his distraction, because the explanation would lead to questions, and questions would lead to truths he wasn't permitted to share.

This is ridiculous. He was an adult. A student. A member of a shadowy magical organization that apparently had no problem with him failing his classes as long as he kept their secrets.

"Mr. Markinsoul, perhaps you should visit the health center." Hendricks had moved on, calling on another student, a girl with perfect notes and no magical obligations weighing on her soul.

Drevik gathered his things with shaking hands. His notebook slid off the desk, papers scattering across the floor. He knelt to retrieve them, and the motion sent a fresh wave of agony through him. For a moment, he stayed there, forehead nearly touching the cold tile, breathing through the pain.

Get up. Get out. Figure this out.

He made it to the hallway before his legs gave out.

The student lounge was crowded for a Tuesday afternoon. Drevik found a corner table, far from the windows where sunlight streamed too bright for his shadow-adjusted eyes. He'd managed to grab a coffee on the way, but his hands were still trembling too badly to drink it without spilling.

"You look like absolute hell."

He looked up to find Jenna Torres hovering over him, backpack slung over one shoulder, concern etched into her features. They'd had World Civ together last semester, studied in the same library corners, exchanged the kind of casual friendship that came from shared academic suffering.

"Thanks," Drevik managed. The pain had settled into a dull throb, bearable if he didn't think too hard about anything important. "I've had a rough couple days."

"Rough couple days?" Jenna dropped into the chair across from him, setting her bag down with a thump. "Drevik, you look like you haven't slept in a week. Are you sick? Should I call someone?"

"No." The word came out sharper than intended. He softened his tone. "No, I'm fine. Just—stress. You know how it gets."

Jenna studied him, unconvinced. "I brought my poli-sci notes if you want to study together. Professor Chen's exam is Thursday, and you look like someone who forgot it existed."

Thursday. The word landed like a blow. Three days away. He'd completely forgotten.

"Actually—" He decline, but the Oath tightened again, a warning pulse. He couldn't explain why he needed to be alone, couldn't articulate that studying felt impossible when his brain was being squeezed by magical restrictions. "Yeah. Yeah, that'd be great."
