Jenna's smile was sympathetic. She pulled out her laptop, opened a document covered in color-coded highlights. "I've got the study guide pretty well mapped out. We can start with the foundational concepts and work our way up to the contemporary applications."

Drevik nodded, reaching for his own notebook. His hand brushed the coffee cup, knocked it sideways. Dark liquid spread across the table, dripping onto his notes, staining the pages he'd managed to salvage from his earlier disaster.

"Shit." He grabbed napkins from the dispenser, dabbing frantically at the spreading mess. His chest constricted, and for a horrible moment, he thought the Oath was punishing him for his clumsiness.

But no—this was just frustration. Real, ordinary, non-magical frustration.

"Here." Jenna had grabbed more napkins, helping him clean. "It's just coffee, Drevik. It's not the end of the world."

He laughed, a hollow sound. "No. No, it's not."

They settled into studying, or tried to. Jenna would explain a concept, ask him a question, and Drevik would reach for the answer only to find his thoughts scattered by pain. Every time he tried to focus, his mind drifted to Seraphina—to her warning about traitors in the Order, to the fear in her eyes when she'd said they'll kill me if they find out I talked to you.

If he told Lexi, she'd have to report it. That was her duty as his handler. And Seraphina would die.

If he didn't tell Lexi, he was violating his Oath to the Order. And the Oath was making sure he knew it.

"Drevik?" Jenna's voice cut through his spiraling thoughts. "You're doing that thing again."

"What thing?"

"The spacing out thing. The 'I'm somewhere far away and unhappy about it' thing." She closed her laptop. "Look, I don't know what's going on with you, but maybe you need a break. Have you eaten today? Drank water? Done anything that doesn't involve staring into space and looking miserable?"

The question caught him off guard. He tried to remember the last time he'd eaten. Breakfast? Had he eaten breakfast? The day blurred together, a smear of classes and pain and the constant weight of secrets.

"I don't think so," he admitted.

"Then we're getting food." Jenna stood, slinging her bag over her shoulder. "Come on. My treat. And you can tell me what's actually bothering you, or you can not tell me, but at least you'll be fed while you're being mysterious."

Drevik opened his mouth to refuse, but another spike of pain drove the words back down his throat. He couldn't explain. Couldn't even decline properly without triggering the Oath's restrictions.

"I need to—" He gestured vaguely toward the bathroom. "I'll meet you there."

Jenna's expression shifted, concern flickering into something harder to read. "Yeah. Sure. The café on Fifth?"

He nodded, not trusting himself to speak. She left, and he stayed in his chair for a long moment, breathing through the pain.

Then he walked to the bathroom, locked himself in a stall, and pressed his forehead against the cold metal door.

What am I supposed to do?

The Oath didn't answer. It never did. It simply punished, restricting, binding him in chains he couldn't see and couldn't break.

He thought of Lexi. Of her hands on his face when she'd healed him after the warehouse, the way her eyes had softened before she'd pulled away. He wanted to tell her everything. Wanted to trust her with the secrets eating him alive.

But Ivy's warning echoed in his memory: There are people in the Order who want you to fail. Who wanted your parents gone.

If Lexi was one of them...

He couldn't finish the thought. Wouldn't.

Drevik splashed cold water on his face, gripped the edges of the sink, and stared at his reflection. Dark circles shadowed his eyes. His skin had gone grey. He looked like a corpse waiting to happen.

"Get it together," he muttered to himself. "You've survived worse."

The lie felt hollow even as he spoke it.

He didn't make it to the café.

Drevik made it halfway across the quad before the pain crested again, dropping him to his knees on the grass. Students streamed past, a few casting curious glances, most ignoring him entirely. He'd become adept at being invisible, at blending into the background even when his world was falling apart.

This is getting worse. The realization settled cold in his stomach. The Oath wasn't just warning him anymore. It was actively hurting him, punishing him for secrets he couldn't share.

"Hey! Hey, are you okay?"

Hands gripped his shoulders, steadying him. He looked up into familiar green eyes, auburn hair falling across a worried face.

Lexi.

Of course it was Lexi.

"I'm fine," he managed, but the words came out strangled. He wasn't fine. He was the opposite of fine, and the Oath was making sure he couldn't even lie about it properly.

"You're not fine." Her voice was sharp, commanding, the voice of someone used to being obeyed. "You're on your knees in the middle of the quad, Drevik. That's not fine. That's 'I need a doctor' or 'I need to tell me what the hell is going on.'"

"I can't—" He gasped as the pain spiked again. "I can't tell you."

Lexi's expression hardened. "Can't or won't?"

"Can't." The word came out as a whisper. "The Oath. It won't let me."

Something shifted in her eyes. The anger didn't fade, but it was joined by understanding, and beneath that, a fear she was trying to hide. She helped him to his feet, supporting his weight against her side.

"We need to get you somewhere private." She glanced around the quad, scanning for threats Drevik couldn't see through the haze of pain. "Can you walk?"

He nodded, though he wasn't sure it was true. Lexi took his arm anyway, guiding him across the grass, past the clusters of students, toward a building he didn't recognize.

"Where are we going?" he asked.

"Somewhere the Order's eyes won't follow." Her voice was low, meant only for him. "There are places on this campus that aren't monitored. Old buildings. Forgotten rooms. You're not the first person who's needed to hide from the Oath's restrictions."

The revelation struck him, even through the pain. "Other people have—"

"Had secrets they couldn't share? Yes." Lexi's grip tightened on his arm. "The Oath is supposed to protect the Order, but sometimes it protects the wrong people. Sometimes it protects the ones who are hurting us."

They entered a building Drevik vaguely recognized—one of the oldest on campus, brick and ivy, scheduled for renovation that never start. Lexi led him down a hallway, through a door that looked like it hadn't been opened in years, up a narrow staircase that creaked with every step.

The room at the top was small, dusty, lit by a single window letting in the afternoon light. Lexi guided him to a worn couch against the wall, pushed him down with gentle pressure.

"Lie back," she ordered. "Let me see if I can help."

Drevik obeyed, too exhausted to argue. The pain had settled into a constant ache, flaring whenever he thought about Seraphina, about the file, about anything that might lead to truth he couldn't share.

Lexi knelt beside him, her hands hovering over his temples. "This might hurt at first. The Oath's restrictions are woven into your energy. I can try to ease the pressure, but I can't remove it without your vow."

"Do it," he said. "Please."

Her fingers touched his temples, cool against his feverish skin. For a moment, nothing happened. Then heat built at the points of contact, spreading through his skull, and the pain shifted.

It didn't disappear. But it moved, settled into something less consuming. Like a wound that had gone from bleeding to scabbed over.

"Better?" Lexi's voice was soft now, intimate in the quiet room.

"Better." Drevik opened his eyes, finding her face inches from his own. This close, he saw the flecks of gold in her green irises, the faint scar at the corner of her mouth, the way her breath came slightly faster than normal.

She didn't pull away.

"You scared me," she admitted. "I saw you go down from across the quad. I thought—" She stopped, swallowed. "I thought something had attacked you. A shadow creature. One of the things from the other side."

"Just the Oath," Drevik said. "Just my own choices, coming back to hurt me."

Lexi's expression flickered. "What did you do? What are you hiding that's worth this?"

He opened his mouth to answer, but the pain flared again, a warning. Not that far. Not yet.

"I can't tell you." The admission was like defeat. "I want to, Lexi. I want to trust you with everything. But the Oath—"

"The Oath is a tool," she cut in, sharp. "It's not supposed to torture you. It's supposed to ensure loyalty, not punish you for having thoughts the Order doesn't approve of."

"Maybe the Order doesn't approve of my thoughts."

The words hung between them. Lexi's hands were still on his face, her thumbs brushing against his cheekbones in a gesture that felt far too tender for the conversation they were having.

"Then maybe the Order is wrong." Her voice was barely a whisper. "Maybe you're not the one who needs to change."

Drevik's heart stuttered in his chest. She was too close. He was too aware of her—the warmth of her palms, the slight tremble in her fingers, the way her lips parted as she spoke.

"Lexi," he started, but he didn't know how to finish.

"I know." She pulled back slightly, but her hands remained, framing his face. "I know you can't tell me. I know the Oath won't let you. But Drevik, whatever this is—whatever secret you're carrying—I need you to know that I'm on your side. Always. Even when the Order isn't. Even when the Oath makes you doubt it."

"How can you say that?" He searched her face for deception, for any sign of the traitor Ivy had warned him about. "You don't know what I'm hiding."

"I know you." Her thumb traced the line of his jaw. "I know you wouldn't keep a secret unless you had to. I know you wouldn't protect something that would hurt the Order unless the Order was wrong to begin with. And I know—" She paused, something vulnerable passing across her features. "I know I've spent the last three months trying not to care about you, and I'm failing spectacularly."

The confession hit him like a physical blow. He'd suspected, maybe, in the way she looked at him, the way she always appear when he needed help. But hearing it spoken aloud, in a dusty room in an abandoned building, with her hands still cradling his face—

"Lexi."

"I'm not asking you to tell me," she said quickly. "I'm not asking you to break your Oath. I'm just asking you to believe that when you're ready—when you can—I'll be here. And I won't betray you. I won't use your secrets against you. I won't be the person you're afraid I might be."

Drevik reached up, covering one of her hands with his own. The contact sent a shiver through him, her magic intertwining with his, the shadow energy that bound them both humming with recognition.

"I want to believe that," he said. "I want to trust you."

"Then trust me with what you can." Her eyes searched his. "Test the boundaries. Find the edges of what the Oath will allow. And let me help you carry whatever's hurting you."

He considered it. The Oath restricted specific truths—names, locations, details that could expose the Order's secrets. But what about feelings? Suspicions? Questions without answers?

"There's someone," he said, testing each word before speaking it. "Someone who warned me about traitors in the Order. Someone who said there are people who want me to fail. Who wanted my parents gone."

Lexi went very still. "Who?"

"I can't tell you that." The Oath tightened, but not painfully. A warning, not a punishment. "But I can tell you that I don't know if they're telling the truth. I don't know if the traitor is them, or someone else, or no one at all."

"Your parents." Her voice was careful. "The file Ivy showed you—that's what this is about?"

Drevik nodded. "It was redacted. Pages removed. I don't know what they were hiding, but someone went to a lot of trouble to make sure I couldn't find out."

"Your handler." Lexi's expression darkened. "She's supposed to guide you, not use you for her own investigations."

"She's trying to help."

"She's trying to use you to expose something that might get you killed." The words were harsh, but her tone held more fear than anger. "Drevik, the Order doesn't forgive betrayal. If you're caught digging into classified files, if you're caught conspiring with someone who's already under suspicion—"

"I know." He closed his eyes, feeling the weight of everything pressing down on him. "I know the risks. But Lexi, these are my parents. Someone removed information about their deaths. Someone wanted me to stay ignorant. How can I ignore that?"

Her hand moved from his face to his chest, resting over his heart. The contact was grounding, her magic seeping into him, easing the last traces of the Oath's punishment.

"You can't ignore it," she said. "But you can't face it alone either. And you can't keep destroying yourself to protect secrets that might not even matter."

"They matter to me."

"Then let me help you find a way to share them." Her thumb traced small circles over his heart. "There are rituals—ways to share information under the protection of a blood bond. The Oath can't restrict what's shared between bonded partners."

Drevik's eyes snapped open. "A blood bond?"

"It's not—" Lexi pulled back slightly, color rising in her cheeks. "It's not permanent. It's a temporary connection, a way to share information without speaking it aloud. It's used for sensitive missions, when handlers and acolytes need to communicate without risking exposure."

"And you're offering to do this with me?"

"I'm offering to help you." Her voice was firm, but her eyes dropped, unable to meet his gaze. "Whatever you need. However I can give it."

The air between them felt thick, charged with possibility. Drevik was acutely aware of how close she still was, of the warmth of her hand against his chest, of the way her breathing had quickened.

This is dangerous, he thought. Not the blood bond—though that carried its own risks—but the way he wanted to say yes for all the wrong reasons. The way he wanted her hands on him, her magic inside him, her trust wrapped around him like armor.

"I need to think about it," he said finally. "The bond, the secrets—all of it. I need to figure out who I can trust before I start making magical commitments."

Lexi withdrew her hand, and he felt the loss like a physical ache. "I understand."

"Lexi—" He caught her wrist before she could pull away completely. "I'm not saying no. I'm saying I need time. This isn't something I can rush into."

"Time." She laughed, a hollow sound. "That's something we might not have much of. The Order doesn't wait for acolytes to feel ready. The next trial is in two weeks, and if you're distracted, if you're hiding things, if you're compromised by Oath restrictions—"

"Then I'll fail."

"Then you'll die." The words were blunt, brutal. "The trials aren't academic exercises, Drevik. They're tests of survival. And if you go in distracted, in pain, carrying secrets that are actively hurting you—"

"I know." He released her wrist, running a hand through his hair. "Believe me, I know."

She stood, moving toward the window, her back to him. The afternoon light caught the red in her hair, turned it to fire. "Then let me help you. Let me be the ally you need. Because whatever you're hiding, whatever the Oath is protecting—it's not worth your life."

Drevik wanted to believe her. Wanted to trust that the woman standing in the dusty abandoned room, offering him a magical bond that would let him share his secrets, was exactly who she be.

But Ivy's warning echoed in his mind. Traitors in the Order. People who wanted your parents gone.

And Lexi was the daughter of an Elder.

He made it through the rest of the day in a fog.

Drevik attended his remaining classes, took notes he couldn't focus on, answered questions he barely understood. The Oath's pain had faded to a dull ache, manageable as long as he didn't think too hard about anything important.

By evening, he'd retreated to his dorm room, door locked, lights off. He sat on the edge of his bed, staring at nothing, trying to organize thoughts that kept slipping away from him.

The blood bond. Lexi's offer circled in his mind. A way to share information without speaking, to bypass the Oath's restrictions, to finally unburden himself of the secrets eating him alive.

But also a way to expose himself completely. To let another person into his mind, his memories, his fears. To trust someone with everything, including the truths that could get him killed.

His phone buzzed. Drevik glanced at the screen, expecting Lexi, or maybe Jenna asking why he'd never made it to the café.

Instead, he found a message from a number he didn't recognize.

I found what was removed from your parents' files. Meet me tomorrow night. Come alone.

His heart stuttered. He didn't need a signature to know who'd sent it. Ivy had found something. Something worth risking everything to see.

I'll be there, he typed back.

The response came immediately: Midnight. The old cemetery on Thornwood Road. Don't be followed.

Drevik set the phone down, staring at the dark screen. Tomorrow night. Another secret. Another risk. Another thread in the web of lies he was weaving around himself.

But somewhere in those redacted files lay the truth about his parents. And he would face anything—Oath restrictions, traitor accusations, even death—to find it.

Chapter 10: The Truth About His Parents

Chapter 10: The Truth About His Parents

The restricted section of the Order's library smelled of dust and secrets.

Drevik stood at the threshold, his heart hammering against his ribs as Ivy's pale hand pressed against the small of his back, urging him forward. The archive room stretched before them, shelves climbing toward a ceiling lost in shadow, each volume bound in leather that drink the dim light.

"We shouldn't be here," he said.

"We have to be." Ivy's voice carried a steel edge he'd never heard from her before. The shy girl who barely met his eyes during training had vanished, replaced by someone with purpose burning behind her violet gaze. "What I'm about to show you doesn't exist. Not officially."

She led him past rows of categorized texts—Shadowlands Bestiary, Lunar Rites and Their Applications, *The History of the Veiled Ones—until they reached a section marked only with a symbol. A crescent moon pierced by a dagger.

Drevik's blood chilled. He'd seen that symbol before. In his grandmother's things, after she passed. He'd assumed it was decorative.

"Your parents were Wardens," Ivy said, pulling a leather-bound journal from the shelf. Her fingers trembled, but her voice remained steady. "High-ranking ones. They weren't killed in a Shadowlands breach like the official records state."

She opened the book and laid it on a reading table. The pages were yellowed, covered in precise handwriting that made Drevik's throat tight. He recognized that script. He'd seen it on the back of a photograph he'd kept hidden for years.

"These are my mother's notes." His voice came out raw.

Ivy nodded, her dark hair falling forward to curtain her face. "They were investigating corruption within the Order. Someone was deliberately weakening the barriers between realms during specific lunar phases. Creating breaches on purpose."

Drevik's hands shook as he turned the pages. His mother's handwriting detailed meetings, financial transactions, names. Some he recognized from Order ceremonies. Others were coded, marked with symbols he couldn't decipher.

"They found proof," Ivy continued. "Evidence that someone at the Elder level was orchestrating the breaches. But before they could present their findings to the Veiled Council—"

"They were silenced." The word tasted like ash.

"The breach that killed them was engineered. Your father's Shadow Walking was sabotaged. Your mother was trapped on the other side of a closing veil." Ivy's voice cracked. "I'm sorry, Drevik. I'm so sorry."

He gripped the edge of the table, his knuckles white. For years, he'd told himself their deaths were an accident. A tragedy. Something random and cruel that the universe had thrown at him. But this—

"How long have you known?"

Ivy's cheeks flushed. "My mother was their friend. She suspected something was wrong when the official story didn't match the magical signatures at the site. She started digging. When she got too close—" The flush drained from her face, leaving her pale. "The same thing happened to her. 'Natural causes,' they said. But I knew."

Drevik looked at her with new eyes. The quiet girl in the back of his acolyte classes. The one who excelled at research and avoided combat training. She'd been carrying this weight alone, just like him.

"Why tell me now?"

"Because the pattern is repeating." Ivy flipped to a later section of the journal, pointing to a series of dates. "The breaches your parents identified? They happened during full moon ceremonies. When the Order's leadership is gathered and focused on the ritual. When security is concentrated in one place."

The full moon ceremony. Three days away.

Drevik's stomach dropped. "Someone's planning another breach."

"I believe so. And I think—" Ivy hesitated, chewing her lower lip. "I think they're going to use you to do it."

The words hit him like a physical blow. "Me?"

"Your Shadow Walking ability. It's exceptional. The Veiled Ones themselves have commented on it." Ivy's gaze dropped to the journal. "If someone wanted to create a catastrophic breach, they'd need a powerful walker to anchor it. Someone who wouldn't realize what they were doing until it was too late."

Drevik thought of the strange attention he'd received since his initiation. Elder Thornwood's personal interest in his training. The extra lessons, the specialized techniques. He'd assumed it was favoritism. Recognition of his talent.

What if it was something else entirely?

"Who?" The question came out harsh. "Who's behind this?"

Ivy's fingers hovered over a list of names in the journal. Drevik followed them with his eyes, and his blood ran cold.

Elder Thornwood's name appeared three times.

"There are others," Ivy said quickly, seeing his expression. "Elder Vance. Warden Corith. But Elder Thornwood—he's the one who's been positioning you. Suggesting you for advanced rituals. Ensuring you're at the center of the full moon ceremony."

Lexi's father.

Drevik's mind spun. Lexi, with her fierce loyalty and protective nature. Lexi, who'd trained him, challenged him, pushed him harder than anyone. Lexi, who'd kissed him in the shadow of the last moon ritual with desperate intensity.

She couldn't know. She couldn't be involved. The thought of her being aware of this, complicit in—

"I don't believe it," he said, but doubt gnawed at the edges of his certainty.

Ivy touched his arm gently. "I don't want to believe it either. Elder Thornwood has a daughter close to our age. Lexi. She's—" A pause, loaded with meaning. "You know her."

The implication hung between them. Drevik thought of Lexi's warnings about trusting too easily. Her suspicious nature. The way she'd questioned his presence from the beginning.

Had she been trying to protect him? Or had she been monitoring him for her father?

Footsteps echoed in the corridor outside.

Drevik's combat instincts flared. He grabbed Ivy's hand and pulled her toward the shadows between shelves, pressing them both into the narrow gap between two massive bookcases. The darkness swallowed them as he wrapped shadow around them like a cloak—a technique he'd mastered beyond what any acolyte should be capable of.

Ivy's body pressed against his in the tight space. Her breath came quick and shallow, her pulse quickening so hard he felt it against his chest. Her wide violet eyes found his in the darkness, inches away.

"Don't move," he said against her ear. "Don't make a sound."

She nodded, her hair brushing his cheek. Her fingers curled into the fabric of his shirt, holding on.

The footsteps grew closer. Measured. Confident. Someone who belonged here.

Drevik held his breath, acutely aware of every point of contact between their bodies. Ivy's soft curves fitted against his harder lines. The warmth radiating from her skin. The subtle scent of her—jasmine and old books and something uniquely her.

A figure passed the end of their aisle. Tall, robed, moving with the authority of rank. Drevik caught a glimpse of silver hair.

Elder Vance.

He waited until the footsteps faded completely before releasing the shadow cloak. But he didn't step back. Neither did Ivy.

"Thank you," she said. "For hiding us."

"Thank you for telling me." The words came out rough with emotion he couldn't contain. "You've been carrying this alone. Risking everything. Why?"

Ivy's gaze held his, fierce and vulnerable at once. "Because someone should have helped them. Your parents. My mother." Her voice dropped to barely audible. "Because when I look at you, I see someone who might finally be strong enough to make it matter."

Drevik's hand came up without conscious thought, cupping her face. Her skin was soft, warm, trembling slightly under his touch.

"I won't let their deaths be meaningless," he said. "Either of them."

Ivy's eyes glistened. "I know. That's why I chose you."

She rose on her toes and pressed her lips to his.

The kiss was soft, tentative, nothing like the firestorm of Lexi's kisses. But there was something deeper in it—shared grief, shared purpose, a connection born of secrets and sorrow. Drevik found himself responding, his other hand coming to rest on her hip, steadying her as the kiss deepened.

Ivy made a small sound against his mouth, her fingers tightening in his shirt. She tasted of mint tea and desperation. Of loneliness finally acknowledged.

When they broke apart, both of them breathing harder, Ivy's cheeks were flushed pink.

"I'm sorry," she said. "That was—I shouldn't have—"

"Don't apologize." Drevik's thumb stroked her cheekbone. "Not for that."

The words hung between them, weighted with promise. Ivy's lower lip trembled.

"The full moon ceremony," she said, her voice steadying as she stepped back slightly. "Whatever they're planning, it happens then. You need to be prepared. You need to know what to watch for."

She spent the next hour walking him through the journal's contents. His mother's coded notes. The financial irregularities. The pattern of deaths that had been dismissed as accidents over the past two decades. All of it pointed to a conspiracy that reached the highest levels of the Order.

"They're not just creating breaches," Ivy explained, her academic demeanor returning even as her lips remained swollen from their kiss. "They're harvesting shadow essence from the entities that come through. Selling it to outside interests. The barrier between worlds is being deliberately weakened for profit."

Drevik thought of the Shadowlands. The endless twilight realm that pressed against the edges of reality. If the barrier fell, creatures would pour through unchecked. Thousands would die.

"Who's buying it?"

"I don't know yet. But there are references to a 'client' in the later entries. Someone powerful outside the Order." Ivy's expression darkened. "Your parents were close to identifying them when they were killed."

Drevik's jaw tightened. "And Elder Thornwood?"

"Has been the primary advocate for expanding Shadow Walking training. Specifically for acolytes with unusual talent." Her gaze met his. "Specifically for you."

The pieces clicked together with sickening clarity. The extra sessions. The advanced techniques. The way Elder Thornwood had personally overseen his progress.

He was being groomed. But not for advancement.

For sacrifice.

"I need to tell Lexi," Drevik said, though the words tasted bitter. If she wasn't already compromised. If she wasn't already part of this.

Ivy's expression flickered. "Be careful. If she's loyal to her father—"

"I know." He touched Ivy's hand briefly. "I'll be careful."

They left the restricted section separately, timing their exits to avoid notice. Drevik's mind whirled with everything he'd learned. His parents, murdered. A conspiracy spanning decades. And somewhere in the center of it all, the man who had taken a personal interest in his development.

The man whose daughter had become the first woman Drevik had let past his defenses.

He needed time to think. To plan. To figure out who he could trust.

The message was waiting for him when he returned to his quarters.

A folded parchment, sealed with wax impressed by a crescent moon and dagger. The symbol that marked his mother's journal. The symbol of the conspiracy that had killed her.

Drevik broke the seal with steady hands, forcing himself to remain calm.

ACOLYTE MARKINSOUL,

Your presence is requested in the Elder chambers before the full moon ceremony. Elder Thornwood wishes to discuss your upcoming role in the rituals.

Fail to appear at your peril.

No signature. No indication of who had delivered it.

Drevik read the message three times, each pass making the implications clearer. Elder Thornwood wanted to speak with him. Alone. Before the ceremony where, if Ivy was right, they planned to use him to create a catastrophic breach.

He could run. Use his Shadow Walking to escape the compound entirely. Disappear into the world beyond the Order's reach.

But that would mean leaving his parents' deaths unavenged. Leaving Ivy alone with her dangerous knowledge. Leaving Lexi—

Lexi, who might be innocent. Who might be just as much a pawn as he was.

Drevik crumpled the message in his fist.

His parents had died trying to expose the truth. They'd been silenced before they could act.

He wouldn't make the same mistake.

The full moon ceremony was in three days. Elder Thornwood wanted a private meeting.

Drevik would give him one.

But he wouldn't walk in blind. And he wouldn't walk in alone.

He thought of Ivy's fierce determination beneath her shy exterior. Of Lexi's protective instincts and suspicious nature. Of the two women who had, in very different ways, claimed pieces of his heart.

If he was going to survive this, he'd need both of them.

The question was whether either of them could truly be trusted.

Drevik stared out his window at the moon, already swelling toward full. In three days, it would hang heavy and silver in the sky, pulling at shadow magic and dark ambitions alike.

In three days, he'd find out who his real enemies were.

And he'd make them pay for what they'd done to his parents.

His mother's journal sat heavy in his jacket pocket, a dead woman's testimony against the Order's leadership. Evidence that could expose everything—if he lived long enough to use it.

Drevik closed his eyes and let the shadows gather around him, drawing strength from the darkness that was both his gift and his heritage.

His parents had died for the truth.

He would not let their sacrifice be in vain.

The knock came at midnight.

Drevik had been lying in bed, staring at the ceiling, running scenarios through his mind until they blurred together. The quiet tap on his door made him sit up instantly, shadows coiling around his fists.

"Who is it?"

"Me." Ivy's voice, barely audible through the wood. "Let me in. Please."

He crossed the room in three strides and pulled the door open. Ivy stood in the hallway, wrapped in a dark cloak that swallowed her slight frame. Her eyes were red-rimmed, her expression tight with fear.

"I had to come," she said, slipping past him into his room. "I couldn't stay in my quarters after—after everything."

Drevik closed the door, suddenly aware that they were alone. In his room. At midnight. With the weight of their shared secrets pressing them together.

"What happened?" He kept his voice low, conscious of the thin walls.

"I found something else." Ivy pulled a folded paper from her cloak, pressing it into his hands. "A letter. Hidden in my mother's things. It's from your father."

Drevik's world narrowed to the paper in his grip. His father's handwriting—bolder than his mother's, more angular—covered the page.

Caroline,

If you're reading this, I've failed. The conspiracy goes deeper than we imagined. Thornwood is not the architect—he's a pawn. The true enemy wears a face we trust. Trust no one with the silver mark.

Tell our son the truth. Tell him his gift is his inheritance and his weapon. Tell him—

The letter ended there. Torn, as if someone had interrupted the writing. Or the writer had been interrupted permanently.

"Silver mark," Drevik said. "What does that mean?"

"I don't know." Ivy's voice shook. "But your father trusted my mother. He sent her this letter as insurance. A dead man's switch."

Drevik looked up from the paper to find Ivy watching him with those wide violet eyes. Tears tracked silently down her cheeks.

"I'm scared," she admitted. "For the first time since my mother died, I'm actually scared. And I don't know what to do."

Drevik set the letter aside and opened his arms.

Ivy stepped into them without hesitation.

Her body pressed against his, trembling with suppressed sobs. Drevik held her, one hand cradling the back of her head, feeling her warmth seep into him. She felt fragile in his embrace, but he knew that was an illusion. Ivy had been carrying the weight of her mother's murder for years. She was stronger than anyone gave her credit for.

"I won't let anything happen to you," he said against her hair. "I promise."

"You can't promise that." Her voice was muffled against his chest. "None of us are safe."

"Then we'll be unsafe together." He pulled back slightly, tilting her chin up to meet his eyes. "You're not alone anymore, Ivy. You hear me? Whatever comes next, you don't face it alone."

Her lower lip quivered. "Why are you being so good to me?"

"Because someone should have helped you." He echoed her earlier words back to her. "Because when I look at you, I see someone who might be strong enough to make it matter."

Ivy's laugh was watery but genuine. "That's my line."

"I'm stealing it." Drevik brushed a strand of hair from her face, his thumb lingering on her cheek. "Consider it payment for everything you've risked for me."

Her eyes dropped to his mouth. The air between them thickened with awareness.

"Drevik," she said. "I—"

He kissed her before she could finish.

This kiss was different from the one in the library. Less tentative, more certain. Ivy responded instantly, her fingers curling into his shirt as she pressed closer. Drevik's hands slid down her sides, feeling the curves hidden beneath her shapeless cloak, the way her body fitted against his.

She made a soft sound of need that went straight to his core.

"Stay," he said against her lips. "Tonight. Stay with me."

Ivy pulled back just enough to meet his eyes. Her expression was torn—want and fear and hope tangled together.

"I shouldn't," she said. But her hands didn't release his shirt. "We have so much to do. So much to prepare."

"The world might end in three days." Drevik's voice was rough. "I don't want to spend tonight alone."

Something shifted in Ivy's face. The fear receded, replaced by determination.

"Then don't."

She let her cloak fall to the floor.

Drevik woke to gray dawn light and an empty bed.

The sheets beside him were still warm, carrying Ivy's jasmine scent. A note lay on the pillow where her head had rested.

I'll see you at breakfast. Act normal. We'll find a way through this.

Ivy

He read the note twice, memorizing the shape of her handwriting. Another piece of evidence to add to his collection—though this one he'd guard more jealously than any journal.

The full moon ceremony loomed. Elder Thornwood's summons waited. His father's warning about a silver mark echoed in his mind.

But for the first time since his parents died, Drevik felt something other than grief or rage.

He felt purpose.

And he wasn't alone anymore.

That had to count for something.

The day passed in a blur of normalcy that felt increasingly surreal. Training sessions. Classes. Meals in the communal hall where he sat across from Lexi, acutely aware of her father's name in his mother's journal.

Lexi watched him with sharp green eyes throughout lunch, her expression unreadable.

"You're distracted," she said, stabbing at her food. "Something wrong?"

"Nothing." The lie came easily. "Just pre-ceremony nerves."

Lexi's gaze narrowed, but she let it go. "The full moon ritual is intense. You'll need your focus."

"I know."

A pause. Then, softer: "My father wants to meet with you. Before the ceremony."

Drevik's heart stuttered, but he kept his face neutral. "I received the summons."

"He's taken a special interest in your development." Lexi's voice carried an edge he couldn't interpret. "It's an honor."

"Is it?"

The question came out sharper than intended. Lexi's eyes flashed.

"What does that mean?"

Drevik forced himself to relax. "Nothing. I'm just wondering why an Elder would bother with an acolyte."

"You're not just any acolyte." Lexi leaned closer, lowering her voice. "You're talented. Powerful. The Order needs people like you."

Needs me to be a sacrifice, Drevik thought. Needs me to create a breach.

Needs me dead.

But he smiled and nodded, playing the part of the grateful initiate.

"Of course. I'm honored by the attention."

Lexi studied him for a long moment, her expression troubled. Then she stood abruptly.

"I'll see you at training."

She walked away, leaving Drevik with the weight of secrets he couldn't share and suspicions he couldn't voice.

Ivy caught his eye from across the hall, a subtle question in her gaze.

Drevik gave an almost imperceptible nod.

They were in this together now. Whatever came next.

The full moon rose in two days, carrying with it the promise of revelation or ruin.

Drevik intended to be ready for either.

Chapter 11: The Elder's Test

Chapter 11: The Elder's Test

The walk to Elder Thornwood's private chambers was like a march toward judgment.

Drevik moved through the eastern corridor of the compound, where the shadows pooled deeper than physics should allow. The torches here burned with that distinctive violet hue the Order favored, casting everything in shades of amethyst and charcoal. His footsteps echoed against stone that had been old when the university above was nothing but pasture and ambition.

Lexi's father. The words kept circling in his mind like vultures over carrion. He was about to meet Lexi's father, a man who might have ordered his parents' murders. A man who had been testing him, pushing him into increasingly dangerous situations.

A man who, by all accounts, had every reason to want him dead—or worse.

The door at the corridor's end was ancient oak, blackened with age and carved with symbols that shift when Drevik looked at them directly. He paused before it, forcing his breathing to slow. His chest tightened against his ribs like something caged and desperate.

Get it together. You've faced Shadowlands creatures. You've walked through nightmares made flesh. This is just a conversation.

But his instincts screamed otherwise. Every shadow in this corridor felt heavy with intent, as if the darkness itself were watching, waiting to report his failures to some higher power.

He knocked. Three times, as protocol demanded.

"Enter."

The voice was deeper than he'd expected, roughened by years or sorrow or both. Drevik pushed the door open and stepped into a room that defied his expectations.

Elder Thornwood's chambers were sparse. A desk cluttered with papers and artifacts. Bookshelves lined with journals and texts in languages Drevik didn't recognize. A single window that shouldn't have existed—the compound was underground—looking out onto a twilight landscape that definitely wasn't the mortal realm.

And standing at that window, his back to Drevik, was a man who looked nothing like what he'd imagined.

Elder Marcus Thornwood was not the imposing, menacing figure Drevik had constructed in his mind. He was of average height, with the lean build of someone who had once been athletic but had let time erode his edges. His hair was steel-gray, thin at the crown, and his posture carried the particular weight of a man who had buried too many people he loved.

He turned as Drevik entered, and the resemblance to Lexi struck like a physical blow. Those eyes—the same pale green, the same assessing quality, the same guarded depth that Drevik had spent hours trying to read.

"Acolyte Markinsoul." Thornwood's voice was carefully neutral. "Please, sit."

Drevik remained standing. "With respect, Elder, I'd prefer to see your face clearly."

Something flickered in those green eyes—surprise, perhaps, or approval. "Wise. Your mother would have done the same."

The words landed like a punch. Drevik's breath held, and he watched Thornwood's expression for any sign of deception, any hint that this was manipulation.

"Sit," Thornwood said again, and this time Drevik obeyied, lowering himself into the chair across from the Elder's desk. "We have much to discuss, and little time before the full moon ceremony. I imagine you have questions."

"Hundreds."

"Then let's begin with the one that brought you here." Thornwood settled into his own chair, the leather creaking beneath him. "You want to know if I killed your parents."

Direct. Brutally so. Drevik hadn't expected that.

"Did you?"

"No."

The word hung between them, simple and stark. Drevik searched the Elder's face for the tells he'd learned to read—micro-expressions, shifts in the shadows around a person's form that indicated deception. In the Order, lying was an art form, but Elder Thornwood's shadows remained still.

"I believe you," Drevik said. "But that doesn't mean you're innocent."

"No, it doesn't." Thornwood leaned forward, folding his hands on the desk. "I've done many things I'm not proud of, Acolyte. Things that would horrify you. But I did not kill Elena and Marcus Markinsoul. I loved them both, in different ways, and their deaths have haunted me for twenty-one years."

Elena and Marcus. His parents' names, spoken aloud by someone who had known them. The sensation was disorienting, like hearing a song from a language you'd forgotten you once spoke.

"You knew them well."

"Better than most." Thornwood's gaze drifted to the impossible window, that twilight landscape beyond. "Your mother and I were initiates together. We trained side by side, advanced through the ranks together. I thought..." He paused, jaw tightening. "I thought we would build a life together. She had other plans."

The implication settled slowly. "You loved her."

"I did. Desperately." No shame in the admission, only an old wound scarred over but never fully healed. "She chose your father instead. A man I initially resented and eventually came to respect. Marcus Markinsoul was stubborn, brilliant, and absolutely devoted to her. He made her happy in ways I never could have."

Drevik's hands clenched beneath the desk. "And when they died, you were here. Already an Elder."

"I was. And yes, I know how that looks." Thornwood met his gaze without flinching. "I was one of five Elders then, as I am now. Any one of us could have given the order. I've spent two decades trying to discover which one, and I have failed."

"Why should I believe you're not simply protecting yourself?"

"Because you have your mother's eyes, and your father's infuriating need to question everything, and I am tired." The words carried exhaustion bone-deep. "I am tired of secrets. Tired of watching promising acolytes die because someone in this Order feeds information to our enemies. Tired of burying people I care about."

Drevik absorbed this, weighing each word. "You said you've been testing me. The attacks, the assignments—they've all been deliberate."

"Yes."

"Why?"

Thornwood rose from his chair, moving to a shelf and pulling a leather-bound journal from between larger tomes. "Your parents were investigating something before they died. They believed there was a traitor among the Elders—someone working with the Shadowborn, entities that should have been our enemies. They were close to a discovery when they were killed."

"And you think this traitor is still active."

"I know they are." He turned back to Drevik, journal in hand. "The attacks on you haven't been random. Someone has been testing your abilities, probing your weaknesses, trying to determine if you inherited your parents' talents."

The attacks that nearly killed me. The ambush in the Shadowlands. The creature that shouldn't have been able to find me.

"You've been putting me in danger intentionally," Drevik said, the realization bitter in his mouth. "To see if I could survive."

"To see if you were worth investing in." Thornwood's voice hardened. "To see if you had the potential to finish what your parents started. The Order is rotting from within, Acolyte. Someone in a position of power is feeding our people to the Shadowborn, and I need to know if you're the one who can help me root them out."

"And if I wasn't?"

"Then you'd be dead already." Blunt, matter-of-fact. "The traitor would have seen to that. Your survival tells me you have your parents' gifts—and possibly their fate."

Drevik stood abruptly, the chair scraping against stone. "You're using me as bait."

"I'm using you as a weapon." Thornwood didn't flinch from the accusation. "The difference is, I'm trying to ensure you survive long enough to strike. Others want you dead before you can become a threat."

The room felt too small suddenly, the shadows pressing close. Drevik's pulse thundered in his ears, but he forced himself to think, to parse the implications.

"If you're telling the truth, why reveal this now? Why not continue the tests?"

"Because tomorrow night's ceremony changes everything." Thornwood moved closer, his voice dropping. "The full moon empowers our magic, but it also thins the barriers between realms. If the traitor acts, it will be then. And you need to be prepared."

"Prepared for what?"

"Prepared to choose a side." The Elder's green eyes bore into his. "There are those in the Order who believe your parents were traitors themselves, that they died because they got caught conspiring with the Shadowborn. I've spent years trying to clear their names, but the evidence was sealed away—hidden by someone who didn't want it found."

Evidence. Hidden. Like the journal in his hands.

"You have proof."

"I have questions. Answers are in here." Thornwood extended the leather-bound journal. "Your father's record of the investigation. He entrusted it to me the night before they died, with instructions to give it to you when you were ready."

Drevik took the journal with hands that weren't quite steady. The leather was worn, the pages yellowed with age. His father's handwriting, cramped and precise, filled the first visible page.

If you're reading this, I didn't survive long enough to tell you myself. Trust Marcus Thornwood. He loved your mother enough to let her go, and he loves you enough to tell you the truth.

His father had written that. Had known, somehow, that Drevik would one day hold this book, would need these words.

"How long have you had this?"

"Twenty-one years. Hidden in the one place the traitor wouldn't think to look—my personal collection, disguised as a mundane diary." Thornwood's mouth curved in something that wasn't quite a smile. "Your father was paranoid, but brilliantly so. He encoded the sensitive information in a cipher only family would recognize. Your grandmother taught it to him, apparently."

Grandmother. The woman who had raised him after his parents died, who had never spoken of the Order, who had died when he was sixteen, leaving him alone.

"She knew?"

"She was a Veiled One. Retired from active service when she took you in." Thornwood's expression softened fractionally. "She asked me not to contact you until you found your own way to us. She believed you needed to choose this path freely, not be guided to it."

Drevik's throat tightened. So many secrets. So many people making decisions about his life without his knowledge.

"There's something else," Thornwood said, and his tone shifted. The hardness remained, but something else crept in—something that sounded almost like paternal concern. "Something personal."

"I'm listening."

"Lexi."

The name landed like a stone in still water. Drevik's posture shifted, alertness sharpening.

"What about her?"

"She's my daughter." Thornwood's voice carried the particular weight of a father speaking of his child. "Her mother died in service to the Order when Lexi was nine. She watched me bury the woman I loved, and she swore she'd never let herself care about someone who could be taken from her."

And then she met me.

"She's been different with you," Thornwood continued. "More invested than I've seen her in years. She argues your case in council meetings. She volunteers for your training sessions. She nearly killed three Shadowborn single-handedly when you were trapped in the eastern passage last month."

Drevik remembered that night. The creature that had cornered him, the way Lexi had appeared from nowhere, her blade singing through shadows.

"I didn't know she—"

"She doesn't know I'm telling you this." Thornwood stepped closer, his presence suddenly imposing despite his unremarkable frame. "But you need to understand something, Acolyte Markinsoul. My daughter has a habit of loving people who get themselves killed. Don't make her bury another."

The words hung between them, weighted with history and warning and something that might have been plea.

Loving people who get themselves killed.

Lexi. Fierce, guarded Lexi, who had pushed him away as often as she'd pulled him close. Lexi, who looked at him sometimes with terror in her eyes that had nothing to do with external threats.

"I won't let anything happen to her," Drevik said, and meant it with every fiber of his being.

"You can't promise that." Thornwood's voice was rough. "No one can. But you can promise not to throw your life away recklessly. You can promise to survive, to fight smart instead of just hard. You can promise to come back to her."

The journal was heavy in Drevik's hands. His father's words. His parents' investigation. A traitor among the Elders. Lexi, who might actually—

"I need to know something," Drevik said. "If the traitor is an Elder, why haven't they killed you? You've been investigating for twenty-one years."

Thornwood's expression flickered, the first crack in his controlled facade. "I've wondered the same thing. The only explanation that makes sense is that my survival serves their purpose somehow. That leaving me alive is more valuable than eliminating me."

"Or they're confident you'll never find them."

"That too." He moved back to his desk, retrieving a small crystal vial from a drawer. "This is for tomorrow night. A protective talisman, blessed by the last Veiled One before your grandmother. It will shield you from direct magical assault for approximately thirty seconds."

"Thirty seconds doesn't seem like much."

"It's enough to strike or escape. Use it wisely." He pressed the vial into Drevik's palm. "And read your father's journal before the ceremony. There are names in there, names of people your parents trusted. Trust no one who wasn't investigated by them."

Drevik pocketed the vial, the journal tucked under his arm. "Why give me all this now? Why trust me?"

Thornwood studied him for a long moment, those green eyes—Lexi's eyes—unreadable.

"Because you're still here. Despite every test, every attack, every attempt to break you, you're still standing. You've earned the right to know the truth." He paused. "And because you look at my daughter the way your father looked at your mother. Like you'd burn the world down to keep her safe."

Drevik couldn't deny it. Didn't try to.

"If I find the traitor," he said, "what do you want me to do?"

"Survive. Bring me proof. And let me handle the rest." The Elder's shadows flickered, dark and dangerous. "I've waited twenty-one years for justice. I can wait a little longer to deliver it myself."

The dismissal was implicit in his tone. Drevik turned toward the door, mind racing with revelations, but Thornwood's voice stopped him.

"Acolyte."

He looked back.

"Lexi will be at the ceremony tonight. She's been assigned as your escort for the lunar rites." The words were carefully casual. "Whatever happens between now and dawn, make sure she doesn't have to mourn you."

Drevik nodded once and stepped into the corridor.

The walk back to his quarters felt endless, his father's journal pressing against his side like a living thing. The shadows said around him, carrying secrets he was only beginning to understand.

His parents had been murdered. A traitor sat among the Elders. Elder Thornwood, Lexi's father, had been testing him, pushing him, measuring his worth against some invisible standard.

And Lexi—

She argues your case in council meetings. She volunteers for your training sessions.

She nearly killed three Shadowborn single-handedly when you were trapped.

My daughter has a habit of loving people who get themselves killed.

The implication settled into his bones, warm and terrifying. Lexi cared about him. Genuinely, deeply, in ways she probably hadn't meant to show.

And tomorrow night, at the full moon ceremony, he would have to face her knowing that her father had just given him permission—no, a command—to survive.

Don't make her bury another.

Drevik reached his quarters and closed the door behind him, leaning against the cool wood. The journal felt heavy in his hands, filled with his father's secrets and his parents' last desperate attempt to protect him from beyond the grave.

He opened to the first page and read.

If you're reading this, I didn't survive long enough to tell you myself. But know this: the Order is not what it seems. We are not the only ones who serve the shadows, and the true enemy has been among us all along.

These are the names of those I trust. Everyone else is suspect.

Drevik read into the night, his father's words painting a picture of conspiracy and betrayal that reached higher than he'd ever imagined.

And somewhere in the compound, Lexi Thornwood was preparing for a ceremony that might change everything.

Tomorrow night, he thought, we'll see who survives.

The shadows in his room had deepened to near-black by the time he finished reading. His father's journal contained names, dates, locations—evidence of a conspiracy that spanned decades. The traitor wasn't just working with the Shadowborn; they were feeding the Order's own people to them, sacrificing acolytes and seekers in a twisted bargain for power.

And three names appeared repeatedly, circled in red ink:

Elder Vance. Elder Seirin. Elder Thornwood.

His father hadn't known which one to trust either.

Drevik closed the journal carefully, his mind racing. Elder Thornwood had given him this book, had claimed to be his ally, but his own father had suspected him.

Trust no one who wasn't investigated by your parents.

And yet Thornwood had been investigated. His name appeared in the journal dozens of times, always with question marks, always with notes like motives unclear and actions don't align with suspected goals.

If Thornwood was the traitor, this was an elaborate manipulation. If he wasn't, then the real enemy remained hidden, watching, waiting.

Drevik's door opened without a knock.

He looked up to find Lexi standing in the threshold, her pale green eyes sharp with barely contained emotion. She wore training clothes, her auburn hair pulled back, and she looked like she'd been running.

"We need to talk." Her voice was clipped. "My father just sent word. You're assigned to me for tomorrow's ceremony."

"I know. He told me."

She crossed the room in three quick strides, stopping inches from him. Her shadow flickered with agitation, the darkness around her form restless and unsettled.

"What did he say to you?"

"That he's been testing me. That my parents were murdered. That there's a traitor among the Elders." Drevik met her gaze steadily. "That you've been arguing my case in council meetings."

Lexi's jaw tightened. "He had no right—"

"He had every right. He's your father." Drevik set the journal aside. "He told me not to make you bury anyone else."

Something shifted in her expression. The anger remained, but beneath it—fear. Genuine, raw fear.

"Drevik, tomorrow night is dangerous. The full moon ceremony isn't just ritual. It's when the barriers between realms thin enough for things to cross over. If someone is planning to betray the Order—"

"Then we'll face it together." He stood, closing the distance between them. "I won't let anything happen to you."

"That's not—" She stopped, breath catching. "You can't promise that."

"Your father said the same thing."

"Then listen to him." Her voice cracked. "I've buried too many people, Drevik. My mother. Friends. People I thought would always be there. I can't—" She turned away, shoulders rigid. "I can't do it again."

Drevik reached for her, his hand settling on her arm. "Lexi."

She didn't pull away.

"I'm not going anywhere," he said. "Whatever happens tomorrow, whatever your father's tests revealed, whatever my parents discovered—I'm fighting to stay. For the Order. For the truth." He paused. "For you."

Lexi turned back to him, and her eyes glistened with tears she refused to let fall.

"You don't know what you're promising."

"Then tell me."

She stared at him for a long moment, the air between them thick with tension. Then she moved, closing the remaining distance until they were nearly touching.

"Tomorrow night," she said, "after the ceremony, if we both survive... I'm not holding back anymore."

Drevik's pulse spiked. "Lexi—"

"I've been afraid. Afraid of losing you like I've lost everyone else." Her hands came up to rest against his chest. "But your meeting with my father made something clear. If we're walking into danger, if tomorrow might be the end—I don't want any more regrets."

He covered her hands with his own. "We're going to survive."

"Promise me."

"I promise."

Lexi rose on her toes, brushing her lips against his in a kiss that was barely a whisper—soft, tentative, a vow waiting to be sealed. Then she pulled back, her eyes fierce despite the moisture clinging to her lashes.

"Read your father's journal. Prepare for tomorrow. And when the moon rises, stay close to me."

She was gone before he could respond, the door closing softly behind her.

Drevik stood in the darkness, his lips still burning from her touch, his father's secrets heavy in his hands.

Tomorrow night, everything would change.

He could only hope they'd both survive to see the dawn.

Chapter 12: The Full Moon Ceremony

Chapter 12: The Full Moon Ceremony

The moon hung above the university's clock tower like a silver coin waiting to be spent, and somewhere beneath Drevik's feet, two hundred members of the Order of the Eclipsed Veil were preparing to spend it.

He stood at the entrance to the hidden compound, the weight of ceremonial robes heavy on his shoulders. The fabric was midnight blue, embroidered with silver thread that shift and move in his peripheral vision—shadows given form, the elder who'd presented them explained. The robe of an Initiate transitioning to Acolyte.

"You're not moving," said a voice behind him.

Drevik turned to find Marcus Chen, the senior Warden who'd been assigned as his sponsor for the evening. The man's salt-and-pepper hair was pulled back in a formal knot, his own robes a deep charcoal that marked his higher station.

"Nervous," Drevik admitted.

"Good." Marcus clapped him on the shoulder. "Means you understand what's about to happen. The full moon ceremony isn't just pageantry, boy. It's the one night each month when the Veil thins and every member of the Order can draw power directly from the Shadowlands itself. Tonight, you'll be baptized in that power. It will change you."

"I've already been changed."

A thin smile. "Then prepare to be changed again."

They descended together through the hidden passage beneath the library's eastern wing. The stairs spiraled down into darkness, but torches flickered to life as they passed—actual flames, not electric lights, burning without fuel or smoke.

"The old ways," Marcus explained, noting Drevik's glance. "On this night, we honor tradition."

The central chamber opened before them like the throat of some vast creature, vaulted ceilings lost in shadow, walls carved with symbols that breathe. Drevik had been here before, during his initial initiation, but tonight it was transformed. Candles lined every surface—hundreds of them, perhaps thousands—their light reflecting off crystals embedded in the stone until the entire space shimmered like the inside of a geode.

And the people.

Members of the Order filled the chamber in a spiral pattern, their robes creating a gradient from the pale silver of the Veiled Ones at the center to the deep midnight of the initiates at the outer edge. Drevik counted perhaps two hundred figures, faces he recognized and faces he didn't, all standing in silence with their eyes fixed on the domed ceiling.

Where a perfect circle of glass revealed the full moon, already beginning to glow with an intensity that defied physics.

"Your place is there," Marcus said, pointing to a gap in the outer ring. "When the Elder calls your name, step forward. Until then, observe. Learn. And whatever you do, don't break the circle."

Drevik moved to his position, acutely aware of the eyes tracking him. Some curious, some hostile, most simply assessing. He was, after all, an outsider—the scholarship student who'd stumbled into their ranks rather than being born to them. Every step was like walking across a stage under hot lights.

Then he saw her.

Lexi stood three rows inward, her auburn hair loose around her shoulders for the first time since he'd known her. Her robes were the deep green of a Seeker, and they fit her differently than her usual practical clothes—less utilitarian, more ceremonial, hugging her form in ways that made his throat tighten. Candles flickered around her like attendants, and her green eyes found his across the chamber.

For a moment, neither looked away.

She looked powerful. Not the capable, athletic power he was used to seeing from her, but something older, deeper. She looked like someone who could command shadows and summon darkness, because she was. And she was looking at him with an expression he couldn't quite read—something between pride and fear and want.

Want.

The realization settled into his bones. He'd spent weeks watching her push him away, pull him close, alternate between coldness and warmth until he couldn't tell which was the real Lexi. But tonight, stripped of pretense, surrounded by ancient magic, she wasn't hiding anything.

She wanted him.

And gods help him, he wanted her too.

The sound of a bell broke the moment—a deep, resonant tone that come from the walls themselves. The chamber fell into absolute silence, and Drevik turned toward the center of the spiral.

The Veiled Ones had emerged.

Three figures in pale silver robes, their faces obscured by gauzy fabric that shimmered like moonlight on water. They moved in perfect synchronization, their steps leaving ripples of shadow in their wake. Drevik felt the power radiating from them like heat from a forge, and understood for the first time just how far he had to climb.

"Welcome, children of the Veil." The voice came from all three figures simultaneously, layered and strange. "The moon reaches its zenith. The boundary weakens. We gather to renew our bonds and strengthen our purpose."

Another bell tone, and the moonlight through the glass ceiling intensified, turning from silver to pale gold. Drevik felt it in his blood—a pull, a hunger, a connection to something vast and dark and patient.

"Who presents for the rank of Acolyte?"

Marcus's voice rang out. "Drevik Markinsoul, sponsored by the Wardens, tested and found worthy."

Tested. He thought of the past weeks—the trials, the study, the battles against shadow creatures that had slipped through the Veil. He thought of the blood on his hands and the fear in his gut and the moments when he'd nearly broken. All of it leading here.

"Step forward, Drevik Markinsoul."

He walked the spiral path inward, passing rows of robed figures who watched in silence. The air grew thicker as he approached the center, heavy with power and possibility. When he reached the Veiled Ones, they parted to reveal a basin of black stone filled with what looked like liquid shadow.

"Kneel."

He knelt on cold stone, his eyes level with the basin's rim. The shadow-substance rippled without any wind or movement, responding to his presence.

"Drevik Markinsoul, you came to us as an outsider. You have proven yourself through trial and tribulation. Tonight, the moon blesses you with its full power. Tonight, you become one with the Order in truth."

One of the Veiled Ones produced a silver blade and drew it across Drevik's palm. The cut was shallow, but his blood—dark and red and impossibly bright against his skin—dripped into the basin.

The shadow erupted.

Not violently—more like a sigh, a release. The liquid darkness swirled upward, forming a column that wrapped around Drevik's arm, his shoulder, his chest. He felt it searching, probing, tasting him with something like curiosity.

And then it poured into him.

The pain was immediate and absolute—not physical, but something deeper. The shadow was weighing his soul, measuring his worth, and it was not gentle about it. He felt his memories flashing through his mind: his grandmother's hands, his parents' faces in the one photograph he owned, every failure and triumph and moment of weakness laid bare.

He didn't break.

He couldn't have explained why, but he held on. Not through strength—the shadow was stronger than any strength he had—but through surrender. He opened himself to it, let it see everything, and refused to flinch.

Accepted, something whisper. Marked.

The shadow receded, leaving him gasping, his robes now marked with patterns of silver thread that hadn't been there before. He looked down at his hands and saw faint lines of darkness beneath his skin, like veins of ink.

"Rise, Acolyte Drevik Markinsoul. The Shadowlands recognize you."

He rose on unsteady legs, and the chamber erupted—not in applause, that would be too mundane, but in a low, resonant hum that come from every throat simultaneously. The sound vibrated through his bones, welcoming him.

Then the ceremony shifted.

The Veiled Ones began a new chant, and the moonlight intensified again. Drevik felt power flowing into everyone present—each member drawing from the thinned Veil, each feeding on the energy that seeped through from the Shadowlands.

"Pair for the sharing," the Veiled Ones intoned. "Let power flow between you. Let bonds strengthen and grow."

Drevik watched as members turned to each other, forming pairs based on some system he didn't understand. The pairs placed their hands on each other's shoulders, and he saw the power flowing between them—dark and light, shadow and moonlight, moving in circuits that made the whole chamber glow.

"You."

He turned to find Lexi behind him, her face unreadable.

"Me?"

"The pairing is determined by magical affinity. Ours is..." She hesitated. "Strong. The strongest I've felt with anyone."

He didn't know what to say to that, so he said nothing.

"Turn around. Hands on my shoulders."

He obeyed, feeling the warmth of her through the thin ceremonial fabric. She placed her own hands on his shoulders in turn, and for a moment they simply stood there, two people connected by formal necessity.

Then the magic flow.

It started as a tingle, a spark where their skin touched. But it quickly became more—a current of power that ran from her to him and back again, building in intensity with each cycle. Drevik gasped as he felt her magic intertwining with his own, their energies learning each other, merging.

And with the merge came something else.

He felt her.

Not just her power, but her. Her emotions, her thoughts, the shape of her consciousness pressing against his own. He felt her pride in her position, her fear of failing her family's expectations. He felt her loneliness—a profound, aching loneliness that had been with her for years, masked by competence and determination.

He felt her attraction to him, raw and unfiltered.

You, she was thinking, the word carrying a weight of meaning beyond language. It's you. I've been fighting it and I can't anymore.

He opened his eyes—when had he closed them?—and found her staring at him, her green gaze luminous in the candlelight. Her lips were parted, her breath coming fast. She was feeling him too—his confusion, his desire, his desperate need for belonging.

I see you, she said in his mind. I see all of you.

And I see you.

The magic between them intensified, becoming something more than ritual. This was intimate in ways that transcended the physical, two souls touching directly. Drevik felt himself harden in response, his body reacting to the closeness even as his mind reeled from the emotional connection.

Lexi—

Don't. Her mental voice was ragged. Don't say anything. Just feel.

The chamber around them faded. There was only her—her presence in his mind, her hands on his shoulders, her breath across his face as they swayed closer together. He could have kissed her. He wanted to kiss her. Every fiber of his being ached to close the distance and—

The vision hit him like a fist.

He gasped, his grip tightening on her shoulders as the world shattered around him. Suddenly he wasn't in the chamber anymore—he was elsewhere, watching events unfold in fractured, overlapping possibilities.

The ceremony continues. The moon reaches its peak. A figure in shadow moves toward the Veiled Ones, a blade in hand—

An alarm sounds. Members rush toward a breach in the outer ward. The chamber empties, leaving the Veiled Ones unprotected—

A scream. Blood on silver robes. The Veiled Ones fall, one by one, and with each death, the Veil tears further open—

Shadow creatures pour through. Dozens. Hundreds. The Order is slaughtered—

Drevik tries to stop it. He reaches for the assassin, but he's too slow—

He reaches again. He makes it in time, but the assassin is someone he knows, someone he trusted—

He reaches a third time. He stops the blade. The breach is sealed. But the cost—

The visions fractured into a dozen more possibilities, each one showing him a different path, a different outcome. He saw victories that cost lives, sacrifices that saved no one, choices that led to ruin. He saw Lexi dying in his arms, the Veiled Ones falling, the Order burning.

He saw, through it all, one constant: the alarm was a distraction.

The real attack came from within.

"Drevik!"

He came back to himself with a start, finding Lexi's face inches from his own, her expression tight with concern. The magic between them had faded—when had the ritual ended?—but she was still holding him, still close.

"You collapsed," she said. "Your nose is bleeding."

He touched his face, felt the wetness. Around them, other members were watching with curiosity and concern, but the ceremony continued, the Veiled Ones moving through the crowd.

"What did you see?" Lexi's voice was barely a breath. "Your magic spiked. I felt it. What did you see?"

He wanted to tell her. He wanted to warn her, to share the terrible knowledge pressing against his skull. But the vision had been clear—if he acted openly, if he revealed what he'd Seen, everything would fall apart.

"Nothing," he lied. "Overwhelmed. Too much power."

Her eyes narrowed. She knew he was lying. Their brief connection had been too intimate for deception. But she didn't push, didn't expose him.

"Take a moment," she said instead. "The ceremony continues for another hour. Stay close to me."

Stay close to me. He felt the weight of those words, the promise hidden within them. Later, when this was over, they would talk. They would have to.

But first—

An alarm split the air.

It came from everywhere at once—the walls, the ceiling, the floor itself—a shrieking sound that sent every member of the Order into motion. Drevik felt the chamber's energy shift from ritual calm to battle readiness in an instant.

"Breach," someone shouted. "Outer ward breach!"

Members were running now, streaming toward the exits in organized patterns that spoke of long practice. The Veiled Ones remained at the center, their silver robes shimmering with protective magic.

"Come on." Lexi grabbed his wrist. "We're needed at the perimeter."

Drevik let her pull him toward the exit, but his mind was racing. The vision had shown him this—the alarm, the rush to respond, the chamber emptying. It was happening exactly as he'd foreseen.

And the real attack was still coming.

"Lexi, wait—"

"There's no time for—"

"I know." He stopped, pulling her to a halt in the flow of bodies. "I know this is a distraction."

Her eyes searched his face. "How?"

"I told you. The power was overwhelming." He couldn't explain, not here, not now. But he needed her to believe him. "Lexi, the real attack is inside. Someone's going after the Veiled Ones."

Her expression flickered—disbelief, then something else. She'd felt his magic, felt the strength of his gift. She knew, on some level, that he was capable of seeing things others couldn't.

"The Veiled Ones have guards," she said, but her voice was uncertain.

"Not enough. Lexi, please—"

Another alarm sounded, different from the first. This one came from within the chamber itself.

Drevik spun around, his blood running cold.

The crowd had thinned significantly—perhaps half the members had rushed to respond to the outer breach. Those who remained were turning toward the center of the spiral, where the Veiled Ones stood frozen in place.

Where a figure in dark robes was moving through the shadows, a blade gleaming in their hand.

"Stop!" Drevik shouted, breaking away from Lexi and running toward the center.

He felt the magic in his blood responding, the power he'd absorbed during the ritual rising to meet the moment. He didn't know what he was doing—only that he had to reach them, had to stop what was coming.

The figure in dark robes turned, and Drevik caught a glimpse of a face—

No.

The recognition hit him like ice water. He knew that face. He'd trusted that face.

The assassin smiled, raised their blade, and stepped through a shadow, vanishing completely.

"No!" Drevik reached the Veiled Ones, his chest heaving. "They're inside the shadows! They can Shadow Walk!"

The remaining members were converging now, the ceremony forgotten. Lexi appeared at his side, her face pale.

"Drevik, who was that? You saw their face."

He had. And the knowledge was a weight he wasn't ready to carry.

"Later," he said. "Right now, we have to—"

The lights went out.

Every candle, every magical flame, every source of illumination in the chamber vanished simultaneously. Darkness descended like a physical weight, absolute and complete.

Drevik felt Lexi's hand find his in the dark.

"Stay close to me," she said, echoing his earlier words.

Then the screaming started.

End of Chapter 12

Chapter 13: Chaos and Choice

Chapter 13: Chaos and Choice

The screaming started three seconds before the world tore apart.

Drevik was standing at the edge of the ceremonial chamber, watching Elder Thornwood address the gathered initiates, when the first rift opened. It wasn't like the controlled portals the Wardens created—this was a ragged wound in reality, bleeding shadow and cold air into the room. Something crawled through it.

He didn't have time to see what. His vision went white, and then—

—a figure in the crowd, hood drawn low, moving against the flow of fleeing bodies. Hands moving in a pattern Drevik recognized from his training. Shadow-knife forming. Elder Thornwood's back exposed. The blade flying. Three seconds. Two. One—

Drevik moved.

He didn't think. Thinking took too long. His body simply lunged, crossing the distance between the crowd and the dais in three desperate strides. He hit Elder Thornwood from behind, shoving the older man aside just as the shadow-knife sang through the air.

It was meant for the Elder's heart.

It found Drevik's shoulder instead.

The pain was immediate and absolute—not just physical, but wrong, like the blade was made of frozen nothing that wanted to eat him from the inside out. He heard himself scream as he went down, cracking his head against the stone floor. Above him, chaos erupted. More rifts opening. Shadow creatures pouring through. The assembled initiates scattering in panic.

And through it all, a hooded figure turning toward him, frozen in the act of retreat.

Their eyes met.

Drevik saw the shock in the stranger's face—shock that an initiate had stopped the assassination. Then the figure raised one hand, shadows gathering, and a portal swallowed them whole. But not before Drevik saw it: the glint of silver at the figure's collar.

Warden insignia.

A Warden. A Warden had just tried to kill an Elder.

"Drevik!"

Lexi's voice cut through the screaming and the roaring of shadow beasts. He tried to answer, but his lungs weren't working right. The shadow-knife had dissolved, leaving a wound that gaped and wept something that wasn't quite blood. He felt it trying to close, trying to heal, but the shadow-taint kept the edges from knitting.

Lexi's face appeared above him, pale and tight with fear. Her hands pressed against his shoulder, and he felt the cool rush of her healing magic trying to counter the corruption.

"You idiot," she said, but her voice cracked. "You absolute idiot, what were you thinking?"

"Assassin," Drevik managed. "Warden. Saw the insignia."

Her eyes went wide. Around them, the battle raged—Wardens and Seekers fighting shadow creatures, trying to close the rifts, trying to protect the fleeing initiates. But Lexi didn't move from his side.

"Thornwood," Drevik gasped. "Is he—"

"I don't care about Thornwood right now!" Lexi's hands pressed harder, and more healing magic flooded through him. It was like ice water in his veins, fighting the shadow-infection. "I care about you, you reckless—"

A shadow creature lunged at them from the darkness. Lexi didn't even turn—she raised one hand and blasted it apart with a bolt of pure lunar light, never breaking eye contact with Drevik.

"We need to move him," someone said—Drevik couldn't see who. His vision was swimming, the edges going dark.

"I can move him," Lexi snarled. "Get back to the fight."

"Lexi, you can't—"

"I said get back to the fight."

There was something in her voice that Drevik had never heard before. Not anger. Not fear. Something rawer. Something that made the other person retreat without another word.

Lexi's arms slid under him, careful of his wounded shoulder. She was strong—stronger than she looked, the result of years of training—and she lifted him like he weighed nothing. The world lurched sickeningly as she shadow-walked them both out of the ceremonial chamber.

They emerged in a small room Drevik didn't recognize. Stone walls, soft candlelight, a narrow bed. A healing space, he realized dimly. One of the Order's private recovery rooms.

Lexi lowered him onto the bed with surprising gentleness. Her hands were shaking.

"Lexi," Drevik tried. "The battle—"

"Others can handle it." Her voice was clipped, professional. "You need healing. Real healing. The shadow-taint is deep."
