She moved to a cabinet on the far wall, pulling out jars and bundles of herbs, a bowl, clean cloths. Her movements were quick and precise, but Drevik saw the tension in her shoulders. The way her jaw was clenched.

"Lexi."

She didn't turn around.

"Lexi."

She froze. Then slowly, she faced him. In the soft candlelight, her face was a mask of controlled fear—but her eyes betrayed her. They were wet. Red-rimmed.

"Healing requires skin contact," she said, her voice barely steady. "I need to remove your shirt."

Drevik nodded, trying to sit up. His body screamed in protest. Lexi was there instantly, supporting his back, helping him pull the ruined fabric over his head. The movement jostled his wound, and he bit back a groan.

She lowered him back to the bed, then stood looking down at him. Her gaze traced the wound—the ugly gash across his shoulder, the blackened veins spreading from it where the shadow-taint was trying to crawl deeper into his body. Her expression shifted from fear to something harder. Determination.

"This is going to hurt," she warned. "The shadow-taint fights back when you try to purge it."

"I trust you."

Something flickered in her eyes at those words. She swallowed hard, then climbed onto the bed beside him, straddling his hips. The position was practical—she needed to brace herself to channel enough healing energy—but Drevik's body didn't care about practicality. Even through the pain, he was aware of her weight settling over him. The warmth of her thighs against his sides. The way her auburn hair fell forward as she leaned close.

"Look at me," she commanded softly. "Don't close your eyes. If you start to fade, I need to know."

He nodded. She placed both hands over the wound.

The healing began.

It was like fire and ice at once—her magic burning through the shadow-taint, freezing the corruption in its tracks. Drevik's back arched off the bed as pain lanced through him. He grabbed at something—her hip, her thigh—and she didn't pull away. Just kept channeling, her face tight with concentration, sweat beading on her forehead.

"That's it," she said, though whether to him or herself, he couldn't tell. "Fight it. Don't let it take hold."

He felt the shadow-taint retreating, pushed back by the relentless force of her healing. But the pain was blinding. He was dimly aware that he was making sounds—gasps, groans, sounds that might have been words if he could form them. Through it all, Lexi held steady, her weight grounding him, her eyes never leaving his face.

"Almost," she said. "Just a little more—"

The last of the shadow-taint gave way. The wound close properly, new tissue knitting together with supernatural speed. Drevik collapsed back against the bed, panting, his whole body trembling with exhaustion and relief.

Lexi slumped forward, catching herself on her hands. Her face was inches from his, both of them breathing hard. The healing had taken a lot out of her—Drevik saw the fatigue in her features, the dark circles under her eyes.

But she was still looking at him with that raw, unguarded expression.

"You could have died," she said.

"I know."

"You idiot." But there was no venom in it. Just pain. "You threw yourself in front of a shadow-knife. For an Elder you barely know. You could have—"

She broke off. Her whole body was shaking now.

"Lexi." Drevik raised his good arm, brushing his fingers against her cheek. She flinched like his touch burned, but didn't pull away. "I'm okay."

"You're not okay." Her voice cracked. "You have a scar that will never fully fade. The shadow-taint left a mark. Every new moon, it'll ache. You'll feel the cold of that knife for the rest of your life."

"Then I'll feel it." He traced his thumb along her cheekbone, catching a tear before it could fall. "I'd rather feel the cold than watch someone die when I could stop it."

She made a sound that was half-laugh, half-sob. "You stupid, noble—"

She kissed him.

It wasn't like their first kiss—that had been tentative, questioning. This was desperate. Hungry. Her hands fisted in his hair, and he pulled her down against him with his good arm, heedless of his healing shoulder. The pain was distant, irrelevant. All that mattered was her mouth on his, her body pressed against his chest, the taste of her tears on his lips.

"Lexi," he gasped between kisses. "The door—someone might—"

"I don't care." She kissed him again, harder. "I don't care about any of it."

But even as she said it, she was pulling back. Not far—just enough to look at him. Her eyes were red-rimmed but dry now. Her expression had shifted into something more composed, though the rawness still showed at the edges.

"I can't lose another person I care about," she said. "I've already lost too many. My mother. My brother. Friends who didn't make it through their trials." Her voice was steady, but Drevik saw the cost of that steadiness. "I won't lose you too. I can't."

He understood then what she was really saying. Not just that she cared about him. That he'd become essential. That the thought of losing him broke something in her that she didn't know how to fix.

"You won't lose me," he said. "I promise."

"Promises mean nothing in the Order." Her thumb traced along his jaw. "We both know that."

"Then I'll just have to survive anyway."

She laughed—a wet, shaky sound. "You're impossible."

"Yeah." He smiled up at her, exhausted and aching and happier than he had any right to be. "But you like impossible."

She shook her head, but she was almost smiling too. "We should get back. They'll need help with the cleanup."

"Right." He tried to sit up, and she shifted off him, helping him upright. His shoulder throbbed, but the healing was holding. "The assassin—"

"Escaped." Lexi's expression hardened. "You're sure it was a Warden?"

"Saw the insignia. Silver collar pin. Only Wardens have them."

Lexi was quiet for a moment. "That means—"

"It means the traitor is someone high up. Someone who's been in the Order long enough to earn that rank." Drevik reached for his ruined shirt, but Lexi handed him a clean one from the cabinet instead. "And it means no one can be trusted."

She watched him dress, her expression thoughtful. "Except each other."

He met her eyes. "Except each other."

The aftermath of the attack was controlled chaos.

Drevik emerged from the healing room to find the ceremonial chamber strewn with debris. The rifts had been closed, the shadow creatures destroyed, but the Order was in an uproar. Initiates huddled in groups, whispering frantically. Wardens strode through the halls with grim faces, their eyes scanning for threats.

Elder Thornwood was alive. Drevik had seen to that.

The Elder was standing near the dais, deep in conversation with two other Elders. His face was pale, his robes torn where Drevik had shoved him aside, but he was whole. When he saw Drevik approaching, he fell silent.

"Ah," Thornwood said. "The young man who saved my life."

The other Elders turned. Drevik recognized Elder Vane—Seraphina's mother—and Elder Kethros, the stern woman who oversaw combat training. Their expressions were unreadable.

"Elder Thornwood." Drevik bowed, ignoring the protest from his healing shoulder. "I'm glad to see you unharmed."

"Unharmed thanks to you." Thornwood's gaze was sharp, assessing. "I'm told you intercepted a shadow-knife meant for my heart. That you saw the assassin when no one else did."

"I had a vision, Elder. Just seconds of foresight. Enough to know something was wrong."

"A vision." Elder Kethros's voice was flat. "Foresight is a rare gift."

"It's unreliable," Drevik admitted. "I can't control it. But when it comes, the images are clear."

Thornwood exchanged a look with Elder Vane. "And what did you see clearly, young initiate?"

" The assassin's insignia. A Warden's collar pin. Silver, with the eclipsed moon symbol."

The Elders went very still.

"You're certain?" Elder Vane asked quietly.

"I know what I saw."

"A serious accusation," Elder Kethros said. "If true, it means—"

"It means we have a traitor among our Wardens." Thornwood's voice was grim. "Someone who has earned our highest trust."

The words hung in the air. Drevik felt the weight of them—the implications, the danger. If a Warden was willing to assassinate an Elder, the corruption went deep.

"The attack was a diversion," Lexi said, stepping up beside Drevik. She'd composed herself, but he could still see the tension in her shoulders. "The rifts, the shadow creatures—all of it was meant to create chaos. To give the assassin cover."

"And it would have worked." Elder Vane's eyes were on Drevik. "If not for this young man's gift."

There was something strange in her expression. Drevik couldn't quite read it—approval, perhaps, or something deeper.

"Initiate Markinsoul." Thornwood's voice was formal now. "Your actions tonight demonstrated courage, quick thinking, and loyalty to the Order. You placed yourself in mortal danger to protect one of our own."

"It was the right thing to do."

"Indeed it was." Thornwood glanced at the other Elders, who nodded. "In recognition of your bravery, and in light of the abilities you've demonstrated, I hereby advance you to the rank of Acolyte."

Drevik blinked. "Elder?"

"You've earned it." A faint smile crossed Thornwood's weathered face. "Though I suspect your path forward will be far more dangerous than most. The traitor saw your face. Knows you can identify them."

Drevik hadn't considered that. But of course—the assassin would see him as a threat now. Someone who could expose them.

"I understand, Elder."

"I believe you do." Thornwood clasped his shoulder—gently, on the uninjured side. "Welcome to the Order, Acolyte Markinsoul. May the shadows serve you well."

The ceremony was brief.

Drevik stood before the gathered members—those who'd survived the attack, those who weren't wounded or busy with cleanup—and spoke the words he'd memorized weeks ago. Vows of loyalty, of service, of sacrifice. The traditional oath of the Acolyte.

I swear by shadow and moonlight to serve the Order. To walk between worlds and guard the Veil. To give my life if the balance demands it.

The words felt different now. Heavier. He'd nearly given his life tonight, and he understood in a way he hadn't before what that promise meant.

Lexi watched from the crowd. Her expression was carefully neutral, but Drevik saw the pride underneath.

After the ceremony, there were congratulations. Slaps on the back, nods of respect, a few curious looks from other initiates who'd been at the gathering. Drevik accepted them all with as much grace as he could muster, but his shoulder was aching, and exhaustion was creeping up on him.

He needed to rest. To process. To figure out what came next.

He was almost to his dorm room when the shadows near his door rippled.

A message scroll materialized from the darkness—the Order's way of sending urgent communications. Drevik caught it before it could fall, breaking the seal with practiced ease.

The handwriting was familiar. Seraphina.

They know I talked to you. I have to run. But I left something for you in your dorm room. It will tell you who the traitor is.

Be careful, Drevik. Trust no one.

Drevik's chest tightened as he read the words again. His hand closed around the scroll, crumpling it.

Seraphina was running. She was scared. And she'd left him evidence—evidence that could expose a Warden.

He pushed open his door.

The room was dark. His roommate was out—probably still helping with the attack aftermath. Drevik stepped inside, his newly-healed shoulder throbbing, and reached for the light.

Something was sitting on his pillow.

A small leather journal, bound with black cord. He didn't recognize it—had never seen it before. But when he opened it, he recognized Seraphina's handwriting.

Notes. Names. Dates. A careful record of everything she'd observed.

And on the first page, circled in red ink, a single name.

Drevik stared at it, his blood running cold.

The traitor wasn't just a Warden.

The traitor was someone he knew. Someone who'd been watching him since his first day in the Order.

Someone who, even now, might be wondering if Seraphina had left anything behind.

Drevik looked at the door. Then at the journal.

He had a choice to make.

And whatever he chose, there would be no going back.

End of Chapter 13

Chapter 14: Evidence and Escalation

Chapter 14: Evidence and Escalation

The message waited for him in the last place anyone would think to look.

Drevik stood in his new quarters—his own quarters, a privilege of his promotion to Acolyte—and stared at the leather-bound journal Seraphina had pressed into his hands three days ago. He'd searched it twice already, finding nothing but her cramped notes on shadow manipulation techniques and observations about lunar phase correlations. Nothing useful.

But something had nagged at him since his conversation with Ivy. She wouldn't have risked meeting you just to pass along study materials.

He flipped to the back of the journal, running his fingers along the inside of the leather binding. The spine crackled under his touch—older than the pages, slightly uneven. His thumbnail caught on a seam he hadn't noticed before.

A hidden compartment.

The folded paper that slid out was thin as onion skin, covered in Seraphina's precise handwriting.

Names, he realized, his heart hammering against his ribs. She gave me names.

The list was devastating in its simplicity.

Seven names. Seven members of the Order of the Eclipsed Veil who Seraphina believed were working to weaken the barrier between the mortal realm and the Shadowlands. Each entry included a location, a role within the Order, and a single line of corroborating evidence.

His eyes caught on the fourth name and locked there.

Mira Blackwood — Warden, Western Chapter. Based in Seattle. Attends all regional gatherings. Present at the Breach incident in Portland, 2019. No official record of her presence exists.

A Warden. Seraphina had implicated a Warden.

Drevik's mouth went dry. Wardens were the Order's elite operatives, the ones who hunted Shadowlands entities that breached into the mortal realm and kept the peace between chapters. Accusing one of treason was akin to accusing a general of aiding the enemy.

He read the next line and nearly dropped the paper.

Mira Blackwood was the investigating officer on the Markinsoul case. She closed it as 'natural causes' within forty-eight hours.

His parents. This woman had signed off on the investigation into his parents' deaths.

The room tilt around him. He'd spent three years believing his parents had died in a car accident—an unfortunate collision with a drunk driver on a rain-slicked highway. The Order had told him there was nothing suspicious, nothing to suggest Shadowlands involvement.

And now Seraphina's list suggested the person who'd made that determination might be compromised.

He thought of his father's journal, the one his grandmother had passed down to him. He'd only skimmed it, assuming it contained personal reflections rather than anything relevant to the Order. But if his parents had been getting close to something dangerous—

A knock at his door made him shove the paper back into the hidden compartment, pulse quickening.

"Drevik?" Lexi's voice, muffled through the wood. "You decent?"

"Coming." He slid the journal under his pillow and crossed to the door.

Lexi stood in the hallway wearing her training gear—tight black leggings, a fitted tank top, her auburn hair pulled back in a severe ponytail. She looked ready for combat. The expression on her face suggested she'd already fought several battles this morning.

"We need to talk." She pushed past him into his room without waiting for an invitation.

"Please, come in," he said dryly, closing the door behind her.

She turned to face him, arms crossed over her chest. "You've been avoiding me."

"I've been busy. There's a difference."

"Three days, Drevik. You've had three days since your promotion ceremony, and you've been impossible to find." Her green eyes searched his face with an intensity that made him want to look away. "What's going on?"

He could tell her about the list. He should tell her about the list. But Seraphina's warning echoed in his memory: Trust no one. Not even the people you want to trust.

"I'm adjusting to my new responsibilities," he said instead. "They've got me shadowing Elder Corvin for the next week. Learning the administrative side of chapter operations."

Lexi's jaw tightened. "That's not what I'm asking about, and you know it."

"What do you want me to say, Lexi?"

"The truth." She stepped closer, close enough that he could smell the faint traces of lavender soap on her skin. "I thought we were past the point where you hide things from me."

The accusation stung because she wasn't wrong. Since his initiation, Lexi had been his guide, his trainer, the person who pushed him to be better even when he wanted to quit. They'd bled together, fought shadow creatures together, shared a bed in a safe house when the alternative was freezing to death during a winter mission gone wrong.

She'd seen him at his worst. He owed her more than deflection.

"I found something," he admitted quietly. "Something Seraphina gave me before she disappeared."

Lexi's expression shifted, suspicion giving way to sharp attention. "What kind of something?"

"Names. Of people inside the Order who might be working with the Shadowlands."

Her eyes widened. "How many names?"

"Seven." He held her gaze. "Including a Warden."

"Shit." She ran a hand through her hair, the composed mask cracking. "Drevik, do you understand what you're saying? You can't just accuse a Warden of—"

"I'm not accusing anyone of anything yet. I'm telling you what I found."

"Who else knows?"

"Just you." He paused. "And whoever Seraphina was working with. She mentioned a contact before she went to ground, but she didn't give me a name."

Lexi absorbed this in silence, her mind clearly racing through the implications. Drevik watched her process the information, watched her weigh the risks of knowing against the risks of ignorance.

Finally, she exhaled. "Okay. We figure this out together. Just like always."

"Lexi..."

"Don't." Her voice sharpened. "Whatever you're about to say, don't. I've been with you since the beginning of this mess, and I'm not going to let you face down a potential conspiracy alone because you're afraid of putting me in danger."

"I'm not afraid for you," he said, the words slipping out before he could stop them. "I'm afraid of what happens if I'm wrong. If I drag you into something that blows back on both of us."

Something flickered in her expression—surprise, maybe, or something softer. She stepped even closer, reducing the space between them to inches.

"You don't get to make that choice for me," she said. "I decide what risks I'm willing to take. And I'm telling you right now: I'm willing to take this one."

The air between them felt charged, heavy with unspoken words. Drevik was suddenly, acutely aware of how close she was, of the rise and fall of her chest beneath the thin tank top, of the warmth radiating from her skin.

"Lexi." Her name came out rougher than he intended.

Her gaze dropped to his mouth, then back up to his eyes. "Yeah?"

He wanted to kiss her. The realization hit him with the force of a physical blow. He wanted to close the remaining distance, to taste her lips, to feel her body against his. Three days of avoiding her hadn't dimmed the pull between them—it had only sharpened it.

But before he could move, before he could act on the impulse, another knock shattered the moment.

"Drevik Markinsoul?" An unfamiliar voice, formal and clipped. "Elder Corvin requests your presence in the assignment chamber."

Lexi stepped back quickly, her expression smoothing into neutrality. "That's your summons. You should go."

"Lexi—"

"We'll talk later." She moved toward the door, pausing with her hand on the frame. "And Drevik? Whatever happens in that meeting, remember that you've earned your place here. Don't let anyone make you feel like you don't belong."

She was gone before he could respond, leaving him alone with the ghost of almost-something and the weight of secrets pressing down on his shoulders.

The assignment chamber was located in the oldest part of the compound, deep beneath the university's administration building. Drevik had never been granted access before, and the heavy iron doors and ward-etched walls made it clear why. This was where the Order made decisions that shaped lives.

Elder Corvin waited for him at a long table, flanked by two other figures Drevik didn't recognize. The Elder's silver-streaked hair was pulled back severely, and his dark eyes held the weight of decades of service.

"Acolyte Markinsoul," Corvin said, his voice resonating in the stone chamber. "Please, sit."

Drevik took the indicated chair, acutely aware of the scrutiny from the two strangers—a severe woman with iron-gray hair and a younger man with a scar that bisected his left eyebrow.

"Your promotion to Acolyte comes with new responsibilities," Corvin continued. "And new oversight. As you are no doubt aware, the transition from initiate to acolyte requires a change in handler assignment."

Drevik's stomach dropped. "Sir?"

"Alexa Thornwood has served as your guide during your initiation period. However, chapter regulations prohibit a handler from overseeing an acolyte with whom they share... personal entanglements."

The word 'entanglements' hung in the air like an accusation. Drevik kept his face neutral, but heat crept up the back of his neck.

"I wasn't aware there were any personal entanglements, Elder."

Corvin's expression didn't change. "The Order makes it our business to be aware of such things. Your connection to Acolyte Thornwood has been noted since your first month of training. It is not a condemnation—such bonds can strengthen our order—but it is a conflict of interest for oversight purposes."

Drevik absorbed this in silence. They'd been watching. Of course they'd been watching. The Order saw everything.

"Who will be my new handler?" he asked.

The severe woman spoke for the first time, her voice as cold as her gray eyes. "I am Warden Thessaly. I will be overseeing your continued training and assignment completion."

Warden. They'd assigned him a Warden as a handler. The oversight was either a tremendous honor or a prison sentence dressed up as protocol.

"Acolyte Markinsoul has shown unusual promise," the scarred man added. "His shadow-walking abilities are developing faster than any initiate in the past decade. The Order wishes to... nurture that potential."

There was something in his tone that made Drevik's skin prickle. Nurture wasn't quite the right word. Control felt closer to the truth.

"You will move to independent quarters effective immediately," Corvin said. "Your new duties include attending chapter briefings, contributing to regional intelligence analysis, and preparing for the upcoming gathering in Seattle. Warden Thessaly will provide your full assignment roster."

Seattle. The city where Mira Blackwood was based. The city where the regional gathering would take place in two weeks.

"Will there be an opportunity to meet with other chapters at the gathering?" Drevik asked carefully.

Corvin's eyes sharpened. "The gathering is a formality. Acolytes do not participate in inter-chapter discussions."

"Of course, Elder. I only meant—"

"You meant to assess the scope of your exposure." Corvin's smile didn't reach his eyes. "An appropriate question for an ambitious young acolyte. But remember: ambition without discretion is merely a faster path to failure."

The warning was clear. Stop asking questions.

"I understand, Elder."

"Good." Corvin stood, signaling the end of the meeting. "Warden Thessaly will escort you to your new duties. Welcome to the next phase of your service, Acolyte Markinsoul."

Drevik rose and bowed, the motion automatic after months of training. As he followed Warden Thessaly from the chamber, he felt the weight of the Elder's gaze on his back like a physical thing.

His new quarters were larger than his previous room, but they felt smaller. The stone walls pressed in from all sides, and the single window looked out onto an interior courtyard rather than the campus beyond.

More space, less freedom, he thought bitterly. The Order's idea of a promotion.

Warden Thessaly had left him with a stack of assignment briefings and a warning about curfew that felt more like a threat. "You are being watched, Acolyte Markinsoul. The Order has invested considerable resources in your development. Do not disappoint us."

Now alone, Drevik pulled out his father's journal—the one his grandmother had given him, not Seraphina's—and flipped to the pages he'd only skimmed before.

His father's handwriting was cramped and hurried, the letters slanting aggressively to the right. The early entries were mundane: observations about shadow-walking techniques, notes on lunar phase correlations, complaints about chapter politics. But as Drevik read deeper, the tone shifted.

September 14th — Met with M.B. today. She's concerned about the Portland chapter, says they've been compromised. I told her she was paranoid. She said I'd change my mind when I saw the evidence. She's sending a dossier through channels.

September 21st — The dossier arrived. I don't know what to make of it. If the information is accurate, this goes higher than I ever imagined. I need to verify before I bring it to the Elders.

September 28th — Verification is proving difficult. Records that should exist don't. Witnesses who should be available have been reassigned or retired. Someone is covering tracks, and they're doing it thoroughly.

October 5th — Elara wants me to stop. She's worried about what happens if the baby comes while I'm chasing shadows. I promised her I'd be careful. But I can't let this go. Whatever's happening inside the Order, it's bigger than one chapter. Bigger than one region.

October 12th — Meeting with M.B. at the regional gathering. She says she has names. I'll finally know who I'm dealing with.

The entry after that was dated October 15th—three days after the regional gathering.

Something went wrong. M.B. is dead. They're saying Shadowlands breach, but I was there. I saw what happened. That wasn't a breach. That was an assassination. I have the names she gave me, but I don't know who to trust with them. Elara says we should run. Maybe she's right.

His father had been investigating the same conspiracy. His father had met with someone—M.B., Mira Blackwood?—who had evidence of Shadowlands sympathizers within the Order. And three days after that meeting, his father was dead.

Drevik's hands shook as he turned to the final entry, dated the day before the car accident.

I've hidden the evidence. If you're reading this, I didn't make it. The names are in the usual place. Trust the Veiled One who walks between chapters. She knows more than she's said. She's the only one who might still be alive who knows the truth.

The usual place. Drevik had no idea what that meant. His father's belongings had been returned to his grandmother after the accident, but he'd never thought to search them for hidden compartments. And after his grandmother's death last year, everything had been boxed up and stored.

He needed to get those boxes. He needed to find whatever his father had hidden.

His door opened without a knock, and Drevik spun, shoving the journal under his pillow.

Lexi stood in the doorway, her expression tight with controlled anger.

"They told me I'm not allowed to be your handler anymore," she said flatly. "Apparently, I'm too personally entangled."

"They told me the same thing."

"Did they also tell you they're reassigning me to the Seattle chapter? Effective immediately?"

The words hit him like a physical blow. "What?"

"Two weeks before the regional gathering. I'm being sent ahead as part of the advance security team." She crossed her arms, the gesture more protective than aggressive. "They're separating us, Drevik. Deliberately."

He stood, moving toward her without conscious thought. "Lexi, I—"

"Don't apologize." Her voice cracked slightly. "Just... don't."

"Then what do you want me to say?"

She looked up at him, her green eyes bright with emotions she was clearly fighting to contain. "Tell me you're going to be careful. Tell me you're not going to do something stupid while I'm gone."

"You're the one who always does the stupid things."

A ghost of a smile crossed her face. "Yeah, well. Someone has to keep you interesting."

The silence held between them, heavy with everything they weren't saying. Drevik thought about the almost-kiss from earlier, about the way she'd looked at him, about the months of tension that had built between them.

"I'll come find you," he said. "At the gathering. Whatever they're trying to do by separating us, it won't work."

Lexi studied him for a long moment. Then, without warning, she closed the distance between them and kissed him.

It wasn't a gentle kiss. It was desperate and fierce, all the things she couldn't say pressed against his mouth in a single, searing moment. Drevik's hands found her waist, pulling her closer, and she made a sound against his lips that sent heat flooding through his veins.

She tasted like mint and something darker, like the edge of a blade. Her fingers tangled in his hair, and he walked her backward until her shoulders hit the wall beside his door.

"Lexi," he said against her mouth.

"Shut up." She pulled him back to her, kissing him harder, deeper. Her body pressed against his, all lean muscle and desperate intensity. "You talk too much."

He laughed against her lips, the sound surprised out of him. "That's rich, coming from the woman who just—"

She silenced him with another kiss, and this time he let her. His hands slid under the hem of her tank top, finding the warm skin of her back, and she shivered at his touch.

They stayed like that for long minutes, kissing in the dim light of his spartan quarters, learning each other in ways they never had before. Drevik was like he was drowning in her, in the scent of her skin and the taste of her mouth and the desperate way her fingers clutched at his shoulders.

Finally, Lexi pulled back, her breathing ragged. Her lips were swollen, her hair mussed from his hands.

"I should go," she said. "Before I do something that gets us both in more trouble."

"Stay."

The word slipped out before he could stop it. Her eyes softened, and for a moment he thought she might actually agree.

"I can't." She touched his cheek, the gesture almost tender. "They're watching, Drevik. They're always watching."

"Then let them watch."

She smiled, but it was sad. "You don't mean that. Not yet." She stepped back, putting distance between them. "I leave in the morning. Early. I probably won't see you before..."

"I'll find you at the gathering."

"You better." She moved toward the door, then paused with her hand on the frame. "And Drevik? Don't do anything stupid while I'm gone. That's my job."

Her voice wavered on the last word, and then she was gone, leaving him alone with the taste of her still on his lips.

He didn't sleep.

Instead, he lay in his narrow bed, staring at the ceiling, his mind racing through everything he'd learned. His father's journal. Seraphina's list. The promotion that felt more like a leash. Lexi's kiss and the way she'd looked at him before she walked away.

Around midnight, a shadow moved in the corner of his room.

Drevik sat up, power gathering in his hands, but a familiar voice stopped him.

"Relax, Acolyte. It's just me."

Ivy materialized from the darkness, her dark hair loose around her shoulders, her expression grave.

"Ivy. What are you doing here?"

"Warning you." She moved closer, keeping her voice low. "The gathering in Seattle is a trap. Multiple chapters, multiple factions. Everyone has an agenda, and half of them involve using you as a pawn."

"Using me for what?"

"Your abilities. Your connection to the Markinsoul line." Her eyes held his. "Your father wasn't just an ordinary member, Drevik. He was the last of a bloodline that the Order thought had died out centuries ago. When they discovered what he could do..."

"What could he do? What can I do?"

Ivy hesitated. "I don't know all of it. But I know it involves the Veil itself—the barrier between our world and the Shadowlands. Your father could manipulate it in ways no one else could. And from what I've seen of your training reports, so can you."

The implications washed over him like cold water. "They're not training me to fight Shadowlands creatures, are they? They're training me to control the Veil."

"They're training you to be a weapon." Ivy's voice was soft. "The question is: whose weapon?"

Drevik thought of the list hidden in Seraphina's journal. Of Mira Blackwood and the seven names. Of his father's desperate final entries.

"I have names," he said. "Seraphina gave me names of people inside the Order who might be working with the Shadowlands."

Ivy's expression flickered. "How many names?"

"Seven. Including a Warden."

"Mira Blackwood."

Drevik went still. "You know her."

"I know of her. She's dangerous, Drevik. More dangerous than you can imagine. And she'll be at the gathering."

"Then the gathering is my chance to get close to her. To find out what she knows about my parents."

Ivy gripped his arm, her fingers digging into his skin. "That's exactly what they want. You walking into a room with a woman who's killed everyone who's ever gotten close to the truth. You'll be dead before the night is over."

"Then what do you suggest?"

"Trust no one." She paused, something shifting in her expression. "Except maybe Lexi. She's requested transfer back to you."

Drevik blinked. "What?"

"After the gathering. She put in a formal request to be reassigned as your partner rather than your handler. It would give her proximity without the conflict of interest." Ivy's mouth twisted wryly. "She's smarter than I gave her credit for."

The news settled into Drevik's chest like a warm weight. Lexi had found a way. Despite everything the Order was doing to separate them, she'd found a way back.

"The gathering is in two weeks," he said. "What do I do until then?"

"Stay quiet. Do your assignments. Don't give anyone a reason to suspect you know more than you should." Ivy moved toward the shadows she'd emerged from. "And Drevik? Your father's evidence—the 'usual place' he mentioned. It's not in his belongings."

"Then where?"

Ivy's form was already fading, her voice coming from somewhere deep in the darkness. "The same place all secrets are kept in this Order. The archives. Behind the Veiled One's portrait in the eastern wing. The eyes are hollow."

And then she was gone, leaving Drevik with more questions than answers and the burning certainty that everything was about to get much, much worse.

He dreamed of his parents that night.

Not the vague, soft-focus memories he usually held onto—the way his mother laughed, the steady strength of his father's hands. No, this dream was sharp and terrible.

He stood in a dark room, shadows pressing in from all sides. His father knelt before a figure in ceremonial robes, the Veiled One's mask gleaming in the dim light.

You understand what we're asking? The voice was neither male nor female, young nor old.

I understand. His father's voice was steady, but Drevik saw the fear in his eyes. But my wife—my son—

Will be protected. That is our covenant.

And if I fail?

The masked figure tilted its head. Then the Veil falls. And everything you love dies.

His father reached out, pressing his palm against a stone tablet covered in symbols that writhe and shift under Drevik's gaze. Light blazed from the contact—

And Drevik woke, gasping, his palm burning as though he'd touched fire himself.

He stared at his hand in the darkness, half-expecting to see blisters forming. Instead, he found a mark he'd never noticed before: a thin silver line that curved from his wrist to his palm, glowing faintly in the moonlight.

The mark of a covenant.

His father had made a deal with the Veiled One. And somehow, Drevik had inherited the terms.

What the hell did you do, Dad?

He wouldn't find answers by staying in bed. Pulling on clothes in the darkness, Drevik grabbed his father's journal and Seraphina's hidden list, tucking both into the inner pocket of his jacket.

The archives were in the eastern wing. The Veiled One's portrait. The hollow eyes.

And somewhere in Seattle, a Warden who had killed his parents was waiting for him to walk into a trap.

Two weeks. He had two weeks to find the evidence his father had hidden, figure out who among the Order's leadership could be trusted, and prepare for a confrontation that might kill him.

But as Drevik slipped out of his quarters and into the shadowed corridors of the compound, he felt something he hadn't felt since his initiation: certainty.

He was going to find the truth. And when he did, the Order would never be the same.

End of Chapter 14

Chapter 15: Reunion and Reconnaissance

Chapter 15: Reunion and Reconnaissance

The knock came three hours before midnight, sharp and deliberate.

Drevik set aside his economics textbook—the mundane coursework felt increasingly absurd against the weight of what he'd learned about the Order—and crossed to his apartment door. He checked the peephole out of habit now, a reflex born from weeks of looking over his shoulder.

Lexi stood in the hallway, arms crossed, wearing the same leather jacket she'd worn the night they'd first met. Her auburn hair was longer than he remembered, brushing her shoulders in loose waves. Her green eyes met his through the small lens with an intensity that made his chest tighten.

He opened the door.

"Surprised to see me?" Her voice carried that familiar edge of challenge, but something underneath it had shifted. Something softer.

"You disappeared for two weeks." He stepped aside to let her in. "After everything that happened with the Shadowlands breach, I thought—"

"I know what you thought." She moved past him, her shoulder brushing his arm in the narrow doorway. The contact sent electricity skittering across his skin. "You thought I was avoiding you. That I couldn't handle what happened between us."

"Was I wrong?"

She turned to face him, and the lamplight caught the shadows under her eyes. She looked tired. Worn. But her chin lifted in that stubborn way he'd come to recognize as her armor.

"I was reassigned. Temporarily." Her jaw tightened. "Elder Thorne wanted me away from you while the internal investigation played out. Someone had to take the fall for the breach, and my mother—well, let's just say she has opinions about who that should have been."

Drevik's stomach dropped. "They blamed you?"

"They tried." A ghost of a smile crossed her lips. "I have allies too. Not as many as my mother, but enough. Enough to pull strings and call in favors I've been saving for years."

He studied her face, reading the exhaustion beneath the defiance. "What kind of favors?"

"The kind that got me reassigned." She stepped closer, close enough that he could smell the cold night air clinging to her jacket, mixed with something warm and distinctly her. "As your handler. Officially. Permanently."

The words hung between them, weighted with implication.

"Lexi, I don't—"

"I know what you're going to say." She held up a hand, forestalling his protest. "You don't need a handler. You've proven yourself. You're more powerful than half the Seekers in this chapter, and everyone knows it. But that's exactly why you need someone watching your back."

"Because I'm a target."

"Because you're valuable." Her eyes softened, just slightly. "And because I wasn't going to let anyone else do it."

The admission cost her something. He saw it in the way her shoulders tensed, the way her gaze flickered to the window and back. Lexi Thornwood didn't admit weakness. Didn't ask for permission.

He wanted to kiss her. The realization hit him with the force of shadow-walking—that sudden displacement of reality, the vertigo of wanting something he shouldn't.

"Thank you," he said instead. "For fighting for this."

Something flickered in her expression—surprise, maybe, or relief that he hadn't made it harder. "Don't thank me yet. The regional gathering is tomorrow night, and you're going to need me more than you know."

The Order's regional gatherings were held in rotating locations—always beneath the veneer of mundane society. This month's host was the Blackwood family, whose ancestral estate had been converted into a "private events venue" that catered to the wealthy and oblivious.

Drevik stood in the entryway of the restored Victorian mansion, adjusting the cuffs of the suit Lexi had insisted he wear. The charcoal fabric was expensive—borrowed from another acolyte who owed her a favor—and it fit well enough to make him look like he belonged.

"You clean up nice." Lexi appeared at his elbow, and his breath held.

She'd traded her usual leather for a deep green dress that hugged her athletic frame, leaving her shoulders bare. Her auburn hair was swept up, exposing the curve of her neck. She looked elegant. Dangerous. Utterly untouchable.

"You look incredible," he said, the words escaping before he could filter them.

Her mouth curved. "Eyes forward, Acolyte. We have a job to do."

The interior of the mansion had been transformed. Candles floated in the air—actual floating candles, held aloft by subtle shadow magic—casting flickering light across the assembled crowd. Order members from six regional chapters mingled in clusters, their conversations a low hum of politics and power.

Drevik recognized the dynamics immediately. The way Elders gathered in tight circles, their body language exclusionary. The way Seekers orbited them, angling for promotion. The way acolytes like him kept to the margins, watching and learning.

"Who are we looking for?" he asked, offering his arm.

Lexi took it, her fingers curling around his bicep with a familiarity that felt dangerously natural. "Mira Blackwood. Warden rank, third in her family's hierarchy. She's been flagged by internal investigations as a potential leak."

"Flagged how?"

"Orders from the Shadowlands have been traced back to someone in her network. Not directly to her—she's too careful for that—but close enough to warrant attention." Lexi guided him through the crowd, nodding at faces Drevik didn't recognize. "She's ambitious. Charming. And if she's the traitor, she's been playing this game for years without getting caught."

"How do I approach her?"

"You don't. You let her approach you." Lexi's grip tightened on his arm. "She's competitive, remember. She'll see you as a threat—or an opportunity. Either way, she'll want to figure out which."

They moved deeper into the gathering, and Drevik felt eyes following them. Whispers at the edges of conversations. He was still new enough to be a curiosity—the acolyte who'd survived a Shadowlands breach, who'd displayed power beyond his station.

"Smile," Lexi said. "Look like you belong."

He smiled. It was like wearing a mask.

The ballroom opened before them, a vast space with vaulted ceilings and crystalline chandeliers. Shadow magic shimmered at the edges of his vision—protective wards, surveillance enchantments, the subtle glow of heart bonds linking couples across the room.

"Heart bonds?" Drevik asked quietly.

"Conservative chapters love them. Ensures loyalty, tracks emotional states." Lexi's expression flickered. "My parents have one. My mother uses it to monitor my father's devotion."

The clinical way she said it made his chest ache.

They stopped near a table laden with refreshments, and Lexi pressed a glass of champagne into his hand. "Drink. You look too tense."

"I am too tense."

"Then pretend better." She leaned into him, her shoulder brushing his chest, and lowered her voice. "Eleven o'clock. Dark hair, silver dress, talking to the Elder from the Northern chapter."

Drevik glanced without turning his head. A woman stood near the far wall, her posture languid, her smile sharp. She was beautiful in a calculated way—high cheekbones, dark eyes that missed nothing, a silver gown that caught the candlelight like water.

"Mira Blackwood."

"Watch how she moves through the room. See how everyone she speaks to walks away feeling like they've won something, even when they haven't?"

He tracked her path through the crowd. The way she touched arms, leaned close, laughed at the right moments. She was collecting secrets, he realized. Every conversation was an exchange.

"She knows we're watching," Lexi said. "She's known since we walked in."

As if on cue, Mira's gaze lifted, finding Drevik across the room. Her smile widened, and she began moving toward them with the unhurried grace of a predator who knew her prey wasn't going anywhere.

"Ready?" Lexi asked.

"No."

"Perfect."

Mira Blackwood reached them in twelve steps—Drevik counted—and stopped at a distance that was intimate without being improper. Up close, she was older than she'd appeared from across the room. Late twenties, maybe. Experienced.

"Lexi Thornwood." Mira's voice was honey over glass. "I heard you'd been reassigned. Congratulations on... landing on your feet."

"Mira." Lexi's tone matched hers, sweet with an edge. "The gathering is lovely. Your family outdid themselves."

"We try." Mira's attention shifted to Drevik, and he felt the weight of her assessment. "And this must be the famous acolyte everyone's talking about. The one who walked through a Shadowlands breach and came back with more power than he started with."

"I had good teachers," Drevik said, keeping his voice neutral.

"Did you?" Mira stepped closer, and he caught her scent—jasmine and something darker, something that reminded him of the Shadowlands themselves. "I wonder if that's the whole story."

"Should it be more?"

Her eyes lit with interest. "Direct. I like that." She extended her hand, palm down, as if expecting him to kiss it. "Mira Blackwood. My family has been with the Order for six generations. We don't often see new blood rising so quickly."

Drevik took her hand and shook it, deliberately ignoring the expected gesture. Her smile flickered—annoyance or approval, he couldn't tell which.

"Drevik Markinsoul. I'm still learning the ropes."

"Modest too." She didn't release his hand. Her fingers were cool, her grip firm. "Tell me, Drevik—may I call you Drevik?—what do you think of the Order's current direction?"

"Careful," Lexi said, her voice low. "That's a loaded question."

"Every question is loaded at these gatherings." Mira's thumb traced a circle against Drevik's palm. "I'm simply curious about the perspectives of our newest talent. The Elders can be so... set in their ways. Fresh eyes might see possibilities they've missed."

He understood what she was doing. Testing him. Looking for cracks in his loyalty, or perhaps recruitment opportunities if his ambition matched hers.

"I think the Order exists to protect the balance," he said. "Anything beyond that is above my rank."

Mira laughed—a genuine sound, surprisingly warm. "The diplomatic answer. Smart." She finally released his hand, but her gaze remained fixed on his face. "You'll go far, Drevik. Or you'll burn out trying. The Order has a way of consuming those with too much potential."

"Is that a warning or an offer?"

The question surprised her. He saw it in the slight widening of her eyes, the momentary stillness of her smile.

"Both, perhaps." She stepped back, adjusting her stance. "I'd like to talk more when you're not so... chaperoned. There are conversations worth having, if you're interested."

She turned and walked away without waiting for a response, her silver dress catching the light as she disappeared into the crowd.

"Well," Lexi said, releasing a breath Drevik hadn't realized she was holding. "That was interesting."

"Did I pass?"

"You didn't fail." She took his arm again, her grip tighter than before. "She's careful. Whatever she's hiding, she won't give it up easily."

They moved away from the refreshment table, drifting toward the edge of the ballroom. The crowd thickened around them, faces blurring together—Elders in elaborate robes, Seekers in formal wear, acolytes watching from the shadows.

"There's someone else you need to meet," Lexi said. "Someone who's been watching you all evening."

"Who?"

"Zara Nightshade. Veiled One."

Drevik's stomach tightened. The Veiled Ones were the Order's highest rank—those who'd mastered shadow magic so completely they could exist partially in the Shadowlands without losing themselves. They were legends. Myths.

"You're serious."

"Very." Lexi's expression was unreadable. "She asked about you last month. Before the breach. I've been trying to figure out why."

They climbed a curved staircase to the second floor, where the crowd thinned and the air grew cooler. Shadow magic pooled in the corners here, darker and more concentrated. Drevik felt it pressing against his awareness like a living thing.

A woman waited at the top of the stairs.

She was unlike anyone he'd seen in the Order. Her skin was pale—not the paleness of illness, but the color of moonlight on snow. Her hair was black and cropped short, exposing the elegant lines of her neck and jaw. Her eyes were silver, without iris or pupil, reflecting the candlelight like mirrors.

She wore a simple black dress that absorb the light around it, and when she moved, Drevik saw the Shadowlands moving with her—a flicker of twilight at the edges of her form, a whisper of something vast and eternal.

"Zara Nightshade," Lexi said, her voice carefully neutral. "May I present Acolyte Drevik Markinsoul."

The Veiled One's silver eyes fixed on Drevik, and he felt himself being seen—not just his body, but something deeper. Something that made him want to look away and unable to.

"Markinsoul." Her voice was soft, layered with harmonics that come from somewhere else entirely. "The one who walks between possibilities."

"Excuse me?"

"Your gift." She tilted her head, studying him. "You don't just see shadows. You see what could be. Futures that branch and twist and turn back on themselves. It's rare. Beautiful."

Drevik glanced at Lexi, but her expression gave nothing away.

"I don't understand," he said.

"You will." Zara stepped closer, and the temperature dropped around them. "The Order values power, but it fears vision. Those who can see what's coming are dangerous to those who benefit from things staying the same."

"Is that why you're talking to me?"

"I'm talking to you because I see what you're looking for." Her silver eyes flickered, and for a moment, Drevik could have sworn he saw futures reflected in them—paths branching and dividing, possibilities stretching into darkness. "The traitor. The one who's been feeding information to the Shadowlands."

His breath held. "You know who it is?"

"I know where the trail leads." Her expression remained serene, but something in her voice shifted—a note of warning, perhaps, or regret. "The traitor is protected by someone higher than you can imagine. Be careful who you trust, little Acolyte."

Her gaze moved past him, settling on Lexi with an intensity that made his skin prickle.

"Even her."

The words hung in the cold air, and Drevik felt the world tilt beneath him. He turned to look at Lexi, but her face was a mask—shock and denial and something else, something that might have been fear.

"Zara—"

"The truth has many shadows, Drevik Markinsoul." The Veiled One fade, her form dissolving into twilight at the edges. "Learn to tell them apart before they swallow you whole."

She was gone.

The candlelight returned to normal. The warmth seeped back into the air. But the weight of her words remained, pressing down on Drevik's chest like a stone.

"What was she talking about?" he asked, turning to Lexi fully.

"I don't know." Her voice was steady, but her eyes were too bright. "Veiled Ones speak in riddles. They see futures, not facts. She could have meant anything."

"Did she?"

The question hung between them. Drevik searched her face, looking for the Lexi he'd come to know—the woman who'd fought for him, who'd admitted she wanted to protect him, who'd stood against her own family's politics to be his handler.

He saw all of that. He also saw shadows.

"Drevik." She stepped closer, and her hand found his. Her fingers were cold, trembling slightly. "I have secrets. Everyone in the Order does. But I would never—"

"I know." He squeezed her hand, making a choice he couldn't take back. "I trust you."

Her eyes searched his face, looking for the lie. When she didn't find it, something in her expression cracked—relief and fear and something deeper, something she wasn't ready to name.

"Thank you," she said.

Below them, the gathering continued. Mira Blackwood moved through the crowd, collecting secrets. Zara Nightshade's warning echoed in the shadows. And somewhere in the maze of politics and power, a traitor watched and waited.

But for this moment, in the cold air at the top of the stairs, Drevik held Lexi's hand and chose to believe.

The night was far from over.

Chapter 16: The Setup

Chapter 16: The Setup

The trap was elegant. Drevik had to give them that much.

He'd followed Mira for three days, tracking her movements through the compound, noting the way she slip into restricted areas with the casual ease of someone who belonged there. She was good. Too good. Every time he got close to confirming his suspicions, she'd vanish into a shadow corridor or get called away by some convenient emergency.

But today, she'd made a mistake. Or what looked like one.

The door to the archive chamber stood slightly ajar, light spilling through the gap in a way that violated every security protocol the Order maintained. Drevik pressed himself against the corridor wall, listening. Silence. No footsteps, no said incantations, nothing.

He should have known then. He should have recognized the perfection of it—the way everything aligned to draw him forward, the absence of witnesses, the timing that coincided with the Elder council's monthly recess.

Instead, he pushed through the door.

The archive chamber stretched before him, rows of crystalline tablets and bound manuscripts glowing faintly with protective wards. At the center, a reading pedestal held an open tome, its pages illuminated by a focused beam of lunar light from the ceiling. Shadow Communication Protocols, the spine read. Restricted. Eyes Only.

And scattered across the reading table, as if left in haste, were pages of transcribed symbols. Shadowlands entity codes. Contact frequencies. The kind of information that could compromise the entire Order's defensive network.

Drevik's stomach dropped. He stepped forward, reaching to examine the pages more closely—

"Step away from the materials, Acolyte."

The voice came from everywhere at once. Elder Thornwood's voice. Lexi's father.

Drevik froze, his hand hovering inches from the incriminating documents. Slowly, he turned. The chamber's shadows had resolved into figures—three Elders flanking the entrance, their faces grim, their hands already weaving containment wards.

"Elder Thornwood." Drevik kept his voice steady, though his chest tightened against his ribs. "I can explain."

"You can explain to the council." Thornwood's green eyes—so like Lexi's, but colder, harder—bored into him. "You can explain why you're standing over classified materials that detail our entire communication network with the Shadowlands border patrols. You can explain why these documents have been photographed and transmitted to an unknown recipient."

"Photographed?" Drevik's mind raced. "I haven't—"

"Silence." Elder Voss, a sharp-featured woman with silver-streaked hair, stepped forward. Her fingers moved in a complex pattern, and Drevik felt the Oath tattoo on his chest begin to warm. "The binding will determine truth. Resist, and the pain will be... considerable."

The warmth became heat. Drevik gritted his teeth as the Oath's magic activated, probing his memories, his intentions, his very thoughts. It was invasive in a way he'd never experienced—like fingers rifling through the pages of his mind.

"What is your purpose in this chamber, Acolyte Drevik Markinsoul?"

The question burned through him, demanding answer. "I was investigating a suspected traitor. I followed Mira Vance to this location."

Eyes narrowed. Elder Thornwood exchanged glances with Voss.

"Mira Vance," Voss repeated. "The same acolyte who reported suspicious behavior from you three days ago? Who provided testimony that you'd been asking questions about Shadowlands communication protocols?"

The heat intensified. Drevik felt sweat beading on his forehead. "She's lying. She's the one—I saw her accessing restricted areas. I have witnesses who can—"

"Your witnesses." Thornwood's voice cut like a blade. "Would that include the unbound civilian you've been consorting with? The exotic dancer with no connection to our Order? Or perhaps the Thornwood daughter you've been... distracting from her duties?"

The Oath seared. Drevik bit back a cry of pain.

"Answer the question, acolyte." Voss's voice was clinical, detached. "Who are your sources? Who told you to investigate Mira Vance?"

Ivy. Seraphina. Lexi, who'd noticed discrepancies in the patrol reports.

Names rose to his lips, and the Oath pushed them forward, demanding release. But Drevik clamped his jaw shut. If he named them now, without proof, without preparation, they'd be dragged into this. Ivy, with no protection from the Order's justice. Seraphina, whose position as an archivist would crumble. And Lexi—

He couldn't. He wouldn't.

The pain redoubled. Drevik dropped to one knee, gasping.

"You refuse to name your sources." Thornwood's voice held something that might have been disappointment. Or might have been satisfaction. "You stand in a restricted chamber, caught with transmitted classified documents, and you protect those who aided you. The evidence is clear, Acolyte Markinsoul. You've been feeding information to the Shadowlands entities. The question is only—why?"

"No." The word came out strangled. "I would never—I've given everything to this Order."

"Then prove it. Name your accomplices."

Drevik looked up, meeting Thornwood's eyes through the haze of pain. "I work alone."

It was a lie, and the Oath punished him for it. Fire raced through his veins, and he doubled over, hands pressing against the cold stone floor. But he held. Somehow, he held.

"Enough." A new voice—Elder Kade, the oldest of the council, his face weathered by centuries of service. He stepped forward, and his touch cooled the burning in Drevik's chest. "The boy's strong. Stronger than he should be. Killing him with the Oath won't get us answers."

"I'm not trying to kill him," Thornwood said coldly. "I'm trying to get the truth."

"The truth is complicated." Kade's ancient eyes studied Drevik with unsettling intensity. "Take him to the holding cells. Let him consider his position. Perhaps isolation will loosen his tongue."

Hands grabbed Drevik's arms, hauling him upright. He stumbled between two wardens—faces he didn't recognize, their expressions professionally blank—and they dragged him from the chamber.

The corridors blurred past. Drevik's mind churned through possibilities, looking for escape, for explanation, for anything that could change what was happening. But the Oath still throbbed against his chest, and every shadow press in on him, watching, waiting.

They threw him into a cell.

It was small—barely ten feet square—with stone walls that drank the light and a floor that chilled through his boots. The door slammed shut behind him, and he heard wards activating, sealing him in with magic as much as iron.

Drevik slumped against the far wall and let the trembling take him.

Framed. Someone had framed him.

The realization crystallized with horrible clarity. Mira had been setting him up from the beginning. The suspicious behavior, the trail he'd been meant to follow, the conveniently open door. She'd known he'd investigate. She'd known he'd walk right into it.

And now he was trapped.

Time passed strangely in the cell. Without windows, without any connection to the outside world, Drevik had only the growing ache in his chest and the racing of his own thoughts. Hours might have passed. Or minutes. Or days.

He dozed at some point, drifting in and out of uneasy dreams. His grandmother's face swam before him, disappointment in her eyes. His parents—faces he'd never known—hovered at the edges of his vision, always just out of reach.

When the door opened, he jerked awake, heart pounding.

But it wasn't the Elders. It wasn't wardens come to drag him to judgment.

It was Lexi.

She slipped through the door quickly, closing it behind her, and for a moment she just stood there, looking at him. Her auburn hair was pulled back severely, her uniform crisp and proper, but her eyes—

Her eyes were red-rimmed. She'd been crying.

"You idiot." The words came out thick, unsteady. "You absolute idiot."

Drevik pushed himself to his feet, swaying slightly. "Lexi. You shouldn't be here."

"Shouldn't be—" She crossed the cell in three quick strides and grabbed his collar, yanking him close. "My father is calling for your expulsion. For your unmaking. And you're telling me I shouldn't be here?"

"Your father." Drevik's voice cracked. "He's one of the ones who—"

"He's doing his duty." Lexi's grip tightened. "The evidence, Drevik. The photographs. They show transmissions from your personal terminal to an encrypted address in the Shadowlands. They show access logs with your credentials entering restricted archives a dozen times over the past month."

"Those are forged."

"Of course they're forged!" She released him, stepping back, running a hand through her hair in frustration. "I know they're forged. I know you. You'd never betray the Order—you'd never betray me."

The conviction in her voice cut through the fog of despair. Drevik stared at her, seeing the fear beneath the anger, the love beneath the frustration.

"Then help me," he said. "Tell them—"

"I tried." Lexi's voice dropped. "I stood before the council and told them you were with me the night of the supposed transmissions. I told them your credentials must have been stolen. They said—" She swallowed. "They said I was compromised. That you'd seduced me into covering for you."

Seduced. The word hung between them, weighted with everything that had passed in her apartment, everything they'd shared.

"Lexi." Drevik reached for her, then stopped, his hand hovering in the air. "If you keep defending me, they'll—"

"I don't care." She stepped into his space, catching his hand and pressing it to her cheek. Her skin was warm, damp with tears she'd clearly been fighting. "I don't care what they do to me. I'm not losing you."

"You could lose everything." His thumb traced the line of her jaw, feeling the tension in her muscles. "Your position. Your family. Your—"

"My family." Lexi laughed bitterly. "My father looked at me like I was a stranger. Like everything I've worked for, everything I've sacrificed, meant nothing compared to my feelings for you." Her eyes met his, fierce and bright. "He's wrong. You mean more."

Drevik's chest tightened—not the Oath this time, but something deeper. Something that had been growing since that first sparring match, since the first time she'd looked at him and seen something other than a threat.

"I need you to leave." The words scraped against his throat. "You need to distance yourself from me. If you're there when they question me again, they'll—"

"I'm not leaving." Lexi's hands found his chest, pressing flat over his heart. "I don't care what they say. I don't care what evidence they've manufactured. I know you. And I know you'd rather die than betray this Order."

"I would." Drevik covered her hands with his own. "But I can't prove that. I can't explain my sources without dragging—"

"Ivy." Lexi's voice softened. "Seraphina. I know."

Drevik stilled. "How—"

"I'm not an idiot." A hint of her usual fire returned to her eyes. "I've seen the way you look at them. The way they look at you. And I know they've been helping you investigate."

"If your father finds out—"

"My father already suspects." She stepped closer, eliminating the last distance between them. "He asked me directly if you were involved with the unbound girl. I told him she was a friend from your college classes. That you helped her with her studies." A bitter smile. "He didn't believe me."

Drevik closed his eyes, feeling the warmth of her body against his, the steadiness of her presence. In this cold cell, with his world crumbling around him, she was the only solid thing.

"Lexi." Her name came out like a prayer. "I'm so sorry."

"For what?" Her fingers curled into his shirt. "For being targeted? For fighting for the truth?"

"For dragging you into this. For making you choose."

"Choose?" She laughed softly, and the sound held no bitterness this time—only wonder. "Drevik, I chose you the moment I realized you weren't what I expected. Everything since then has just been... confirming what I already knew."

He opened his eyes. Her face was inches from his, her green eyes bright with unshed tears, her lips parted slightly. She was beautiful in a way that made his chest ache—not just her features, but the strength in them, the courage.

"I don't deserve you," he said.

"Probably not." Her smile was tremulous. "But you're stuck with me anyway."

She kissed him.

It wasn't like the first time—urgent, desperate, a claiming. This kiss was slower, deeper, weighted with everything they couldn't say. Her lips moved against his with a tenderness that broke something open in his chest. Drevik's hands found her waist, pulling her closer, and she melted against him, her body fitting into his like she belonged there.

For a long moment, the cell, the accusations, the trap—all of it faded. There was only Lexi. The scent of her hair, something floral and clean. The warmth of her body pressed against his. The soft sounds she made as the kiss deepened, her fingers threading through his hair.

When she finally pulled back, her breathing was unsteady. Her forehead rested against his, her eyes still closed.

"I'm scared," she said. "I've never been scared of anything in my life. But the thought of losing you—"

"You won't." Drevik's voice was rough. "I'll find a way out of this."

"The evidence—"

"Is forged. Which means there's a trail. I just need to find it."

Lexi's eyes opened. "The council is meeting tomorrow morning. They'll decide your fate then." Her hands slid to his chest, pressing flat over his heart. "My father is pushing for unmaking. Elder Kade wants to wait. But if they vote to strip your magic—"

"They won't."

"Drevik—"

"I said they won't." He cupped her face, tilting it up to meet his gaze. "I didn't survive twenty-one years alone just to be taken down by a frame job. I didn't find this Order, find you, just to lose it all because Mira Vance is better at politics than I am."

Lexi's mouth curved. "That's the Drevik I know."

"Get out of here." He pressed a kiss to her forehead. "Before someone notices you're gone."

"I don't care if—"

"I know." He smiled, and it felt strange on his face after so many hours of despair. "But I need you outside. I need someone who believes me on the other side of these walls."

She studied him for a long moment, then nodded. Stepped back. Straightened her uniform.

"I'll find something." Her voice was fierce. "There's a hole in their evidence somewhere. I just need to find it."

"Be careful."

"Always." She moved to the door, then paused, looking back. "Drevik?"

"Yes?"

"I love you."

The words hung in the air, simple and devastating. She was gone before he could respond, the door sealing shut behind her with a whisper of wards.

Drevik slid down the wall, his legs suddenly unable to support him. His hand pressed against his chest, over the spot where the Oath still throbbed faintly.

She loved him.

The thought should have brought joy. Instead, it brought terror. Because if she loved him, she would put herself in danger to save him. She would sacrifice everything she'd worked for, everything she'd built, on the chance that she could prove his innocence.

And if she failed, they would burn for it together.

The next hours were the longest of Drevik's life.

He paced. He sat. He tried to meditate, to access the strange future-sight that had manifested during his testing, but the wards dampened his magic, leaving him frustratingly mortal. He examined the cell, looking for weakness, but the stone was seamless and the wards were flawless.

He thought about Lexi's confession. About the way she'd said it—matter-of-fact, almost defiant, as if daring him to reject her. As if there was any universe where he could.

He thought about Ivy, alone in the outside world, probably hearing rumors about his arrest. About Seraphina, in the archives, surrounded by the very documents that had been used to frame him.

He thought about his parents. About the mystery that had drawn him to the Order in the first place. About the journal his father had left, hidden somewhere in this compound, waiting to be found.

The journal.

Drevik straightened, a thought forming. His father had been a member of the Order—his mother too, though he'd only learned that recently. They'd left him clues, breadcrumbs leading to the truth of their disappearance. What if—

The door opened.

Drevik rose, expecting wardens, expecting Elders. Instead, a small figure slipped through—barely five feet tall, with close-cropped dark hair and a round face that looked far too innocent for someone who moved like a shadow.

"Ivy?"

"Shh." She pressed a finger to her lips, her eyes darting around the cell. "I've got thirty seconds before the ward rotation. Listen carefully."

"How did you—"

"Doesn't matter." She crossed to him, pulling something from her jacket—a small folded paper. "Lexi told me what happened. She got word to me through Seraphina."

"Ivy, you can't be here. If they catch you—"

"They won't." Her voice was steady, but her hands trembled slightly as she pressed the paper into his palm. "I've been doing my own investigating. Through non-Order channels. And I found something."

Drevik unfolded the paper. It was a copy of a journal page, written in a cramped, hurried hand he didn't recognize. But the signature at the bottom—

"Your father's journal," Ivy said. "I couldn't get the whole thing—it's warded too heavily. But I found fragments. Copies that someone made before the original was sealed."

Drevik's eyes scanned the page. Dates. Coordinates. And a series of symbols that made no sense—until he looked at them from the corner of his eye, letting his Veil Sight activate.

The symbols shifted, resolved into words.

The cipher is in the last five entries. The traitor's name is hidden there. Trust only those who bear the mark of the eclipse.

"I don't understand," Drevik said. "Where is the original journal?"

"Archives. Restricted section. The same section you were supposedly caught stealing from." Ivy's voice was grim. "Which means whoever framed you knows about the journal. Knows it could clear your name."

"Or incriminate them."

"Exactly." She squeezed his arm. "The council meets in six hours. If you can get to that journal, find the cipher, decode those last five entries—you might have a chance."

"I'm locked in a cell, Ivy."

"Lexi's working on it." Ivy's eyes met his, filled with a determination that surprised him. "She's not going to let you die in here, Drevik. None of us are."

"Ivy—"

"Save it." She stepped back, moving toward the door. "We all chose to be part of this. Whatever happens next, we chose it."

She was gone before he could respond, slipping through the door just as the wards flickered through their rotation.

Drevik was alone again.

But now he had a direction. Now he had a purpose.

The journal. His father's last five entries. A cipher that could prove his innocence.

He just had to figure out how to escape an inescapable cell, break into the most restricted section of the archives, and find a document that someone had already killed to protect.

Drevik looked at the paper in his hand, at his father's signature, at the hidden message that shifted beneath the ink.

The traitor's name is hidden there.

He thought of Mira, of her too-innocent smile, of the way she'd anticipated his every move. Of the evidence that had appeared like magic, perfectly placed to destroy him.

Someone in the Order was working with the Shadowlands entities. Someone had killed his parents to protect their secret. And now that same someone was trying to silence him.

Drevik folded the paper carefully and slipped it into his boot.

Six hours.

He had six hours to prove his innocence, expose a traitor, and stay alive long enough to tell Lexi that he loved her too.

The trap had been elegant. But they'd made one mistake.

They'd left him alive.

And Drevik Markinsoul was done being a victim.

End of Chapter 16

Chapter 17: Escape and Proof

Chapter 17: Escape and Proof

The confinement cell had no windows, no clocks, nothing to mark the passage of time except the slow fade of the suppressor runes etched into the walls. Drevik had counted the cracks in the stone ceiling forty-seven times when the shadows in the corner rippled.

He was on his feet before Lexi fully materialized, her form bleeding out of the darkness like ink spreading through water.

"You're late," he said.

"Traffic was terrible." She stepped closer, and even in the dim light, he saw the tension in her jaw, the way her hands trembled slightly at her sides. "We have exactly eleven minutes before the next patrol rotation. Can you shadow-walk in your condition?"

He tested his connection to the shadows—the suppressor runes made it feel like reaching through mud, but his core still pulsed with power. "I can manage. What's the plan?"

"I told Elder Sevestian I spotted an intruder near the eastern perimeter. He's pulling every available Warden to investigate." A ghost of a smile crossed her lips. "You're welcome."

"Lexi, if they catch you—"

"They won't." She moved closer, close enough that he could smell the night air clinging to her skin, the faint trace of jasmine from whatever soap she used. "I've been planning this for three days, Drevik. While you've been counting ceiling cracks, I've been memorizing patrol routes, bribing kitchen staff, and forging documents. You're not the only one who can be clever."

Three days. He'd lost track somewhere after the second interrogation, after Warden Blackwood's questions had circled around his father like a vulture waiting for a dying animal to stop twitching. Tell me about the journal, acolyte. What did Marcus Markinsoul hide in his final entries? Who did he trust?

They'd taken everything from him—his communication stone, his student ID, the small knife he kept in his boot. But they hadn't found the journal. He'd hidden it too well for that.

"The journal's in my quarters," he said. "Behind the loose stone near the window, the one with the crack running through it."

"I know. I checked." Her eyes met his, and something flickered there—something that looked almost like hurt. "Why didn't you tell me about your father's research? About what he was investigating before he died?"

"Because I didn't trust you."

The words hung between them. Lexi's expression didn't change, but her shadows writhed at her feet, agitated.

"And now?" she asked.

"Now I'm beginning to think I was an idiot."

"You were." She stepped back, creating distance, and the cold that rushed into the space between them was like a physical thing. "We need to move. Follow my shadow and stay quiet."

She dissolved into darkness, and Drevik reached for his own power, letting it wrap around him like a familiar cloak. The suppressor runes screamed in protest, but he pushed through, following the thread of Lexi's essence through the twisting paths of shadow-walking.

They emerged in a supply closet on the third floor of the acolyte dormitory. Drevik pressed his ear to the door, hearing nothing but the distant hum of the building's old heating system.

"Clear," he said.

Lexi was already moving, her body low, her footsteps silent on the worn carpet. She moved like someone who had done this a hundred times—like someone born to the shadows in ways Drevik was only beginning to understand.

His quarters were three corridors away. Three corridors that could be filled with Wardens at any moment, every shadow a potential threat.

They made it through the first without incident.

The second corridor brought voices—two acolytes arguing about something mundane, their words echoing off the stone walls. Lexi pulled Drevik into an alcove, their bodies pressed together in the narrow space.

Her breath was warm against his neck. Her hand rested on his chest, feeling the rapid beat of his heart.

"Relax," she said. "They're just passing by."

"I am relaxed."

"You're vibrating." Her lips twitched. "Your heart's trying to escape your ribs."

"Maybe I'm just excited to be this close to you."

The words slipped out before he could stop them. Lexi's eyes widened slightly, and for a moment, the fierce, controlled mask she wore cracked. He saw the girl beneath—the one who had been watching him for months, the one who had fought beside him and trained him and pushed him harder than anyone else.

"Drevik—"

The voices faded. The corridor fell silent.

She pulled away first, her expression smoothing back into neutral territory. "We should keep moving."

They reached his quarters without further incident. The door was still sealed with the Order's confinement ward, a shimmering barrier that would trigger an alarm if breached by anyone but a Warden.

"Can you break it?" Lexi asked.

Drevik studied the ward's structure, tracing the threads of power woven into the barrier. It was complex, but he saw the anchor point—a small knot of energy near the lock, tied to the suppressor runes throughout the building.

"I don't need to break it." He placed his hand against the door. "I just need to convince it I'm supposed to be here."

His father had taught him that much, in the months before his death. Wards are like people, Drevik. They have patterns, habits. Learn to speak their language, and they'll open any door for you.

The barrier shivered, recognized something in his touch, and dissolved.

"That's new," Lexi said. "I've never seen anyone talk down a confinement ward before."

"It's a family trick." He pushed the door open and slipped inside.

His room was exactly as he'd left it—the narrow bed with its standard-issue gray sheets, the desk covered in textbooks and notes, the small window overlooking the eastern courtyard. Nothing had been disturbed.

Which meant they hadn't found the journal.

Drevik crossed to the window, running his fingers along the stone frame until he found the crack Lexi had mentioned. The loose stone came away easily, revealing the hollow space behind it.

The leather-bound journal was still there, its cover worn soft by years of handling. Marcus Markinsoul's final research, hidden in a space his son had carved out when he was twelve years old.

"I found it," he said, turning to show Lexi—

And froze.

The shadows in the corner of his room were moving. Not the gentle ripple of shadow-walking, but something darker, more violent. They coalesced into shapes—vaguely humanoid, with too-long limbs and faces that were nothing but gaping voids.

"Lexi, get back—"
