The first shadow creature lunged.

Drevik threw himself sideways, feeling the brush of something cold and wrong against his shoulder. The thing's touch left frost on his shirt, ice spreading through the fabric.

"What are these?" Lexi shouted, her hands wreathed in shadow as she prepared to fight.

"Shadowstalkers." Drevik scrambled to his feet, the journal clutched against his chest. "They're from the Shadowlands. Someone summoned them."

Shadowstalkers were assassins—the Order used them rarely, only for executions, only for targets deemed too dangerous to approach directly. The fact that someone had sent them meant his death had been ordered.

By someone with the authority to command entities from the Shadowlands.

A Warden. Or higher.

The second creature attacked from the left. Drevik's foresight kicked in—a flash of possible futures, each one showing a different angle of the strike. He dodged left instead of right, feeling the creature's claws pass through the space where his throat would have been.

"There are too many," Lexi gasped. Four of them now, spreading through the room, cutting off the door. "We can't fight them in here."

"The window." Drevik grabbed her arm, pulling her toward the small opening. "Can you shadow-walk us both?"

"From inside a warded building? Not reliably."

"Then we go out the hard way."

He didn't give her time to argue. The journal went into his jacket, pressed flat against his ribs, and then he was throwing himself at the window, leading with his shoulder.

Glass shattered. Cold air rushed in. They fell.

Drevik reached for his power, wrapping it around both of them, turning the fall into something almost like flight. They hit the courtyard grass rolling, and behind them, the shadow creatures poured through the broken window like smoke from a fire.

"Run," Lexi snarled, pulling him to his feet. "I'll hold them here."

"Like hell."

"Drevik, you have the journal. That's proof of everything your father found. If you die here, his work dies with you." Her eyes were fierce, bright with something that looked almost like tears. "Go. Get it to Elder Sevestian. I'll catch up."

The first Shadowstalker landed between them, its void-face turning toward Lexi. Drevik didn't think—he moved. His power surged, and he shadow-walked the three steps needed to place himself at Lexi's side.

"I told you," he said, his voice raw, "I'm done doing things alone."

She looked at him, really looked at him, and the Shadowstalkers closed in.

They fought together.

It shouldn't have worked—Drevik was still an acolyte, his training incomplete, and Lexi was exhausted from the effort of breaking him out of confinement. But there was a rhythm to it, a synchronization that felt less like practice and more like instinct.

Lexi struck high while Drevik swept low. She created openings with her shadows, and he filled them with precise strikes. When a creature came at Drevik from behind, Lexi was already there, her blade of solidified darkness cutting through its form.

It was like fighting with an extension of himself.

"Left," she called, and he was already moving, his foresight showing him the attack a second before it came. "Two more incoming."

The Shadowstalkers weren't endless, but they were relentless. Every time Drevik dissolved one into mist, another take its place. The cold was seeping into his bones, making his movements sluggish, slowing his reactions.

"Drevik!" Lexi's voice cut through the haze. "Your power—use it to track the summoning anchor."

Right. Shadowstalkers didn't just appear—they had to be anchored to the mortal realm by a summoner's will. If he could find that thread and sever it—

He reached for his foresight, pushing past the immediate futures to the deeper currents beneath. There. A thin line of power running from the largest Shadowstalker, threading back toward the dormitory, toward—

His breath held.

The thread led to Warden Blackwood's office.

"The summoner is Corvin Blackwood," he gasped. "Lexi, the Warden who interrogated me—he's the one who—"

A Shadowstalker's claw caught him across the ribs, and pain exploded through his side. He staggered, his concentration breaking, and the futures scattered like leaves in a storm.

"Drevik!" Lexi was there, her body between his and the creatures, her shadows forming a protective barrier. "Stay with me."

"I'm fine." He wasn't fine. Blood was soaking through his shirt, and his vision was starting to blur at the edges. "The anchor—I can break it, but I need time."

"Then take it." She planted her feet, and her shadows exploded outward, forming a wall of darkness between them and the Shadowstalkers. "I'll hold them. Do what you need to do."

He wanted to argue. He wanted to pull her back, to protect her the way she was protecting him. But the Shadowstalkers were pressing against her barrier, their void-faces pressing through like fingers through wet paper, and he knew he didn't have time for pride.

Drevik closed his eyes and reached for the thread.

It was slippery, coated in something that was like oil and wrongness. Blackwood's will held it tight, but Drevik felt the places where it frayed—the corners the older man hadn't bothered to reinforce because he'd never expected anyone to look.

Arrogant, Drevik thought. You sent these things to kill an acolyte, and you didn't even bother to do it properly.

He wrapped his own power around the thread and pulled.

The backlash was immediate and violent. Drevik's body arched as energy surged through him—Blackwood's will meeting his, the Warden's power crushing down on him like a physical weight. For a moment, he felt the older man's surprise, his fury, his cold calculation as he realized what was happening.

And then he felt something else: recognition.

Marcus's boy, came the thought, pressing through the connection like a knife. You have your father's eyes. And his foolishness.

The thread snapped.

Drevik crashed to the ground, his body screaming in protest, every nerve ending on fire. The Shadowstalkers dissolved instantly, their forms collapsing into wisps of darkness that scattered on the wind.

Silence.

"Drevik?" Lexi was at his side, her hands pressing against his wound, her face pale. "Stay awake. Look at me."

He looked at her. Her green eyes were bright with unshed tears, her auburn hair coming loose from its braid, and there was a cut across her forehead where something had grazed her. She looked fierce and fragile and terrified all at once.

"I told you," he managed, his voice rough, "I learned from you."

"What?"

"Don't do anything stupid." He tried to smile, but his face didn't seem to want to cooperate. "That's what you always tell me."

Her laugh was half a sob. "You absolute idiot." Her hands pressed harder against his side. "You could have died. You almost died. For a journal?"

"For my father's work." He reached up, his hand shaking, and touched the cut on her forehead. "You're hurt."

"I'm fine. You're the one bleeding out in a courtyard."

"I'm not bleeding out." He wasn't, probably. The wound was deep, but it had missed anything vital. He'd had worse in training accidents. "The journal's safe. That's what matters."

"That's not—" She broke off, her jaw tightening. "You matter, Drevik. You stubborn, reckless, infuriating man. You matter."

There was something in her voice that made the pain recede, just a little. Something that made the cold night air feel warmer.

"Lexi—"

"I'm going to kill whoever did this." Her voice had gone flat, dangerous. "Blackwood. You said it was Blackwood."

"He's the one who summoned the Shadowstalkers." Drevik tried to sit up, winced, and let Lexi push him back down. "He interrogated me. He asked about the journal, about my father's research. He wanted to know what I knew."

"Because your father found something." Lexi's hands were gentle despite the pressure she was keeping on his wound. "Something dangerous enough to kill for."

"Let's find out."

He pulled the journal from his jacket, wincing at the movement. The leather was stained with his blood now, the cover slick under his fingers. But the pages inside were still intact.

"We need somewhere safe," Lexi said. "Somewhere the Wardens won't look."

"The old archives." Drevik had found the abandoned section during his first month, when he'd been exploring the compound's forgotten corners. "No one goes there anymore. The preservation wards are still active, but the location isn't monitored."

Lexi nodded, her expression grim. "Can you walk?"

"With help."

She pulled his arm over her shoulder, taking most of his weight as they moved toward the shadows at the courtyard's edge. Her body was warm against his, solid and real, and despite everything—the pain, the blood loss, the creatures that had just tried to kill him—Drevik felt something shift between them.

She'd come for him. She'd risked everything—her position, her safety, her family's standing in the Order—to break him out of confinement. And then she'd stood between him and death without hesitation.

"I owe you," he said as they moved through the darkness. "For all of this."

"You don't owe me anything." Her voice was soft, barely audible. "I did it because I couldn't—because watching them take you was—"

She didn't finish. She didn't need to.

The old archives were exactly as Drevik remembered: dusty, forgotten, and utterly silent. The preservation wards hummed at a frequency just below hearing, keeping the worst of the decay at bay. They found a corner hidden behind collapsed shelves, and Lexi helped him settle against the wall before taking position by the entrance, her shadows spreading out to warn of any approaching threat.

Drevik opened the journal with shaking hands.

His father's handwriting covered the pages—familiar strokes that brought a lump to Drevik's throat. He'd avoided reading the final entries for months, unable to face the words Marcus Markinsoul had written in the days leading up to his death.

But now, with blood still seeping through his shirt and Lexi standing guard in the darkness, he turned to the last pages.

The pattern is clear now, his father had written. The breaches aren't random. Someone in the Order is deliberately weakening the Veil, allowing Shadowlands entities through for study. Or worse—for power.

Three Wardens have access to the necessary protocols. Three have the authority to suppress investigation. I've tracked the authorization seals to a single source, but I need more proof before I can bring this to the Veiled Ones.

The summoning rituals match ancient texts—pre-Order texts. Someone isn't just using Shadowlands creatures. They're trying to bring something through. Something that was banished centuries ago.

If you're reading this and I'm gone, trust no one above the rank of Warden. The corruption goes deep. The evidence is in the eastern archive, behind the Veiled One's seal. Use the cipher below to decode the authorization trails.

And Drevik—if you've found this, I'm sorry I couldn't be there to teach you what you need to know. Your mother and I always knew you'd be special. The bloodline runs strong in you, son. Stronger than it ever did in me.

Be careful. Trust your instincts. And remember: the shadows aren't just our tools. They're our responsibility.

—M

Below the signature was a string of characters—a cipher that Drevik recognized from the codes his father had taught him as a child. He could decode it, but it would take time.

Time they didn't have.

"Lexi." He looked up, finding her watching him from across the room. "My father found evidence of corruption. Someone in the Order is deliberately weakening the Veil, letting Shadowlands entities through. And they killed him to keep it secret."

"The Shadowstalkers."

"Sent by Blackwood. But there's more." Drevik turned the page, showing her the cipher. "This will tell us who else is involved. Who's behind everything. But I need to decode it first."

"Then decode it." She moved to his side, settling next to him against the wall. "I'll keep watch."

He should have argued. He should have told her to rest, to let him handle it, to stop taking care of him when she'd already done so much. But his body was heavy with exhaustion, and her presence was warm at his side, and he found himself leaning into her as he worked.

The cipher was complex but not impossible. His father had used a variant of an old Order code, one that Drevik had learned in his first year of training. Letter by letter, he translated the string into something readable.

Authorization source: WB

Co-conspirators: RV, STH, MK (deceased)

Target: Veiled One ascension

Next breach: 3 days after next new moon

Entity designation: THE HOLLOW KING

Drevik's blood ran cold.

"WB," Lexi said, reading over his shoulder. "Warden Blackwood."

"And RV, STH..." Drevik's mind raced through the Order's hierarchy. "Ruth Vane, the head of acolyte training. Samuel Thorn—"

"Thornwood." Lexi's voice was hollow. "That's my mother's maiden name. She was a Thornwood before she married my father."

The implications hung between them. If Lexi's maternal family was involved in the conspiracy...

"My father," she said. "He'd know. If Uncle Samuel was part of this, my father would have known."

"Maybe he didn't." Drevik reached for her hand, his fingers finding hers in the darkness. "Maybe he was kept in the dark like everyone else."

"Or maybe he's part of it too." Her grip tightened on his, almost painful. "Maybe this whole time, when he was telling me to stay away from you, to report on you, to keep my distance—he was protecting the conspiracy, not the Order."

The pain in her voice was palpable. Drevik pulled her closer, ignoring the protest from his wounded side, and let her press her face against his shoulder.

"We'll find out," he said. "We have proof now. We take this to the Veiled Ones, and—"

"And what? Hope they believe an acolyte and a disgraced Seeker over three Wardens?" Her laugh was bitter. "Drevik, the moment we show this to anyone, Blackwood will know. He'll have us killed before we can say a word."

"Then we find another way." He turned to face her, his free hand coming up to cup her jaw. "Lexi, look at me."

She looked up, her green eyes bright with unshed tears.

"I've been alone my whole life," he said. "Even when my father was alive, I knew he was hiding things from me. I learned to rely on myself, to trust no one, to keep everyone at arm's length. But you—" He stopped, swallowed hard. "You broke through that. You forced your way in and refused to leave, and I don't want to do this alone anymore. Whatever happens next, I want you by my side."

Her breath held. "Drevik—"

"I know this is complicated. I know your family might be involved, and I know you're risking everything by helping me. But I need you to know that—" He stopped, the words catching in his throat. "That I—"

She kissed him.

It was nothing like the first time—nothing like that careful, tentative exploration in the training room. This was desperate and raw, her hands fisting in his hair, his grip pulling her closer despite the pain in his side. She tasted like night air and adrenaline, and she kissed him like she was drowning and he was the only air in the world.

When they finally broke apart, both of them were breathing hard.

"I thought I told you not to do anything stupid," she said against his lips.

"I learned from you."

Her laugh was shaky, but real. "We're going to figure this out. Together."

"Together."

The word felt strange on his tongue—foreign, unfamiliar, but right in a way that made his chest ache. For the first time since his father's death, Drevik felt something other than alone.

But the journal was still open in his lap, and the cipher still glared up at him from the page. WB. Next breach: 3 days after next new moon. Entity designation: THE HOLLOW KING.

They had proof now. They had names.

And somewhere in the compound, Warden Corvin Blackwood was learning that his Shadowstalkers had failed.

The game had changed.

And Drevik was finally ready to play.

Chapter 18: Alliance and Assault

Chapter 18: Alliance and Assault

The vision struck Drevik like a blade between the ribs.

He staggered against the brick wall of the alley, gasping as possibilities cascaded through his mind—dozens of futures branching from this moment, each one a fragment of what could be. In some, he watched Elder Thornwood fall to Corvin's shadow blade. In others, Lexi bled out on the compound floor, her green eyes going dull. In one particularly vicious thread, he stood alone in the aftermath, everyone he cared about scattered like broken dolls around him.

"Drevik!" Lexi's hand gripped his shoulder, anchoring him to the present. "What do you see?"

He blinked, the futures fading like morning mist. His hands were shaking. They'd been shaking since they'd fled the warehouse with the evidence that could bring Corvin down—scrolls detailing his dealings with Shadowlands entities, names of Order members he'd corrupted, dates and locations of planned breaches.

"The compound's on lockdown," he managed. "Corvin's people control the eastern entrance. The western approach is mined with shadow-traps." He swallowed hard. "And Elder Thornwood is running out of time."

Lexi's jaw tightened. She looked as ragged as he felt—auburn hair escaping her practical braid, a cut above her eyebrow crusted with dried blood from their escape. But her eyes remained fierce, determined. She'd been the one to pull him through the warehouse window when Corvin's men cornered them. She'd killed two shadow-walkers in the process.

"Then we find another way in," she said.

"There is no other way in." Ivy's voice came from behind them, and Drevik turned to find the willowy blonde emerging from the shadows of the alley. She clutched a leather satchel to her chest, her pale face drawn tight with anxiety. "I've been watching the compound for hours. Every entrance is either sealed or guarded by Corvin's people."

"Ivy." Relief flooded through him at the sight of her. She'd separated from them at the warehouse, taking a different route to avoid the pursuit that had chased Drevik and Lexi. "You made it."

"Barely." She moved closer, and he caught the faint scent of lavender beneath the metallic tang of fear. "They're hunting anyone loyal to Elder Thornwood. Three seekers were dragged from their quarters an hour ago. I don't know if they're still alive."

Lexi moved to stand beside Drevik, her shoulder brushing his in a gesture that was both protective and possessive. "How many are left? People we can trust?"

"A handful, maybe. Most are too afraid to move. Corvin's been planning this for months—he's already turned half the Wardens to his side." Ivy's voice cracked. "Drevik, if we go in there, we might be walking into a tomb."

Before he could respond, the temperature in the alley dropped. Shadows pooled in the corner, darker than the night around them, and Drevik felt the familiar pull of someone shadow-walking nearby. His hand went to the knife at his belt, but Lexi was faster, positioning herself between him and the emerging figure.

The shadows parted like a curtain.

Seraphina Vane stepped into the dim light of the alley.

She looked like she'd been through a war. Her usually immaculate dark hair hung loose around her shoulders, tangled and streaked with something that might have been ash. A tear in her fitted jacket revealed an angry red scratch across her ribs, and she moved with a slight limp that she was clearly trying to hide. But her dark eyes were sharp as ever, and when they met Drevik's, something flickered there—something that looked almost like relief.

"Seraphina." Her name left his mouth before he could stop it.

"Did you miss me?" A ghost of her usual smirk crossed her face, but it didn't reach her eyes. "You look terrible, by the way."

"You disappeared." He stepped forward, ignoring Lexi's warning growl. "After the warehouse, you just—"

"I know what I did." Seraphina's voice hardened. "I had to make sure Corvin didn't have eyes on me before I could bring you this."

She reached into her jacket and withdrew a folded parchment, sealed with black wax. The seal of the Shadowlands.

"What is it?" Ivy moved closer, her curiosity overriding her caution.

"Insurance." Seraphina held it out to Drevik, her fingers brushing his as he took it. The contact sent a spark through him—her touch was cold, but the look in her eyes was anything but. "Corvin's been communicating with something in the Shadowlands. A void walker. It's been feeding him information about the Order's weaknesses in exchange for... well. You can read it yourself."

Drevik broke the seal and scanned the contents. His stomach turned.

Names. Dates. The locations of shadow-breaches that Corvin had orchestrated—each one timed to weaken Elder Thornwood's position, to make it seem like the old man was losing his grip on the Order's power. And at the bottom, a signature that made his blood run cold: a mark that belonged to something that had no business existing in either realm.

"This is enough to hang him," Lexi said, reading over his shoulder. Her body pressed against his back, warm and solid. "If we can get this to your father—"

"My father is exactly the problem." Seraphina's voice dripped with bitterness. "He's been helping Corvin. Not willingly—I'm sure Corvin has something on him—but that doesn't change the facts. We can't trust any of the Elders except Thornwood."

"Then we get to Thornwood." Drevik folded the parchment and tucked it into his jacket. "Tonight. Before Corvin makes his move."

"And how do you propose we do that?" Lexi crossed her arms, fixing Seraphina with a glare that could have frozen fire. "In case you haven't noticed, the compound is locked down. Corvin's people are crawling over every entrance."

Seraphina's mouth curved. "That's why I brought the map."

She withdrew another parchment from her jacket—this one covered in intricate lines and symbols. A layout of the compound, but with passages Drevik had never seen before.

"Ventilation shafts," Seraphina explained, tracing a thin line with her finger. "They run through the entire compound. Most of them are too small for shadow-walking, but there's a maintenance corridor here—" she tapped a section near the eastern wing "—that connects to the Elder's private chambers. It hasn't been used in decades. Corvin won't have it guarded."

Ivy leaned in, her brow furrowing. "How did you get this?"

"I have my sources." Seraphina's smile didn't reach her eyes. "Let's just say my father's position has certain advantages."

The tension in the alley was palpable. Lexi hadn't moved from her protective stance beside Drevik, and Seraphina take pleasure in that fact, deliberately stepping closer to him when she spoke. Ivy watched the exchange with careful eyes, her fingers twitching toward the satchel she carried.

Three women. Three very different connections to him.

And right now, all three of them were looking at him like he held the answers.

"Okay," Drevik said, forcing his voice to remain steady. "Here's what we're going to do."

The planning session took place in Ivy's apartment—a cramped space above a bookstore that served as her unofficial sanctuary. The walls were lined with books and maps, and the air smelled of old paper and the herbs Ivy used in her minor workings.

Drevik spread the materials across her table: Seraphina's map, the evidence from the warehouse, the Shadowlands parchment. Four people against an entire compound of corrupted Order members. The odds were so bad they would have been laughable if they weren't so terrifying.

"Ivy, you'll take the eastern approach," Drevik said, pointing to a section of the map. "There's a shadow-trap here, here, and here. Can you disable them?"

Ivy nodded, her fingers tracing the positions. "Give me twenty minutes. I've been studying the new trap configurations—Corvin changed the patterns, but the underlying structure is the same."

"Good. Once the traps are down, you shadow-walk to this junction and wait. If anyone follows us, you're our exit strategy."

"Understood." Ivy's pale eyes met his, and something passed between them—gratitude, maybe, or the weight of things unsaid. She'd been preparing for this moment for weeks, gathering intelligence, mapping the compound's defenses. All without anyone asking her to.

"Lexi." Drevik turned to the auburn-haired woman who had been silently fuming since Seraphina's arrival. "You and I go through the maintenance corridor. We reach Elder Thornwood, get him the evidence, and protect him until we can call in loyal seekers."

"And what about her?" Lexi jerked her chin toward Seraphina, who was examining her nails with studied nonchalance.

"I go where I'm needed." Seraphina's voice was cool, but Drevik caught the edge beneath it. "Someone has to create a distraction at the main entrance. Unless you'd prefer to fight your way through Corvin's guards?"

Lexi's hands curled into fists. "I'd prefer you weren't part of this at all."

"And yet here I am." Seraphina rose from her chair, moving to stand in front of Drevik. This close, he saw the exhaustion beneath her composed mask, the shadows under her eyes. "Look, I know you don't trust me. You have no reason to. But Corvin killed my mother, and he's been using my father as a puppet for years. I want him to burn as badly as you do."

The raw honesty in her voice made Lexi hesitate. Drevik watched the two women face each other—Lexi with her fierce protectiveness, Seraphina with her careful walls finally showing cracks.

"Fine," Lexi said finally. "But if you betray us—"

"You'll kill me yourself. I know." Seraphina's lips twisted. "Get in line."

The tension broke, and Drevik exhaled slowly. They had a plan. A terrible, desperate plan, but it was something.

"I need a moment," he said. "Before we move."

He walked to the window, staring out at the city lights. The moon was three-quarters full, its light lending strength to his abilities. He would need every advantage tonight.

Soft footsteps approached behind him. He didn't need to turn to know it was Lexi.

"You don't have to do this." Her voice was low, meant only for him. "We could find another way."

"There is no other way." He turned to face her, struck as always by the fierce beauty of her—the sharp angles of her face, the determination in her green eyes. "You know that."

"I know you're the one taking all the risks." She stepped closer, close enough that he felt the heat radiating from her. "Every vision you've had, every path you've seen—you're always at the center. Always the one in danger."

"Lexi—"

"I can't lose you." The words came out rough, almost angry. "Do you understand? I've lost too much already. My mother, my brother—I can't—"

He silenced her with a kiss.

It wasn't gentle. He cupped her face in his hands and pressed his mouth to hers with a desperation that surprised them both. She responded instantly, her fingers twisting in his shirt, pulling him closer. The kiss tasted like fear and determination and something deeper—something that had been growing between them since the day she'd been assigned to evaluate him as an acolyte.

When they broke apart, both of them were breathing hard.

"I'm coming back," he said against her forehead. "I promise."

"Don't make promises you can't keep." But her arms wrapped around him, holding tight for a moment before she pulled away. Her eyes were bright, but her voice was steady when she spoke. "We should move. The longer we wait, the worse our chances."

He nodded, but before he could turn, Ivy appeared at his elbow.

"Drevik." She held out a small pouch. "Shadow-dust. It'll mask your presence for a few minutes—long enough to get through the maintenance corridor without triggering any alarms."

He took it, their fingers brushing. "Thank you. For everything."

"I didn't do it for thanks." Her pale eyes studied his face. "I did it because you're the first person in this Order who saw me as more than a researcher. Who trusted me to be more."

"You've always been more, Ivy."

She smiled—a small, fragile thing that made something twist in his chest. Then she stepped back, her expression returning to its usual careful neutrality.

Seraphina was the last to approach. She waited until the others were occupied before sliding up beside him, her voice pitched low.

"I wasn't entirely honest earlier." She pulled something from her pocket—a small crystal that pulsed with faint light. "This is a truth-stone. Elder Thornwood gave it to my mother years ago. If you get it to him, he'll know the evidence is genuine. Corvin can't counterfeit its light."

"Why are you giving this to me?"

"Because you're the one walking into the dragon's den." Her dark eyes met his, and for once, there was no smirk, no practiced seduction. "Because if anyone can pull this off, it's you."

He took the stone, feeling its warmth against his palm. "Seraphina—"

"Don't." She held up a hand. "Whatever you're going to say, save it until this is over. One way or another."

She turned to leave, but he caught her wrist. Her pulse jumped under his fingers.

"Thank you," he said simply.

She hesitated, then covered his hand with hers. A brief squeeze—almost too quick to notice.

"Stay alive, Drevik Markinsoul. I haven't decided what to do with you yet."

Then she was gone, slipping out the door to begin her distraction.

Lexi appeared at his shoulder. "We should move."

"Yeah." He took a breath, centering himself. "Let's go."

The maintenance corridor smelled of dust and old magic.

Drevik moved through the darkness with Lexi close behind, one hand on the wall to guide him. The shadow-dust Ivy had given him created a thin veil around his presence, but it wouldn't last forever. Every step was a countdown.

The corridor was barely wide enough for one person, carved from the same stone as the rest of the compound but untouched for decades. Cobwebs brushed his face, and he tried not to think about what might have made them.

"Stop." Lexi's whisper cut through the darkness. "There's a ward ahead."

He froze, extending his senses. She was right—a thin thread of power stretched across the corridor, invisible to normal sight but shimmering with warning to anyone with Veil Sight. If they passed through it, alarms would sound throughout the compound.

"Can you disable it?"

"Maybe. Give me a moment." She moved past him, her fingers tracing the air. He felt her power reaching out, shadows responding to her call. A soft pop echoed through the corridor, and the ward collapsed. "There. Move quickly."

They emerged into a wider passage, and Drevik recognized the eastern wing of the compound. The walls here were hung with tapestries depicting the Order's history—scenes of shadow-walkers battling void creatures, of Elders raising barriers against breaches. The irony wasn't lost on him. The organization that had protected the mortal realm for centuries was now being corrupted from within.

Voices drifted from around the corner. Drevik pressed himself against the wall, Lexi beside him.

"—searched the lower levels. No sign of them."

"Keep looking. Corvin wants them found before midnight."

Footsteps retreated, and Drevik let out a breath he hadn't realized he'd been holding.

"Elder Thornwood's chambers are just ahead," Lexi said. "But there will be guards."

"I know." He closed his eyes, reaching for his gift. The futures flickered before him—possibilities branching like lightning. He saw himself fighting through the guards, saw Lexi fall to a shadow blade, saw Elder Thornwood's door breached before they could reach it. He saw them turn back, abandoning the mission. He saw—

There. A thread of possibility, thinner than the others but shining with potential.

"Follow me," he said. "And stay quiet."

He moved through the compound with new purpose, taking turns that seemed random but weren't. The futures had shown him a path—a servant's corridor, a hidden door, a moment when the guards changed shifts. They slipped through gaps in the patrol routes, shadow-walked through stretches that would have been impossible to cross physically.

And finally, they stood before Elder Thornwood's door.

It was carved from dark wood, inlaid with silver runes that pulsed with protective magic. Two guards stood on either side, their faces masked, shadow blades at their hips.

"Four of them," Lexi said. "We can take them."

"No." Drevik's mind raced through possibilities. "There's a better way."

He reached into his pocket and withdrew the truth-stone Seraphina had given him. Its light was faint but steady. He closed his eyes, focusing his will, and pushed.

The stone's light flared.

The guards turned, hands going to their weapons, but Drevik was already moving. He stepped into the shadows between one heartbeat and the next, emerging behind the guard on the left. His knife found the gap in the man's armor, and the guard crumpled without a sound.

Lexi took the second one before he could raise an alarm.

The other two guards came at them together, shadow blades gleaming in the corridor's torchlight. Drevik ducked under a slash, feeling the cold whisper of the blade pass inches from his face. He drove his elbow into his attacker's stomach, then brought his knife up under the man's chin.

Lexi was a whirlwind of motion beside him, her training showing in every precise strike. She disarmed her opponent with a twist of her wrist and brought him down with a blow to the temple.

Four guards. Four bodies on the floor.

"Inside," Drevik gasped. "Now."

He pushed open the door and stumbled into Elder Thornwood's chambers.

The old man sat at his desk, surrounded by scrolls and artifacts, his weathered face turning toward them in surprise. His white hair was disheveled, his robes marked with the signs of a long siege. But his eyes—those piercing blue eyes that had seen decades of Order politics—were sharp as ever.

"Drevik." Elder Thornwood rose slowly, leaning heavily on his staff. "I wondered if you would come."

"Sir." Drevik stepped forward, pulling the evidence from his jacket. "Corvin is planning to overthrow you tonight. He's been working with a void walker from the Shadowlands—here's the proof."

The Elder took the scrolls, his eyes scanning the contents. His expression darkened with each line.

"The foolish boy," he said. "He would risk everything for power."

"There's more." Drevik handed over Seraphina's truth-stone. "This will confirm the evidence is genuine."

Elder Thornwood's fingers closed around the stone, and its light flared brighter—confirmation that the documents were real, that the signatures were authentic.

"I had hoped..." The old man shook his head. "I had hoped there was another way. But this leaves no room for doubt."

He turned to Lexi. "Your father—"

"Is compromised," Lexi said, her voice strained. "I know."

"I'm sorry, child." The Elder's tone softened. "I know what this costs you."

"Save your sympathy for after we survive." Lexi's jaw tightened. "Can you call in loyal seekers? The ones who haven't turned?"

"I've already sent word to those I trust. But they're hours away, and Corvin's people control the compound." Elder Thornwood moved to his window, looking out at the night sky. "He'll move soon. The moon is nearly at its peak—his power will be at its height."

"Then we take the fight to him." Drevik stepped forward. "Before he can bring his forces to bear."

The Elder turned to face him, something like pride flickering in his eyes. "You've grown much since your initiation, Drevik Markinsoul. Your parents would be proud."

The words hit him like a physical blow. "My parents?"

"Another conversation for another time." Elder Thornwood gripped his staff. "For now, we have a traitor to confront."

The explosion shook the entire compound.

Drevik staggered, catching himself on the desk as debris rained from the ceiling. The sound of shouting and running feet filled the corridor outside.

"What—"

"Seraphina." Lexi's voice was grim. "Her distraction."

"It's working." Elder Thornwood moved toward the door. "We must reach the central chamber before Corvin completes his ritual. If he succeeds in opening a full breach, we'll have more than political intrigue to worry about."

They moved through the chaos together, the Elder's staff clearing a path through the wreckage. Order members ran past them—some loyal, some corrupted, all too confused to tell friend from foe. Drevik's shadow-dust had worn off, but it hardly mattered now. Stealth had given way to survival.

The central chamber loomed ahead, its great doors carved with the symbols of the Order. Power pulsed from within—dark, alien power that made Drevik's skin crawl.

"He's already started," Elder Thornwood growled. "The fool."

The doors burst open.

Corvin stood at the center of the chamber, surrounded by a ring of corrupted acolytes. His arms were raised, shadows swirling around him in a vortex that drink the light from the room. And beyond him, in the space where the veil between worlds was thinnest, something was pushing through.

Something hungry.

"You're too late, old man." Corvin's voice echoed with power. "The breach is already opening. Soon, my ally will walk in this realm, and the Order will belong to those with the strength to seize it."

Drevik saw the futures branching before him—dozens of ways this could end, most of them in darkness. But there, in the chaos of possibilities, one thread shone brighter than the others.

He reached for Lexi's hand.

Then for Ivy's, as she materialized beside them, her face pale but determined.

And finally, surprisingly, for Seraphina's, as she emerged from the shadows on his other side.

Four people. Four connections. Four paths converging.

The truth-stone in Elder Thornwood's hand flared, and Corvin's ritual faltered.

"Now!" the Elder shouted.

Drevik closed his eyes and reached for the one future that might save them all.

The vision hit him like a tidal wave—possibilities collapsing into a single thread. He saw the path forward, clear and terrible. He saw what needed to be done.

And he saw, with cold certainty, that one of the people standing beside him would not survive the night.

The question was: who?

He opened his eyes, meeting Corvin's mad gaze across the chamber.

"Let's finish this."

Chapter 19: The Traitor Revealed

Chapter 19: The Traitor Revealed

The corridor stretched before them like a throat, all shadow and stone, and Drevik couldn't shake the feeling that they were walking directly into something that would swallow them whole.

"Left at the next junction," Seraphina said, her voice barely disturbing the air. The mysterious woman moved as if she were part of the darkness itself, her dark hair blending with the shadows that clung to the walls. "Two guards. They haven't seen us yet."

Drevik nodded, feeling the weight of the evidence crystal in his pocket. Inside it, preserved through magic he didn't fully understand, Corvin's confession played on an endless loop—his voice, his words, his treachery made manifest. They just had to get it to Elder Thornwood before Corvin realized how much they knew.

Lexi pressed close to his side, her shoulder brushing his with each step. The contact shouldn't have felt so grounding, not when they were creeping through the Order's compound with every shadow potentially hiding an enemy. But her warmth anchored him in a way nothing else could.

I should tell her, he thought, not for the first time. If this goes wrong, if we don't make it—

"Stop," Ivy said, her hand catching Drevik's arm. The small blonde's eyes had gone distant, that particular look she got when her peculiar gifts showed her something the rest of them couldn't see. "They know we're coming. Corvin—he told them Drevik's the traitor. They're mobilizing."

Drevik's stomach dropped. Of course Corvin had contingencies. A man who'd spent years plotting the collapse of the Veil wouldn't leave his position to chance.

"How many?" Lexi asked, her hand already moving to the blade at her hip.

"Eight. No, ten." Ivy's face paled. "Coming from three directions. We have maybe thirty seconds before—"

The first guard rounded the corner.

Drevik didn't think. He reached for the shadows—the power that had always come so naturally to him, the gift that made him either a prodigy or a freak depending on who you asked—and pulled.

The guard's shadow reached up and wrapped around his ankles like hands. He went down hard, his weapon clattering across the stone floor, and before he could cry out, Seraphina was there. A touch to his forehead, a whisper of words Drevik couldn't quite hear, and the man went limp.

"Sleep," she said simply. "He'll wake with a headache and no memory of the last hour."

"One down," Lexi muttered, already moving. "Nine to go."

Then they were upon them.

Fighting alongside Lexi was unlike anything Drevik had experienced.

They moved together without words, without signals, as if some part of them had already merged into a single unit. When a shadow-blade came for his left side, her knife was there to deflect it. When she overextended toward a guard's throat, he caught the enemy's counter-strike with shadow wrapped around his forearm.

How do we know each other so well? The question flickered through his mind even as he ducked under a swinging fist. We've barely trained together.

But it wasn't training. It was something deeper. Something that made his chest ache even as his body moved through the violent choreography of survival.

Ivy fought with a ferocity that belied her small frame, her hands glowing with pale light as she struck pressure points and sent guards crumpling. Seraphina moved like smoke, always where the enemy least expected, her fingers leaving unconscious bodies in her wake.

But it was Lexi who held Drevik's attention. Lexi, with her auburn hair coming loose from its braid. Lexi, with her green eyes sharp and focused. Lexi, who put herself between him and danger without a second thought.

A guard broke through their formation, his blade aimed at Drevik's chest. Time slowed. Drevik saw the strike coming, knew he couldn't move fast enough—

Lexi's body slammed into his, spinning them both aside. The blade caught her arm instead of his heart.

"Lexi!"

"I'm fine." She was already back on her feet, blood running down her forearm, her eyes never leaving the enemy. "Focus, Drevik. We're not done."

But something in Drevik cracked at the sight of her blood. A wall he'd been carefully maintaining, a distance he'd been keeping, crumbled.

She could have died. She would have died for me.

The fight ended three minutes later. Ten guards lay unconscious or groaning on the stone floor, and Drevik's breath came in ragged gasps. The shadow-magic always took something from him—a little piece of his energy, a fragment of his warmth. Using it this extensively left him hollow.

Lexi's hand found his, her fingers threading between his own.

"We need to keep moving," she said. Her other arm hung at her side, the wound still bleeding freely. "Elder Thornwood's chambers are another two floors down."

"Your arm—"

"Will survive until we've stopped a Shadowlands breach." Her green eyes met his, and in them he saw everything she wasn't saying. "Priorities, Drevik."

He wanted to kiss her. The absurdity of the timing—standing in a corridor surrounded by unconscious bodies, his best friend bleeding, a traitor to confront—didn't make the urge any less powerful. He wanted to press his lips to hers and pour everything he couldn't articulate into that contact.

Instead, he squeezed her hand once and let go.

"Then let's finish this."

They made it to Elder Thornwood's chambers without further incident, which should have been the first sign that something was wrong.

The heavy wooden doors stood open. Candlelight spilled into the corridor, warm and golden, casting dancing shadows across the stone. Inside, Drevik heard voices—low, calm, the kind of conversation that happened over tea, not during crises.

He signaled the others to stop, pressing himself against the wall beside the doorway. Lexi mirrored his position on the opposite side, her knife ready.

"—understand your concerns, Elder, but I'm afraid you've been misled."

Corvin's voice. Smooth, cultured, reasonable. The voice of a man who had never once raised it in anger because he'd never needed to. The voice of a man who could convince you the sky was green if it served his purposes.

"Corvin, I've known you since you were a child. I trained you. I—"

"And that's precisely why this hurts." A pause, the sound of movement. "I need you to sign this order, Elder. The one that transfers gate authority to my office. Just until this business with the Markinsoul boy is resolved."

Drevik's blood ran cold. Gate authority. With that, Corvin could open any of the Order's sealed passages to the Shadowlands. All of them. At once.

Ivy was right. He's not just weakening the Veil—he's planning to tear it apart completely.

Drevik looked at Lexi. Her face had gone pale, her jaw tight with barely contained fury. She knew what gate authority meant. She'd grown up in this world, understood its structures and safeguards better than he ever would.

Her father, he realized suddenly. Elder Thornwood is Lexi's father.

The realization hit him like a physical blow. He'd known, abstractly, that Lexi came from an Order family. But seeing her face now, watching her listen to a traitor threaten her father, understanding the impossible position she was in—

She met his eyes. Something passed between them, silent and complete. I have to do this, her gaze said. I have to face him.

I'm with you, his answered. Whatever happens.

Drevik stepped through the doorway.

The scene inside was worse than he'd imagined.

Elder Thornwood sat behind his desk, his face drawn and pale, his hands flat on the wood as if to steady himself. The man looked older than Drevik remembered, the lines around his eyes deeper, his silver hair thin. But his spine was straight, his chin lifted. Whatever Corvin had done to him, he hadn't broken.

Corvin stood behind the Elder's chair, one hand resting casually on the old man's shoulder. In his other hand, he held a blade—not steel, but shadow-glass, the Order's most dangerous substance. One touch of that blade to bare skin would sever a person's connection to magic forever.

And pressed against Elder Thornwood's throat was the edge of that blade.

"Drevik." Corvin's smile was genuine, almost warm. "I wondered when you'd arrive. You've been so very busy tonight, haven't you? Running through my halls, incapacitating my people, carrying evidence you think will save you."

"Evidence that will hang you," Lexi spat, stepping up beside Drevik. Her voice shook with barely contained rage. "Let my father go, you monstrous—"

"Ah, Alexa." Corvin's eyes moved to her, and something in his expression shifted. Not cruelty—something worse. Pity. "Your father has been a good man. A faithful servant of the Order. But his generation's time is ending. The Veil has held for too long, kept us too separate from the source of our power."

"You're insane." Seraphina's voice came from somewhere behind Drevik. "The Shadowlands will consume everything. You can't control what comes through a full breach."

"Can't I?" Corvin's smile widened. "You assume I'm opening the gates without preparation. But I've been planning this for decades, Seraphina. I've made arrangements. The entity that comes through will be bound to me—will become me. Immortal, unstoppable, powerful beyond your comprehension."

Drevik's shadow-sense flared. Somewhere below them, he felt it—a pressure building, a wrongness pressing against the Veil. Corvin had already started. Whatever he needed gate authority for, he'd found another way to begin.

"You've been weakening the barriers for years," Drevik said, the pieces falling into place. "The incidents—the breaches we've been sent to contain. You caused them."

"Of course I caused them." Corvin laughed, a sound like breaking glass. "Every breach thins the fabric. Every creature that slips through leaves a wound that doesn't quite heal. And you, my talented young acolyte—you've been my unwitting assistant. Every time you sealed a breach, you left a little piece of your power behind. A little fragment of potential that I could use later."

The words hit Drevik like ice water. He's been using me. From the beginning.

"What do you want?" Drevik forced himself to keep his voice steady, even as his mind raced through possibilities. "You have the Elder at knifepoint. You've been planning this for decades. What could you possibly need from me now?"

Corvin's eyes gleamed.

"Your blood, Drevik. The Markinsoul bloodline carries something special—something I couldn't have predicted. Your mother had it too, before she died. The ability to see futures, to walk paths that haven't yet been taken. With that gift added to the ritual, I won't just control what comes through the breach—I'll be able to choose which future becomes real. I'll be a god."

The world narrowed to a single point. Drevik felt the other people in the room—Lexi's barely contained fury, Ivy's terror, Seraphina's calculating stillness—but they seemed very far away.

My mother.

"She knew what you were planning," Drevik said, his voice strange and distant. "That's why she died."

"She knew too much," Corvin agreed. "And she refused to join me. A waste, truly. But her son—her talented, powerful, useful son—might prove more reasonable." The shadow-glass blade pressed closer to Elder Thornwood's throat. "Surrender, Drevik. Give me your cooperation, and I'll let your friends live. Refuse, and I'll take what I need from your corpse."

Drevik looked at Lexi.

Her green eyes were bright with unshed tears, her face pale, her injured arm still bleeding. But beneath the fear, beneath the pain, he saw something else. Trust. Absolute, unwavering trust.

Whatever you decide, her eyes said. I'm with you.

He made his choice.

The vision hit him like lightning.

One moment he was standing in the Elder's chamber, watching Corvin's smile spread across his face. The next, he was somewhere else entirely—standing at the edge of a wound in reality, a tear in the fabric of the world that glowed with sickly purple light.

Through the tear, he saw the Shadowlands. Not the distant glimpses he'd caught before, but the true depth of it—a twilight realm of twisted geometry and hungry things, of shadows that moved with their own will and eyes that watched from the darkness.

And something was coming through.

The entity was vast. Vast in a way that made Drevik's mind stutter and skip, unable to process what it was seeing. It was shadow and hunger and ancient, patient malice, and it had been waiting for this moment for longer than humanity had existed.

In the vision, Drevik saw himself standing at the breach's edge. He saw Lexi behind him, bleeding from a wound he couldn't see. He saw Seraphina and Ivy collapsed on the ground, their eyes glassy and distant.

And he saw what he had to do.

The breach couldn't be closed from this side—not fully, not permanently. The entity had already pushed too much of itself through. But if someone went through to the other side, if they pulled the breach closed from within the Shadowlands...

They could seal it. Forever.

The vision shifted, showing him the aftermath. The Order rebuilding. Lexi, her face lined with grief, rising through the ranks. His name spoken with reverence and sorrow. A memorial, carved in shadow-glass, bearing his image.

And then—further out, harder to see—something that made his heart clench.

He wouldn't die.

The vision wasn't clear on what happened after he stepped through. But it showed him enough. He wouldn't die. He would be trapped, stranded in the Shadowlands, but he would survive. And if he survived...

There might be a way back.

The vision released him.

Drevik staggered, catching himself on the doorframe. The others were staring at him—Lexi with concern, Ivy with knowing eyes, even Corvin with something like curiosity.

"Interesting," the traitor said. "You saw something, didn't you? A gift like yours can't help but show you paths. Tell me, Drevik—what did you see? Your victory? My defeat?"

Drevik straightened slowly. His heart was pounding, his hands shaking, but his mind was clear for the first time since this nightmare began.

"I saw how this ends," he said. "And I choose it."

Corvin's smile faltered. Just for a moment, just a fraction, but Drevik saw it.

"You choose—"

Drevik moved.

He didn't think about the shadow-glass blade at Elder Thornwood's throat. He didn't calculate odds or plan contingencies. He simply moved, reaching for every scrap of power he'd ever touched, every shadow he'd ever walked through, every future he'd ever seen.

The room went dark.

Not dim—dark. Absolutely, completely dark, as if someone had snuffed out every light in existence. Drevik heard Corvin's startled cry, heard Elder Thornwood's chair scrape back, heard Lexi's sharp intake of breath.

And in that darkness, Drevik walked.

Shadow-walking was usually a slow process—step into one shadow, step out of another. But this wasn't usual. This was every shadow at once, every darkness he could reach, and he poured himself into it like he'd never done before.

He emerged behind Corvin.

The traitor was still holding his blade, still reaching for the Elder, but his eyes were wide with shock, his smile finally gone. Drevik's hand closed around Corvin's wrist, his grip iron-tight.

"You killed my mother," Drevik said, his voice flat and cold. "You used me. You threatened everyone I love."

Corvin's eyes met his. There was fear there now—real fear, the fear of a man who had finally lost control.

"The breach," Corvin gasped. "It's already opening. You can't stop it. No one can."

"Maybe not." Drevik squeezed, feeling bones grind together. Corvin's hand opened, and the shadow-glass blade fell. "But I can stop you."

He didn't kill him. Some part of him wanted to—that dark, angry part that had lived in his chest since his grandmother died, since he'd been alone in the world. But killing Corvin wouldn't bring his mother back. It wouldn't undo the damage.

Instead, he pulled.

Shadow wrapped around Corvin like chains, binding his arms to his sides, his legs together, his mouth shut. The traitor fell to the floor, writhing silently, his eyes still wide with terror.

"Lexi!" Drevik turned, already moving. "Your father—are you—"

But Lexi was already there, crouched beside the Elder's chair, her hands checking him over with practiced efficiency. Her father's hand came up to cup her face, a gesture so tender it made Drevik's chest ache.

"I'm fine, little star," Elder Thornwood said, his voice rough. "Thanks to him." His eyes met Drevik's. "You have my gratitude, Acolyte. And my attention. We need to talk about—"

"The breach," Drevik interrupted. He felt it getting stronger, the wound in reality widening somewhere below them. "It's opening. Corvin started the ritual before he came here. We don't have much time."

Ivy appeared at his elbow, her face pale but determined.

"It's in the lower sanctum. The old gate—the one they sealed a hundred years ago. He's been weakening it for years, and tonight he broke the final lock." Her eyes met Drevik's. "It's bad. Really, really bad."

"Then we stop it." Lexi was on her feet, her knife back in her hand, her expression fierce. "We've stopped breaches before."

"Not like this." Seraphina's voice was quiet, her face grave. "This isn't a breach. This is an invasion. The entity Corvin made his deal with—it's already coming through. And if it finishes crossing..."

"Then we don't let it finish," Drevik said.

He looked at each of them in turn. Ivy, small and scared but standing tall. Seraphina, mysterious and powerful and finally showing something real beneath her mask. Elder Thornwood, wounded but still fighting. And Lexi—Lexi with her blood-stained arm and her tear-bright eyes and her unwavering faith.

I have to do this, he thought. I have to go through. But first—

He crossed to Lexi in three steps and pulled her into his arms.

The kiss was desperate and fierce and nothing like he'd imagined. It tasted of blood and fear and something sweeter underneath—hope, maybe, or love, or the shape of a future they might still have.

"I have to go through," he said against her lips. "I have to close it from the other side. I saw—"

"No." Lexi's hands fisted in his shirt, pulling him closer. "No, Drevik, you can't—the Shadowlands will—"

"I won't die." He cupped her face, making her look at him, making her see the truth in his eyes. "I saw it. I'll be trapped, but I'll survive. And if I survive, I can find my way back. There are paths between worlds, Lexi. Paths I can walk now." His voice cracked. "But I have to go. There's no other way."

Lexi's tears spilled over, tracking down her cheeks. But she didn't argue. She'd grown up in the Order; she understood sacrifice. She understood duty.

"Then I'm coming with you."

"You can't." It killed him to say it. "The sealing requires shadow-magic. Real, powerful shadow-magic. You'd be stranded with me, and someone needs to—someone needs to tell the Order what happened. Someone needs to make sure Corvin pays for this."

"Someone else can do that." Lexi's voice was raw. "Drevik, please—I can't lose you. Not now. Not when I just—"

"I know." He pressed his forehead to hers, breathing her in. "I know, Lexi. I love you too."

The words hung between them, simple and devastating.

"Then don't go," she said. "Stay. We'll find another way. We always—"

"There isn't one." He kissed her again, softer this time, trying to say everything he couldn't put into words. "But I'll come back. I promise you. I will find my way back to you."

Lexi made a sound that was half-sob, half-laugh.

"You'd better." She pulled back, wiping her face with her uninjured hand. "Because if you die in there, I'll find a way to bring you back and kill you myself."

Drevik smiled. It was the most real thing he'd felt all night.

"I'm holding you to that."

The lower sanctum was worse than the vision had shown.

The old gate dominated the chamber—a massive arch of black stone covered in symbols that writhe when Drevik looked at them. Through the arch, the Shadowlands pressed close, reality thinning and tearing as something vast pushed its way through.

The entity was already partially manifested. Tendrils of shadow-darkness reached into the mortal realm, tasting, probing, spreading. Where they touched stone, it blackened and crumbled. Where they touched air, it grew cold and thick.

"Go," Drevik told the others. "Get the Elder to safety. Warn the Order. And make sure Corvin doesn't get away."

"We're not leaving you," Ivy said, her voice trembling but firm.

"You have to." Drevik turned to face them, standing between them and the breach. "If you stay, the entity will use you against me. It'll find a way to keep the gate open. You have to go. Now."

Seraphina's hand found his shoulder, her touch surprisingly gentle.

"The Shadowlands are not kind to mortals," she said. "Even those with shadow-gifts. Be careful, Drevik. And..." She hesitated. "Come back."

"I will."

Ivy threw herself at him, her small arms wrapping around his waist in a fierce hug.

"You saved my life," she said against his chest. "More than once. I won't forget it."

"Neither will I." Drevik kissed the top of her head. "Now go. Both of you. Please."

They went. Seraphina's arm around Ivy's shoulders, guiding the smaller woman toward the exit. And then it was just Drevik and the breach and the entity pressing against the wound in the world.

This is it, he thought. This is what I was made for.

He walked toward the gate.

The entity noticed him. He felt its attention like a physical weight, like standing beneath a waterfall of ice water. It was curious, he realized. It had never encountered someone like him—someone who could walk between worlds, who saw futures, who carried shadow-gifts strong enough to challenge its own.

You cannot stop me, little shadow-walker, it say. The voice wasn't words, exactly. It was more like understanding, pressed directly into his mind. The breach is made. I am coming.

"Not if I close it." Drevik reached out with his power, feeling the edges of the tear, the places where reality had been worn thin. "Not if I seal you on the other side."

You would trap yourself. You would be stranded in eternal twilight, surrounded by things that would devour you in moments.

"I'll take my chances."

He stepped through the gate.

The Shadowlands were everything he'd feared and nothing he'd imagined.

Twilight stretched in every direction—a sky that was neither day nor night, a horizon that curved wrong, a landscape of twisted shapes and impossible angles. The air tasted of copper and shadows, and every sound come from everywhere at once.

Behind him, he saw the breach—the wound in reality that led back to the mortal world. The entity was still pressing through, its vast form making the opening bulge and stretch.

Now, Drevik thought. Before it's too late.

He reached for the edges of the breach. Not with his hands—with his power, his will, everything he had. The shadow-magic came, responding to his need, and he felt the wound start to close.

The entity screamed.

It wasn't a sound. It was a pressure, a force, a rage that threatened to tear his mind apart. The thing thrashed against the closing breach, trying to push through before it was too late, but Drevik held on. He held on with everything he had.

Close, he willed the breach. Close, damn you. CLOSE.

The wound in reality seal.

The entity's tendril was cut off, severed from its main body, and it writhed in the mortal world like a snake with its head removed. Drevik felt it being pulled back, felt the pressure easing, felt the barrier between worlds reasserting itself.

No! The entity's voice was rage and hunger and desperation. You cannot—

But he could. He was. The breach was closing, layer by layer, the Veil knitting itself back together.

And then, with a sound like a thunderclap, it sealed.

Drevik stood in the Shadowlands, alone.

The twilight pressed around him, heavy and thick. In the distance, he saw shapes moving—things that lived in this realm, things that would be very interested in a mortal who'd just trapped himself in their domain.

I did it, he thought, almost surprised. I actually did it.

His legs gave out. He collapsed to his knees on ground that was like packed ash, his body shaking with exhaustion, his power completely spent.

Now I just have to survive long enough to find a way back.

He looked up at the sky—at the strange, lightless sky that go on forever—and thought of Lexi. Her face, her voice, her arms around him. The taste of her kiss.

I promised, he reminded himself. I promised I'd come back.

Somewhere in the distance, something howled.

Drevik pushed himself to his feet, swaying slightly, and walk.

Back in the Mortal Realm

Lexi stood at the sealed gate, her hand pressed against cold stone.

"He's gone," she said. The words tasted like ash. "He's really gone."

"We'll find him." Ivy's voice was thick with tears. "The Order has resources, ancient texts, ways to—"

"Stop." Lexi's voice came out harder than she intended. "Just... stop. I need a minute."

Ivy fell silent. Behind them, Order members were flooding into the sanctum, drawn by the surge of power, the closing of a breach that shouldn't have existed. Elder Thornwood was among them, leaning heavily on Seraphina's arm, his face pale but determined.

But Lexi couldn't focus on any of that. She could only stare at the sealed gate and feel the space where Drevik should have been.

He said he'd come back, she reminded herself. He promised.

But promises were just words. And the Shadowlands were not kind.

A hand touched her shoulder. She turned to find her father standing there, his face lined with something she'd never seen in him before—genuine, unguarded emotion.

"I owe that boy my life," Elder Thornwood said. "And more than that—I owe him the truth. About his parents. About what the Order has been hiding." His jaw tightened. "When we bring him back—and we will bring him back—he deserves to know everything."

Lexi's eyes stung. She blinked rapidly, refusing to cry in front of the gathering crowd.

"He told me he loved me," she said, her voice barely audible. "Right before he walked through that gate. He told me he loved me, and then he left."

Her father's expression softened.

"Then we bring him home," he said firmly. "And you tell him again."

Lexi nodded, swallowing hard. Her hand fell away from the sealed gate, her fingers curling into a fist at her side.

Drevik was out there, somewhere in the eternal twilight, fighting to survive. And she was here, safe and whole and useless.

But not for long.

I'm coming for you, she promised silently, staring at the stone as if she saw through it to the realm beyond. Whatever it takes, however long it takes—I'm coming for you.

The Order's members swarm around the sealed gate, examining it, discussing it, planning. Behind them, Corvin was being dragged away in shadow-chains, his face still frozen in that expression of shocked terror.

It was over. The breach was sealed. The traitor was caught.

But for Lexi, nothing was resolved.

Drevik was gone.

And she would move heaven and earth to get him back.

End of Chapter 19

Chapter 20: Through the Veil

Chapter 20: Through the Veil

The breach screamed as Drevik stepped through it.

Not a sound—not exactly. More like the feeling of a scream pressed against his skin, cold and wrong and hungry. The mortal world fell away behind him in a rush of displaced air and Lexi's voice calling his name, and then he was elsewhere.

The Shadowlands stretched before him in eternal twilight.

Drevik's knees buckled. He caught himself on one hand, fingers sinking into ground that wasn't ground—something between ash and velvet, gray-black and faintly luminescent. The air tasted of copper and ozone and something older, something that made his hindbrain scream at him to run.

He didn't run. He'd known what he was walking into.

Mostly.

Above him, the sky was a bruise. No sun, no moon, no stars—just endless gradients of purple and gray that shifted like something breathing. The landscape rolled away in impossible geometries, hills that curved the wrong way, shadows that fell toward the light instead of away from it. In the distance, structures that might have been buildings or might have been bones rose from the obsidian plain, their shapes suggesting architecture that human eyes weren't meant to understand.

Beautiful, some part of him thought. In the way a knife is beautiful before it opens your throat.

He pushed himself upright. Behind him, the breach hung in the air like a wound—a ragged oval of light that shouldn't exist here, spilling mortal-realm illumination into the eternal dusk. Through it, he saw the ritual chamber distorted as if through frosted glass. Lexi's face, her mouth moving. The others around her, their expressions ranging from terror to determination.

But their voices were gone. The breach was a window, not a door—not anymore. He'd known that too. To close it properly, he had to push from this side while they pulled from theirs.

Work fast, he told himself. Time moves differently here.

The entity found him before he took his third step.

It didn't approach. It arrived—one moment the space before him was empty, and the next it wasn't. The creature existed in the spaces between seeing, a presence that his eyes kept trying to slide past. It was darkness given form, hunger given purpose, and when it turned what might have been its attention toward him, Drevik felt his shadow magic writhe in response.

"Little thing." The voice came from everywhere and nowhere, vibrating through the ground and the air and his bones. "You carry the taint of the Veiled Ones."

Drevik's foresight activated without his permission—a cascade of possible futures flickering through his mind. In most of them, he died in the next thirty seconds. In some, he died more slowly. In a precious few—

He grabbed onto those few and held.

"I carry the weight of the Order," he said, surprised at how steady his voice sounded. "And you're going back where you belong."

The entity laughed, and the sound was thunder without clouds, lightning without rain. "I was summoned, little thing. Called by one of your own. I belong where I am wished."

"Corvin didn't know what he was calling." Drevik shifted his stance, feeling the Shadowlands' strange physics resist his movements. Everything here was heavier, slower, as if the realm itself wanted to drag him down and keep him. "He thought he was summoning power he could control."

"He summoned hunger." The entity drifted closer—or the darkness around Drevik deepened; he couldn't tell the difference. "I have fed on your kind before. The ones who call themselves Veiled. They taste of ambition and fear."

Drevik's foresight pulsed. Now, it said. Duck left.

He ducked left.

A tendril of absolute darkness sliced through the space where his head had been, and the air itself scream. Where the tendril touched the ground, reality peeled, revealing layers of nothing beneath nothing.

"Interesting." The entity's attention sharpened—Drevik felt it like a blade against his throat. "You see the paths."

"I see enough."

He didn't see enough. That was the problem. His foresight showed him possibilities, but the Shadowlands bent probability in ways his mortal mind couldn't fully process. Every future he glimpsed had ten more branching from it, and half of them ended with his death in the next instant.

But some of them didn't. Some of them—

Lexi, he thought, and the image steadied him. Her face in the moment before he'd stepped through the breach. The fear she'd tried to hide. The pride she hadn't bothered to conceal.

Go, she'd said, her hand on his chest, over his heart. And come back.

In the mortal realm, Lexi stood before the breach with her arms spread and her eyes blazing.

"You can't close it." Elder Corvin—the real Corvin, the one who'd been freed from his brother's control when the ritual circle shattered—shook his head. "The energy required—"

"I'm not closing it." Lexi's voice cut through the chaos like her blade cut through shadow. The ritual chamber was in ruins, the floor cracked, the walls weeping something that looked like blood but wasn't. The other acolytes huddled near the entrance, too injured or too afraid to approach. "I'm keeping it open."

"The breach is destabilizing." Seraphina Vane moved to Lexi's side, her silver hair wild, her hands still glowing with the remnants of binding magic. "Even if we could hold it, the Shadowlands will—"

"I don't care what the Shadowlands will do." Lexi didn't look away from the oval of wrong light. Through it, she saw shapes moving—Drevik's silhouette against the twilight, and something larger, something darker. "He's coming back. I won't let you close it before he does."

"Lexi." Corvin's voice softened. "Time moves differently there. Hours could pass before—"

"Then we wait hours."

"The breach won't hold that long."

"Then we make it hold." She turned to Seraphina. "You're a Vane. Your family has been binding things for centuries. Bind this."

Seraphina's mouth curved—not quite a smile, but close. "You're asking me to anchor a hole in reality."

"I'm asking you to help me save him."

Something passed between the two women—acknowledgment, perhaps. Or challenge.

"Get the others," Seraphina said finally. "Anyone who can still channel. We'll need a circle."

Lexi moved without hesitation, grabbing acolytes by the arm, pulling them into position. Some were crying. Some were bleeding. All of them looked at the breach with naked terror.

But they moved when Lexi told them to move. They took their places in the circle. And when Seraphina chant, their voices rose with hers.

In the Shadowlands, Drevik was dying.

Not quickly—the entity prefer playing with its food—but inevitably. Each attack he dodged cost him something. Energy. Will. The ability to see the paths ahead clearly.

"You cannot win." The entity circled him now, a darkness that moved against the wind. "Even your foresight cannot show you every possibility. Eventually, you will miss one."

"Eventually." Drevik's breath came hard, his body aching from the constant evasion. "But not yet."

He needed to end this. He needed—

His foresight flickered, and he saw it.

Not a weakness exactly. But a connection. The entity had been summoned—called through a breach by Corvin's brother, anchored to the mortal realm by blood and will. That anchor was gone now, shattered when the ritual circle broke. But the entity had fed on the energy of the summoning, and that feeding had left a trace.

A trail.

Drevik saw it—a thin line of wrongness leading from the entity back toward the mortal realm, toward the breach. The creature was still connected to the summoning site, still tied to the place where it had been called.

That's how you send it back, his foresight said. Not by fighting it. By following the thread.

He didn't hesitate. He stopped dodging.

The entity's next attack caught him across the chest—not a direct hit, but close enough. Pain bloomed through him, cold and wrong, and he felt something tear inside that shouldn't tear. But his hands closed around the thread of connection, and he pulled.

The entity screamed.

It was worse than the breach's scream—worse than anything Drevik had ever heard. It was the sound of something being forced somewhere it didn't want to go, and the Shadowlands themselves recoil from it. The ground cracked beneath his feet. The sky above him tore.

But he kept pulling.

"NO." The entity's voice had lost its thunder. It was desperate now, a thing that had been hungry for so long and now faced starvation. "I will take you with me. I will—"

Drevik pulled harder.

His foresight showed him what would happen next. The entity would be drawn back through the breach—if he could reach the breach himself in time. The closing would happen from both sides at once, and he needed to be in the threshold when it did. Not on this side. Not on that side.

Between.

He ran.

"Something's happening." Lexi pressed her hand against the breach, ignoring the way the edges bit into her palm. "He's—"

The breach pulsed.

Light and darkness spiraled through the oval, and the entity's scream echoed through the ritual chamber. The acolytes in the circle cried out, their connection to the magic wavering.

"Hold!" Seraphina's voice cracked like a whip. "Whatever comes through, you hold!"

Lexi didn't look back. She couldn't. All she saw was the breach, and through it—

Drevik.

He was running toward her, his face set in grim determination, his chest dark with something that might have been blood but moved wrong. Behind him, the entity writhed, fighting against a force it couldn't see.

"Get back!" Drevik's voice, thin and distant, coming through the breach like sound through water. "It's going to—"

The breach contracted.

"No!" Lexi threw herself forward, as if she could hold it open with her bare hands. "Drevik—"

He jumped.

Time stretched. Lexi watched him sail through the air, the twilight of the Shadowlands behind him, the entity reaching for him with tendrils of absolute darkness. He was going to make it. He was going to—

A tendril caught his ankle.

"NO—" Lexi's scream tore from her throat.

Drevik twisted in midair, his hand closing around something at his belt—a knife, she realized, one of the ritual blades they'd brought to the chamber. He drove it down into the tendril, into himself, into the connection between him and the entity.

And he pulled.

The entity shrieked. The breach screamed. And Drevik came through.

He hit the ground hard, rolling, and the breach slammed shut behind him like a door being kicked. The last thing Lexi saw through it was the entity's form dissolving, pulled apart by the closing, its hunger finally silenced.

Then there was silence.

Lexi dropped to her knees beside him.

"Drevik. Drevik—"

His eyes were closed. His chest—she couldn't look at his chest. The wound was too deep, too wrong, still leaking something that moved on its own.

"Medical," she heard someone say. Her voice, maybe. Or Seraphina's. "We need—"

Drevik's eyes opened.

Green-gold, they caught the light of the ruined ritual chamber, and for a moment Lexi saw something else in them. A flicker of twilight. A shadow that moved against the light.

Then it was gone.

"Hey," he said. His voice was a ruin.

"You absolute idiot." Lexi's hands hovered over him, afraid to touch, afraid not to. "You could have died. You should have died. What were you thinking?"

"Thinking..." Drevik coughed, and the sound was wet and wrong. "Thinking I'd come back."

"You came back." Lexi's vision blurred. She blinked furiously, refusing to cry. "You came back, you bastard. You actually came back."

His hand found hers. His fingers were cold, but they curled around hers with familiar strength.

"Told you," he said. "Always."

She laughed—or sobbed, she couldn't tell the difference anymore—and pressed her forehead to his shoulder. He smelled of copper and ash and something else, something she didn't recognize. Something that made her shadow magic stir in response.

Later, she told herself. We'll figure out what that means later.

Right now, he was alive. He was here. He'd come back.

That was enough.

Drevik woke in the infirmary three days later.

The wound on his chest had healed faster than it should have—another mystery to add to the growing list. The healers had done their work, but they'd looked at him strangely afterward, exchanging glances he wasn't supposed to see.

He didn't tell them about the dreams.

He'd been dreaming of the Shadowlands every night since he came back. Not nightmares exactly—more like memories. The twilight sky. The alien geometry of the landscape. The entity's voice, still echoing somewhere in the back of his mind.

You carry the taint of the Veiled Ones, it had said. And now Drevik carried something else too.

He felt it. A piece of the Shadowlands, lodged somewhere between his heart and his soul. It didn't hurt. It didn't feel wrong, exactly. It was like a door that had been left open, letting in a draft from somewhere else.

Later, he told himself. Figure it out later.

Lexi was sitting beside his bed when he opened his eyes.

"You're awake." She didn't sound surprised—she'd been here every time he'd woken, he was told. She'd barely left the infirmary since they'd brought him in. "How do you feel?"

"Like I fought a shadow entity and won." He tried to sit up, winced, and settled for propping himself on his elbows. "How long?"

"Three days." Her hand found his, the touch almost casual. "The Order is... impressed."

"The Order can—" He stopped himself. "What happened to Corvin's brother?"

"Dead." Lexi's expression flickered. "The ritual backlash when the breach closed. There wasn't enough left to bury."

Drevik absorbed that. He hadn't wanted the man dead—but he couldn't exactly mourn either. "And Corvin?"

"Recovering. He's been cleared of wrongdoing—his brother was controlling him through blood magic." She paused. "He's been asking about you. He wants to thank you."

"Later." Drevik's eyes found hers. "Right now, I want to know why you're here."

Lexi's jaw tightened. "You almost died."

"I know."

"You almost died, and the last thing I said to you before you went through that breach was go. Not—"

"Lexi."
