She stopped. Her eyes were too bright, her expression too controlled. He'd never seen her like this—unmade by something as simple as his survival.

"Come here," he said.

She hesitated. Then she leaned down and pressed her lips to his.

The kiss was gentle at first—tentative, as if she was afraid he'd break. But Drevik's hand came up to cup the back of her head, his fingers tangling in her hair, and the kiss deepened. She tasted like coffee and exhaustion and something uniquely her, and he drank her in like water after a drought.

When they finally broke apart, her forehead rested against his.

"I thought I'd lost you," she said. "When you went through that breach, I thought—"

"You didn't lose me." His thumb stroked her cheek. "I told you I'd come back."

"You almost didn't."

"But I did." He kissed her again, softer this time. "I came back for you."

She pulled back to look at him, her green eyes searching his face for something. Whatever she found made her expression shift—relief and wonder and something deeper, something she wasn't ready to name.

"You're an idiot," she said, but she was smiling. "A brave, stupid, wonderful idiot."

"I love you too."

The words slipped out before he could stop them. He hadn't meant to say it—not yet, not like this, not while he was lying in an infirmary bed with a wound that shouldn't have healed and dreams that weren't entirely his own.

But Lexi didn't flinch. Didn't pull away.

Instead, she kissed him again, and this time there was nothing gentle about it. This was a claiming—a promise made with lips and teeth and tongue. When she finally drew back, her breathing was ragged.

"Say it again," she demanded. "When you're not high on healing potions."

"I'll say it every day," he promised. "If you'll let me."

She laughed—a real laugh, bright and surprised. "You're going to be trouble, aren't you? More than you've already been."

"Probably."

"Good." She settled into the chair beside his bed, her hand still holding his. "I like trouble."

The ceremony was held a week later.

Drevik stood in the Order's central chamber, surrounded by faces that had become familiar over the past months. Elder Corvin presided, his voice steady despite the shadows under his eyes.

"Drevik Markinsoul," the Elder intoned. "You entered the Shadowlands and returned. You faced an entity of hunger and darkness and prevailed. You have proven yourself worthy of advancement."

The words were ritual, but Drevik felt their weight. He'd earned this—not just the rank, but the right to stand here. The right to call himself something more than an acolyte.

"By the authority vested in this Order, I name you Seeker. May you walk in shadow and emerge in light."

The title settled over him like a mantle. Seeker—the rank Lexi had held when they'd first met. The rank that meant he was trusted to operate alone, to make decisions in the field, to represent the Order in matters that mattered.

Lexi caught his eye across the chamber and smiled. She was a Seeker still, though she'd been offered advancement to Warden. She'd refused it, she'd told him later. She wanted to stay where she was a little longer.

With you, her smile said. Wherever you go.

The ceremony continued—formal words, formal gestures. But Drevik barely heard them. His attention was on the shadows at the edge of his vision, the way they lean toward him now, as if recognizing him as one of their own.

The Shadowlands left something in you, his foresight said. A gift. Or a curse. You'll find out which soon enough.

He pushed the thought away. Whatever had changed in him could wait. Right now, he had a celebration to attend, a woman to hold, and a future that—for the first time in his life—was like something he wanted rather than something he feared.

Later, there would be questions. The entity had spoken of his parents, of things he didn't understand. The Order had secrets upon secrets, and he'd barely scratched the surface. The Shadowlands had marked him, and eventually, he'd have to discover what that meant.

But that was later.

Tonight, he was alive. He was loved. He was home.

And that was enough.

The dream came on the first night of the new moon.

Drevik stood in the Shadowlands, but they were different now. The twilight sky swirled with colors he'd never seen before, and the ground beneath his feet hummed with power that felt almost familiar.

You carry our mark now, something said from the darkness. You are ours.

He woke with a gasp, his hand pressed against his chest. The wound was completely healed—but beneath his fingers, he felt it. A pulse of shadow, beating in time with his heart.

In the bed beside him, Lexi stirred.

"Drevik?" Her voice was thick with sleep. "What's wrong?"

He looked at her—really looked, letting his new Sight trace the patterns of shadow and light that surrounded her. She blazed with it, her bond to him a golden thread that connected them heart to heart.

"Nothing," he said, and meant it. "Just a dream."

She made a skeptical sound but settled back against his shoulder. Her hand found his chest, over the place where the shadow-pulse beat.

"Your heart's racing," she said.

"I know." He pressed a kiss to her hair. "Go back to sleep."

She did, her breathing evening out within minutes. Drevik stayed awake, watching the shadows dance across the ceiling.

What are you? he asked the darkness inside him.

It didn't answer—not in words. But he felt it stir, felt it settle deeper into his bones, and knew that whatever it was, it wasn't going anywhere.

Later, he told himself again. We'll figure it out later.

But even as he thought it, he knew there would be no later. The Shadowlands had marked him, and sooner or later, he'd have to discover what that meant.

For now, though, he had Lexi's warmth beside him and the Order's protection around him and a future that stretched before him full of possibility.

He closed his eyes.

And let the darkness carry him into sleep.

End of Book One

Chapter 21: Aftermath and Ascension

Chapter 21: Aftermath and Ascension

The first thing Drevik noticed was the weight.

It pressed against his chest like a second heartbeat—heavy, rhythmic, alive in a way that had nothing to do with his body. He tried to sit up, but the weight held him down, and when he opened his eyes, he understood why.

Lexi had fallen asleep with her head on his chest.

Her auburn hair spilled across the white hospital sheets, tangled and unwashed. Dark circles shadowed her eyes, and her hand gripped his hospital gown like she was afraid he might disappear if she let go. She'd changed out of her combat gear into someone's borrowed sweatshirt—too large, slipping off one shoulder—but she hadn't left.

Three days, the nurses had told him when he first woke. Three days she'd stayed in this room, refusing to leave for more than minutes at a time. Three days she'd watched him fight off fevers that shouldn't exist, nightmares that bled into waking, and moments when his heart simply... stopped.

He didn't remember most of it. Just fragments. Cold. Shadows that said in voices he almost recognized. And Lexi's hand in his, anchoring him to something real.

Carefully, so carefully, he lifted his free hand and touched her hair.

She stirred immediately, her green eyes snapping open with the alertness of someone who'd been sleeping lightly for days. When she saw him watching her, something flickered across her face—relief, maybe, or something softer that she quickly buried beneath her usual sharp expression.

"You're awake." Her voice was hoarse. "Again."

"How long this time?"

"Four hours. Longest yet." She pull back, but he caught her hand.

"Stay."

The word came out rougher than he intended. Raw. He saw her throat work as she swallowed, and for a moment, neither of them spoke.

"I'm not going anywhere," she finally said. "I told you. I'm done leaving you to face things alone."

The doctors came an hour later—three of them, in white coats that looked too clean for a hidden facility beneath a university campus. They examined him with instruments that hummed with shadow energy and asked questions that made Lexi's jaw tighten.

"Your vitals are stable," the eldest doctor said, a woman with silver-streaked hair and tired eyes. "But your resonance patterns have changed. The Shadowlands left something in you."

"Left what?"

"We're not sure." She hesitated. "Your connection to shadow magic has deepened. Significantly. Your Veil Sight is now active even in the mortal realm—you're seeing the Shadowlands overlap constantly, aren't you?"

Drevik nodded. He'd been trying to ignore it—the faint shimmer at the edges of his vision, the way shadows sometimes moved wrong, the whispers that came from empty corners. He'd thought it was the fever.

"It's permanent," the doctor continued. "Or at least, we can't find a way to reverse it. Your body has... adapted. You're not just an initiate anymore. You're something in between."

"What does that mean for his promotion?" Lexi asked, her voice sharp.

The doctor exchanged a glance with her colleagues. "That's not for us to decide. The Elders will want to evaluate him."

"Of course they will." Lexi's hand found Drevik's under the sheets, squeezing tight. "They always want something."

Corvin was being held in the deepest cells beneath the Order's compound—places Drevik hadn't known existed until they dragged him there in chains. He learned about it from Marcus, who came to visit on the second day of Drevik's recovery with coffee that tasted like despair and news that tasted worse.

"He's talking," Marcus said, slouched in the visitor's chair while Lexi stood guard by the window. "Telling them everything. Names, locations, the whole network. It's bigger than we thought—cells in three other cities, sympathizers at the Warden level."

"And the conspiracy?"

"Broken. Mostly." Marcus took a long drink of his terrible coffee. "The ones who weren't directly involved are scrambling to prove their loyalty. Elder Thornwood is leading the purge. She's... efficient."

Drevik had met Elder Thornwood exactly twice—both times in passing, both times struck by how much she looked like Lexi. Same auburn hair, though hers was shot through with silver. Same green eyes, though hers held centuries of secrets. She was one of the oldest members of the Order, one of the most powerful, and apparently, Lexi's mother.

He glanced at Lexi, but her expression gave nothing away.

"What happens to Corvin?" Drevik asked.

"Trial. Then execution, probably. The Order doesn't forgive betrayal." Marcus set down his cup. "You know he was going to sacrifice you, right? To open a permanent breach. Your bloodline—whatever your parents were—it would have been the key."

Drevik had pieced that much together in his fever dreams. The way Corvin had looked at him in the Shadowlands, hungry and desperate. The way the shadows had reached for him specifically.

"He said I was special," Drevik said. "That I had a destiny."

"You do." Lexi's voice cut through the room, sharp and certain. "But it's not the one he wanted for you. It's the one you choose."

The promotion ceremony happened on the fifth day, in a chamber Drevik had never seen before—deep beneath the compound, lit by candles that burned with shadow flame. The entire Order was there, or so it seemed. Hundreds of faces watching from the darkness as he knelt before the Elders' council.

Elder Thornwood stood at the center, her presence commanding despite her slight frame. Beside her, the other Elders formed a semi-circle—some he recognized, others strangers in dark robes.

"Drevik Markinsoul," Thornwood intoned, "you stand before this council having faced the Shadowlands and returned. Having exposed a conspiracy that threatened our Order. Having proven yourself worthy of the title you seek."

The words washed over him, formal and ancient, but Drevik barely heard them. His attention kept sliding to Lexi, standing at attention with the other Seekers, her eyes fixed forward with military precision. Except he saw the tension in her shoulders, the way her fingers twitched at her sides.

She was worried. For him.

"The Order of the Eclipsed Veil recognizes your sacrifice and your strength," Thornwood continued. "We grant you the rank of Acolyte, with all rights and responsibilities thereof. Rise, Acolyte Markinsoul. Take your place among us."

He rose. The room erupted in applause—not the polite kind, but the genuine relief of people who'd seen their world nearly fall apart. Marcus was grinning. Seraphina Vane caught his eye from across the chamber and inclined her head, her mysterious smile intact. And Lexi...

Lexi was watching him with an expression he'd never seen on her face before. Pride, yes. But something else beneath it. Something that looked almost like fear.

She knew something he didn't.

The celebration was brief. By unspoken agreement, Drevik and Lexi slipped away from the crowd, finding their way to a small courtyard he hadn't known existed—a pocket of shadow and starlight hidden between buildings, with a single bench that looked out over nothing.

They sat in silence for a long moment. The promotion ceremony had left him drained, the formal robes heavy on his shoulders, the weight of his new rank settling into his bones. But beneath it all, the strange energy that had lived in him since the Shadowlands continued to pulse.

"You're different," Lexi said. Not an accusation. An observation.

"I know."

"Your eyes." She turned to face him. "They shift now. Green to gold and back. I've only seen that in—"

"In who?"

She hesitated. "Old stories. Veiled Ones who spent too long in the Shadowlands. It doesn't mean anything bad. Just... you're changing."

"Everyone keeps saying that." He exhaled. "No one tells me what it means."

"It means you're becoming something more than you were." Her hand found his in the darkness. "It means I need to tell you something I should have said weeks ago."

The air between them shifted. Drevik's heart rate quickened, and he was suddenly, acutely aware of how close she was—the warmth of her body, the slight tremble in her fingers, the way the starlight caught the red in her hair.

"Lexi—"

"I believed in you." The words rushed out like she'd been holding them too long. "Even when I was horrible to you. Even when I pushed you away and told myself you were just some scholarship kid who'd wash out in a month. I knew you were special. I knew you'd be important. I just didn't want—"

"Didn't want what?"

"Didn't want to care about someone who might die." Her voice cracked. "Everyone I've ever let close has been taken from me. My father. My first mentor. People I trained with who didn't make it through their initiations. I couldn't—I couldn't watch that happen to you."

He turned her hand over in his, tracing the lines on her palm. "I'm not going anywhere."

"You can't promise that. None of us can." But she didn't pull away. "I just... I needed you to know. Before the ceremony. Before everything changes again. I believe in you, Drevik. I always did."

The confession hung between them, raw and vulnerable. Drevik reached up, cupping her face in his hand, feeling the slight dampness on her cheek that she'd never admit to.

"You stayed," he said. "Three days in that hospital room. You didn't eat, didn't sleep, just... stayed with me."

"I told you. I'm done leaving you alone."

"Then don't." He leaned closer, his thumb brushing across her cheekbone. "Don't leave. Don't push me away. Don't pretend you don't feel this just because you're scared."

"Who said anything about pretending?" Her whisper was fierce, her eyes bright with challenge even as her breath came faster. "I'm not scared of you, Drevik Markinsoul. I'm scared of what happens if I let myself want this."

"What happens?"

"Everything changes."

"Everything already changed." He smiled, small and certain. "The moment I walked into that library and saw you glaring at me like I'd personally offended your ancestors. Everything changed then. We're just catching up."

She laughed—a real laugh, surprised out of her—and the sound transformed her face. Younger. Softer. The fierce warrior melting into the girl who'd stayed by his bedside for three days.

"You're impossible," she said.

"You like that about me."

"Maybe." Her eyes dropped to his mouth. "Maybe I like a lot of things about you that I shouldn't."

"Try liking them anyway."

She closed the distance between them.

The kiss started gentle—hesitant, almost, like she was waiting for him to pull away. But when he didn't, when his hand slid into her hair and drew her closer, something shifted. Her mouth opened under his, hungry and desperate, months of tension finally finding release. She tasted like coffee and exhaustion and something uniquely Lexi—sharp and sweet and utterly consuming.

Drevik's world narrowed to sensation. The softness of her lips. The heat of her body pressing against his. The small sound she made when his teeth caught her bottom lip, a gasp that shot straight through him. His new senses flared, and he felt the shadow energy between them, saw the way their auras intertwined in the dim light—

And then his vision went white.

He broke away with a gasp, clutching his head as images flooded through him—futures that shouldn't exist, possibilities that spiraled outward from this moment. Lexi in a white dress. Lexi with blood on her hands. Lexi standing over a grave. Lexi laughing in a garden that existed nowhere in the mortal realm. A hundred versions of her, a hundred paths, all spinning out from this single point.

"Drevik!" Her hands were on his face, steadying him. "What's wrong? What happened?"

"Visions." He forced his eyes open, fighting through the afterimages. "I saw... futures. Possibilities. Too many of them."

She searched his face, worry and wonder warring in her expression. "Your power. It's manifesting."

"I didn't ask for this."

"No." Her thumb traced his cheekbone. "But you have it. And we'll figure out what it means. Together."

She kissed him again, softer this time, and he let himself fall into it despite the fear still pulsing at the edges of his mind.

They found Elder Thornwood waiting outside his quarters an hour later, her presence as elegant and unnerving as always. She looked at their joined hands with an unreadable expression.

"Acolyte Markinsoul. A moment of your time."

Lexi's grip tightened. "Mother—"

"Alone, if you please." The words were polite, but the command was absolute.

Lexi hesitated, then squeezed his hand once before releasing it. "I'll be nearby."

They watched her go, the Elder's gaze following her daughter with something that might have been fondness before she turned back to Drevik.

"Walk with me."

They moved through the compound's corridors in silence, passing chambers and passages he'd never seen. Finally, they emerged onto a rooftop overlooking the campus—the university's spires lit against the night sky, the city spreading beyond.

"You've caused quite a stir," Thornwood said. "Exposing Corvin. Breaking a conspiracy that's existed for decades. Returning from the Shadowlands with your mind intact."

"I had help."

"You had my daughter." Her voice softened slightly. "She hasn't left your side since you went into that breach. I haven't seen her this invested in anything—or anyone—since her father died."

Drevik said nothing. He wasn't sure what to say.

"Lexi told me about your... connection. Your developing feelings." Thornwood turned to face him, and for the first time, he saw past the Elder's mask. She looked tired. Old. Worried. "I won't interfere. She's old enough to make her own choices, and she's chosen you. But I need you to understand something."

"I'm listening."

"The Shadowlands changed you. The doctors can see it. The other Elders can sense it. You're not just an Acolyte anymore—you're something between. Something new." She stepped closer, her green eyes—so like Lexi's—boring into his. "You have your father's eyes, Drevik. And his fate, if you're not careful."

Drevik's blood went cold. "My father? You knew him?"

"I knew both your parents." Her voice dropped. "They were Veiled Ones—the highest rank our Order can bestow. They went into the Shadowlands together, years ago, trying to close a breach that threatened to consume this city. They never came back."

"But I was told—"

"Orphaned. Raised by your grandmother. Yes." She nodded. "Your parents made us promise to hide your heritage until you were ready. Until you'd proven yourself. Corvin knew—somehow, he learned what you were, and he tried to use you to finish what he started."

"What am I?"

"A descendant of Veiled Ones. Born with power that most initiates spend decades trying to achieve." She touched his face, almost gentle. "The Shadowlands marked you because they recognize you. You're one of theirs, Drevik. Part of you has always belonged to that realm."

The weight in his chest pulsed, and he felt the truth of her words in his bones. The whispers that called to him in dreams. The way shadows reached for him like long-lost friends. The futures that unfolded before his eyes.

"What do I do?"

"Learn. Grow. Become strong enough to carry the legacy your parents left behind." She stepped back, her mask sliding back into place. "And be careful. The Shadowlands aren't the only thing that will notice you now. There are others—entities, powers, forces beyond our understanding—that will be drawn to what you're becoming."

"Lexi—"

"Will want to protect you. It's her nature." A faint smile. "But you'll need to protect her too. The path ahead of you is dangerous, Drevik. More dangerous than you know. Your parents walked it before you, and it consumed them."

"Then I'll walk it differently."

She studied him for a long moment, then nodded. "Perhaps you will. You've already surprised us all." She turned toward the door. "Take the next week to recover. Train with Lexi. Learn to control your new abilities. After that... we'll see what you're truly capable of."

She was almost through the door when Drevik called out.

"Elder Thornwood. My parents' names. What were they?"

She paused without turning.

"Marcus and Elara. Veiled Ones of the Eclipse Court. Heroes of the Order." Her voice was quiet. "They loved each other very much. And they loved you enough to let you go."

The door closed behind her.

Drevik stood alone on the rooftop, the city lights spread before him, the weight of his legacy settling into his bones. The Shadowlands said at the edges of his consciousness, calling to him in voices that sounded almost familiar.

For the first time since he'd stumbled into the Order's library, he felt ready.

Not ready for everything that was coming. Not ready for the dangers Thornwood had hinted at or the powers growing inside him. But ready to stop running. Ready to stop being the scholarship kid who didn't belong.

He was Drevik Markinsoul. Son of Veiled Ones. Acolyte of the Eclipsed Veil. And he was going to find out what happened to his parents—no matter what it cost.

The shadow at his feet rippled, and for just a moment, he thought he saw a figure standing beside him. Tall, lean, with dark hair and green-gold eyes that matched his own.

Then it was gone.

But the whisper remained, curling through his mind like smoke.

We're waiting, son. Come find us.

Drevik smiled, and the night didn't seem so dark anymore.

Lexi was waiting for him at the entrance to his quarters, leaning against the wall with her arms crossed. The worry lines around her eyes smoothed when she saw him intact.

"What did she say?"

"Things I need to tell you." He stopped in front of her, close enough to feel her warmth. "But not tonight. Tonight, I just want to be here. With you."

Her expression softened. "That can be arranged."

"Can it?"

She grabbed the front of his formal robes and pulled him toward the door. "I've been waiting three days to get you alone, Acolyte Markinsoul. Don't make me wait any longer."

He let her drag him inside, let her push the door closed behind them, let himself fall into her kiss with the desperation of someone who'd nearly lost everything. The weight in his chest pulsed, but for once, it was like a promise rather than a burden.

Tomorrow, he'd start training. Tomorrow, he'd learn to control the powers growing inside him. Tomorrow, he'd begin the search for answers about his parents and the fate that awaited him.

But tonight, he had Lexi in his arms, and that was enough.

For now, it was enough.

Chapter 22: New Beginnings

Chapter 22: New Beginnings

The new quarters were better than he'd expected. Much better than the narrow cot in the initiate dormitory where the ceiling had leaked every time it rained. Drevik stood in the center of his private room—his room, a concept that still felt foreign—taking in the stone walls hung with deep purple tapestries, the narrow window that looked out onto the campus above, the bed that was actually large enough for two.

He didn't let himself think about that last part. Not yet.

A knock at the door broke through his reverie.

"It's open," he called, turning as Lexi slipped inside with the silent grace that came from years of training. Her auburn hair was loose around her shoulders today, a departure from the severe braid she'd worn during their trials. She looked softer somehow. Less like someone preparing for battle.

"Stop brooding," she said, though her smile took any sting from the words. "You've been an official Acolyte for three days. That's barely enough time to brood at all, let alone properly."

"I'm not brooding. I'm... adjusting."

She crossed the room in three quick strides and pressed her palm flat against his chest, right over the new tattoo that marked his collarbone—the stylized crescent moon and veil that declared his rank. Her touch warmed his skin through the fabric of his shirt.

"You're allowed to be happy, you know." Her voice dropped lower. "You earned this."

"I know." He covered her hand with his own. "It just doesn't feel real yet."

"What part? The room? The rank?" Her green eyes searched his face. "Or us?"

"All of it." He exhaled slowly. "A month ago, I was just a college student worrying about finals and student loans. Now I'm part of a secret order that guards reality from shadow monsters, I have magic I barely understand, and you—"

He stopped, uncertain how to finish.

Lexi arched an eyebrow. "Me what?"

"You're here." He said it simply, because the alternative was saying something far more vulnerable. "That's the part that feels least real of all."

For a long moment, she just looked at him. Then she laughed—a real laugh, warm and surprised, nothing like the sharp sound she'd used as armor when they'd first met.

"You're an idiot," she said affectionately. "A romantic idiot, but still."

She kissed him before he could respond. Not the desperate, urgent kisses they'd shared during the chaos of the trials—this was slower, deeper, a promise rather than a claim. Her fingers curled into the fabric of his shirt as his hands found the curve of her waist, pulling her closer.

When they finally broke apart, both slightly breathless, she rested her forehead against his.

"The Order recognizes us as partners now," she said. "Bound. Officially entangled in the records."

"I noticed." He smiled against her hair. "Your mother's face when she signed off on it was... memorable."

"She'll come around." Lexi's tone suggested she didn't particularly care either way. "What matters is that no one can separate us now. Not even an Elder."

The weight of that settled over him. In the Order's hierarchy, a recognized partnership meant something beyond mere romance—it was a magical bond, a declaration that their fates were intertwined. If one fell, the other would feel it. If one rose, they would rise together.

"Are you scared?" he asked quietly.

"Of what?"

"Of being tied to me. Of what that means for your future in the Order."

She pulled back just enough to meet his eyes, her expression fierce. "I chose you, Drevik Markinsoul. Not because some prophecy or vision told me to, but because you're worth choosing. My future is whatever we make it together."

He wanted to kiss her again, wanted to lose himself in the certainty she offered, but a knock at the door interrupted them.

Lexi sighed. "I told Ivy to give us more time."

But when she opened the door, it wasn't Ivy on the other side.

Seraphina Vane stood in the hallway, her silver-blonde hair gleaming in the lamplight, her expression unreadable as ever. She wore the dark robes of a Seeker—a rank above Acolyte—and the silver pendant at her throat marked her as one of the Order's情报 gatherers.

"Am I interrupting?" Her voice was smooth, giving nothing away.

"Seraphina." Drevik stepped forward, suddenly conscious of how close he and Lexi had been standing. "No, we were just—"

"I came to congratulate you." Her dark eyes met his, and for just a moment, he thought he saw something vulnerable beneath her careful composure. "On your promotion. And your... partnership."

Lexi's hand found his, her fingers interlacing with his own. "Thank you, Seraphina. That means a great deal."

The two women regarded each other with the careful assessment of those who understood exactly what lay between them. Whatever Seraphina felt—and Drevik still wasn't entirely certain what that was—she kept it hidden behind her customary mask.

"The Elders have requested your presence this evening," Seraphina said, her attention shifting fully to Drevik. "There's a ceremony to formally recognize your new rank. Your grandmother has been invited to attend."

The warmth of the moment evaporated, replaced by a cold knot in his stomach.

"My grandmother?"

"She thinks you've taken an internship with a prestigious research firm." Seraphina's tone was carefully neutral. "The Order maintains several such cover stories for our members with outside families."

"I know, I just—" He ran a hand through his hair. "I haven't seen her since all this started. She probably thinks I've lost my mind, dropping out of my normal classes, disappearing for weeks at a time."

"Then this is an opportunity to reassure her." Seraphina's hand brushed his arm briefly—so quickly he might have imagined it. "You're still her grandson, Drevik. That hasn't changed."

But everything else had, and they all knew it.

The ceremony was brief, held in the same chamber where Drevik had first sworn his oath as an initiate. Elder Thornwood—Lexi's mother—presided, her sharp features inscrutable as she formally acknowledged his advancement. The other Elders watched from their seats along the curved wall, their faces shadowed by their ceremonial hoods.

Drevik barely noticed them. His attention was fixed on the small woman in the back of the room, her gray hair pulled back in its familiar practical bun, her weathered hands clutching her purse like an anchor.

His grandmother looked older than he remembered. More fragile. The realization hit him like a physical blow.

After the formal words were spoken, after the Elders had filed out and Lexi had given him a reassuring squeeze of the hand before slipping away, he finally approached her.

"Gram."

"Drevik." Her voice wavered. "Look at you. All grown up and working for a... a research firm."

The lie tasted like ash in his mouth. "It's a good opportunity."

"I'm sure it is." She reached up to cup his face, her palm warm and papery against his cheek. "You look tired. Are they working you too hard?"

"I'm fine." He covered her hand with his own. "Really. This is just what I needed."

She studied him with the knowing eyes that had seen through every excuse he'd ever made as a child. "You've changed, Drevik. I can see it. Something's different about you."

The mark on his collarbone burn beneath his shirt. "I've grown up a bit."

"No." She shook her head slowly. "It's more than that. You have a... a weight about you now. Like you're carrying something important."

He couldn't lie to her. Not directly. But he couldn't tell her the truth either. The Order's secrecy was enforced by binding oaths that would literally prevent him from speaking about it to anyone outside their ranks.

"I found my place, Gram." It was as close to honesty as he could get. "For the first time since Mom and Dad... since I lost them... I feel like I belong somewhere."

Her eyes glistened. "Your parents would be proud of you. Whatever this is, whatever you're doing with your life—they'd be proud."

The words landed like a knife between his ribs. He pulled her into a hug, hiding his face against her shoulder so she wouldn't see the emotion that threatened to break through.

"I miss them," he admitted.

"I know, sweetheart." Her arms wrapped around him, strong despite her age. "I miss them too. Every day."

They held each other for a long moment, and Drevik felt the strange dissonance of his two lives—the ordinary world where he was just a college student who'd lost his parents too young, and the extraordinary one where he commanded shadow magic and fought creatures from another dimension.

When she finally pulled back, she was smiling. "Enough of this. You're clearly important now, with your fancy ceremony and your serious colleagues. I saw that girl watching you—the one with the auburn hair. She looked at you like you hung the moon."

Drevik couldn't help but smile. "Lexi. She's... she's important to me."

"I can tell." His grandmother patted his cheek. "She seems fierce. You need someone fierce, Drevik. Someone who'll fight for you when you forget to fight for yourself."

He thought of Lexi facing down her own mother to stand at his side. Of the way she'd thrown herself between him and the Shadow beast during the trials without hesitation.

"She does," he said. "She fights for me."

"Good." His grandmother nodded firmly. "Then I approve. Now, when do I get to properly meet her? Not just a wave across a room, but actual conversation."

"Soon," he promised. "Once things settle down."

She gave him a knowing look. "Things never settle down, Drevik. Not when you're young and in love and building a life. You make time for what matters, or you watch it slip away."

The words echoed in his mind long after she'd left, long after the chamber had emptied and he stood alone in the flickering torchlight.

He found Lexi in the training yard behind the compound, going through a series of forms with a blade that gleamed silver in the afternoon light. She moved like water—each motion flowing into the next with practiced grace.

He watched for a moment, appreciating the play of muscle beneath her fitted training clothes, before stepping into the yard.

She paused, lowering her blade. "How did it go with your grandmother?"

"She knows I'm hiding something." He moved to stand beside her, leaning against the weapons rack. "She's too sharp not to notice. But she accepted the cover story, I think."

"Most families do." Lexi wiped sweat from her brow with the back of her hand. "They want to believe their loved ones are safe, doing normal things. It's easier than the truth."

"Does yours? Believe it, I mean?"

Lexi's expression flickered. "My mother is an Elder. There's no pretending in my family. But my father left the Order when I was young. He wanted a normal life." She paused, something complicated moving behind her eyes. "I haven't seen him in years. He couldn't accept what I would become."

"I'm sorry."

"Don't be." Her voice was firm. "I made my choice. This is my family now." She stepped closer, her blade vanishing into the shadows from which all Order weapons came. "You're my family now."

He reached for her, his hand settling on her hip. "I spoke to Ivy earlier. She's been reassigned to the archives—official Acolyte duties."

"Good. She deserves a safe posting after everything." Lexi's hand came up to rest on his chest. "And Seraphina?"

"Remains a mystery, as always." He exhaled. "She wished me well. But there's something she's not saying. Something she wants to tell me but can't."

"Seraphina keeps more secrets than the entire Shadowlands." Lexi's tone was wry. "But she's been loyal to you. Whatever her reasons, she's stood with us when it mattered."

"I know."

They stood in comfortable silence for a moment, the training yard empty around them, the sounds of the campus above filtering down muffled and distant.

"This is just the beginning, isn't it?" Drevik finally said.

Lexi's eyes met his. "For us? Or the danger?"

"Both."

She nodded slowly. "Yes. The trials were just the first test. There will be more—more threats, more challenges. The Order is facing something it hasn't faced in centuries. And you—" She hesitated.

"Me what?"

"You're going to be at the center of it. I've seen how the Elders look at you. How the Veiled Ones watch you in visions. You're not just a new Acolyte, Drevik. You're something more."

The weight of her words settled over him, heavy and certain. He'd known, on some level, since the moment he'd first channeled shadow magic with an ease that shouldn't have been possible. He was different. Special in a way that frightened even the Order's leadership.

"Then I'm glad you're with me." He pulled her closer. "I can't do this alone."

"You won't have to." Her lips brushed his jaw. "I told you. My future is whatever we build together."

He kissed her properly then, in the empty training yard with the afternoon sun warm on their skin. She melted against him, her fingers threading through his hair, her body fitting against his like she'd been made for this exact moment.

When they finally broke apart, both breathing harder, she smiled against his mouth.

"We should continue this somewhere more private."

"My new quarters have an actual door that locks."

"Lead the way, Acolyte Markinsoul."

They made love slowly, taking their time now that urgency wasn't driving them. Drevik memorized every inch of her—the constellation of freckles across her shoulders, the scar just below her left breast from a training accident years ago, the way her breath held when he kissed the hollow of her throat.

Afterwards, she lay curled against him, her head on his chest, their legs tangled together beneath the sheets. The lamplight cast shadows across the ceiling, familiar patterns that seemed almost to move if he watched them too long.

"Ivy mentioned there's a gathering tomorrow," he said. "All the new Acolytes, being introduced to the broader Order."

"Tradition." Lexi's voice was drowsy, satisfied. "You'll meet people from other compounds. Start building the connections you'll need as you rise."

"Connections." He smiled. "Is that what we call politics in the Order?"

"We call it survival." She propped herself up on one elbow, looking down at him. "The Order isn't unified, Drevik. There are factions, different visions for our future. Some see the Veiled Ones as guides to be followed. Others see them as obstacles to be overcome. You'll need to navigate that."

"And which faction are you in?"

Her eyes gleamed. "I'm in the faction that protects what I love. Whatever that requires."

He pulled her down for another kiss, softer this time. "I don't deserve you."

"No," she agreed. "But you have me anyway."

They drifted off eventually, her warmth a comforting weight against his side. But Drevik's sleep was far from peaceful.

The dream began in twilight.

He stood at the edge of a vast expanse of shadow—not the familiar shadows of the mortal realm, but something deeper, darker. The Shadowlands stretched before him in endless shades of gray and purple, twisted trees rising from ground that breathe, a sky that held no sun, only the pale glow of a crescent moon.

He'd been here before, in visions during his trials. But this was different. More real.

Something moved in the darkness ahead. Something that smiled with too many teeth.

"Drevik Markinsoul." The voice came from everywhere and nowhere, a whisper that echoed through his bones. "We've been waiting for you."

He tried to move, to step back, but his feet were rooted to the ground. The shadows around him shift, forming shapes that might have been human once—or had never been human at all.

"You carry our mark," the voice continued. "You wear our gift. But you don't know what you are, do you? What you were born to become."

"I'm an Acolyte of the Order." His voice came out stronger than he felt. "I serve the Veil."

A laugh, cold and ancient. "The Order. Children playing with power they don't understand. They told you your parents died, didn't they? Told you they were gone forever."

The shadows rippled, and a figure emerged—a woman wrapped in darkness, her features obscured. But as she stepped closer, the shadows fell away like a veil being lifted.

Drevik's heart stopped.

He knew that face. Had memorized it from the few photographs his grandmother had kept, from dreams he'd thought were only dreams.

"Mom?"

Her smile was sad, her eyes holding depths of sorrow and something else—something that might have been hope.

"They lied to you, my son." Her voice was the same voice he remembered from childhood lullabies, from bedtime stories, from a thousand small moments he'd thought he'd forgotten. "I'm still alive. And I need you to find me."

"Where are you? What happened? Dad—"

"The Shadowlands hold many secrets." She reached for him, but her fingers passed through his like smoke. "Not yet, Drevik. You're not ready. But you will be. Grow stronger. Learn what the Order won't teach you. And when the time comes—"

The dream fragment, twilight dissolving into darkness.

"Wait!" He reached for her. "How do I find you?"

Her voice echoed as she faded: "Follow the mark. Trust the shadows. And remember—they're not the only ones watching you."

Drevik jolted awake, his heart pounding, his body drenched in sweat. Beside him, Lexi stirred but didn't wake.

The mark on his collarbone burned like fire.

He touched it with trembling fingers, feeling the raised skin of the tattoo, remembering his mother's face, her words echoing in his mind.

I'm still alive.

The Order had told him his parents died in a car accident. Had shown him the records, the grave site, the evidence of their deaths. But the Shadowlands held many secrets, and his mother had him in a dream that felt more real than any vision he'd experienced.

They had lied to him. About everything.

And somewhere, in a dimension of eternal twilight, his mother was waiting for him to find her.

Drevik lay back down slowly, his mind racing, his heart still pounding. Lexi said something in her sleep and curled closer to him, her arm draping across his chest.

He held her tighter than he meant to, needing the anchor of her warmth, the proof that he wasn't alone.

Tomorrow, he would begin his new life as an Acolyte. He would play the Order's games, learn their secrets, rise through their ranks. But now he had a new goal, one that burned brighter than ambition or duty.

He would find the truth about his parents. He would discover what the Order was hiding. And he would rescue his mother from whatever prison the Shadowlands had become.

The mark on his chest pulsed once, like a heartbeat, and Drevik smiled in the darkness.

This is just the beginning.

For all of us.
